# 2. Sūrat al-Baqara

The longest sūra in the Qurʾān (286 verses). Medinan; it came down after the hijra, spread across a long stretch of time — some of the rulings in it belong to the first year of Medina, others to a period much later. For this reason the sūra should be read not as a text written at one sitting but as a record that accompanies ten years in the life of a community being built.

It takes its name from the story of the "cow" told within it (verses 67-73).

---

## 2/1 — الٓمٓ

*Alif Lām Mīm*

### What are these?

Twenty-nine sūras in the Qurʾān open with letters read out one by one. These are called the *ḥurūf muqaṭṭaʿa* — the disconnected letters, letters read piece by piece. The recitation itself shows this: this verse is not read "alam" but **"alif-lām-mīm"**, each letter named separately. They are not a word, then, but the letters themselves.

The numerical table is this, and it is worth noticing:

- A total of **fourteen distinct letters** are used in these groups.
- The Arabic alphabet has **twenty-eight letters.**
- The letters used are thus exactly half the alphabet.
- These fourteen letters occur at the heads of sūras in fourteen different combinations (alif-lām-mīm, alif-lām-rā, ḥā-mīm, ṭā-hā, yā-sīn, qāf, nūn, ṣād, kāf-hā-yā-ʿayn-ṣād, and so on).

### What do they mean? — the honest answer

**It is not known with certainty.** This has to be said at the outset and plainly, because these letters are the places that have produced the most speculation in the history of exegesis.

From the earliest generations onward a group of scholars said that the meaning of these letters belongs to the *mutashābih* — what must be left to God; sayings of the type "Every book has a secret, and the secret of the Qurʾān is the letters at the heads of the sūras" are transmitted. This stance is not a confession of ignorance but a choice of method: to be silent where the text itself gives no explanation.

Against this there are also views that look for an explanation. The chief ones:

| View | Explanation | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| **The name / mark of the sūra** | The letters are the name of that sūra | Some sūras really are known by these letters (Ṭāhā, Yāsīn). But the same group of letters comes at the head of more than one sūra (seven sūras begin with "ḥā-mīm"), and that weakens the claim that they are a name |
| **An oath** | God is swearing by these letters | Oaths in the Qurʾān generally come with an explicit particle of oath; there is none here |
| **Abbreviations of names** | Alif = Allāh, Lām = Laṭīf, Mīm = Majīd, and so on | It is transmitted with attribution to Ibn ʿAbbās. But it is not systematic — applied to all the groups, the same method does not give consistent results |
| **Drawing attention (*tanbīh*)** | An unfamiliar sound pulls the hearer into the text | A plausible function; but it does not explain *why these letters* |
| **The material of the challenge** | "Here are the letters the Qurʾān is made of; go ahead and make one yourselves" | I take this up separately below — the strongest view, and the one supported by the text |

### A clue that comes from the text itself

Looking at the verses that follow these letters, a regularity appears: **most of the letter-groups are followed immediately by an expression that speaks of the Book.**

- الٓمٓ → *"That is the Book…"* (al-Baqara 2/2)
- الٓمٓصٓ → *"A Book sent down to you…"* (al-Aʿrāf 7/2)
- الٓرٰ → *"These are the verses of the wise Book"* (Yūnus 10/1)
- حٰمٓ → *"The sending down of this Book … is from God"* (al-Muʾmin 40/1-2)
- نٓ → *"By the pen and what they write"* (al-Qalam 68/1)

The repetition is too regular to be counted a coincidence. The text itself establishes a link between the letters and the Book.

The reading that follows is this: the letters may be standing there to show the **raw material** of the Qurʾān. The challenge the Qurʾān throws at the other side — *"Then bring a sūra like it"* (al-Baqara 2/23) — has meaning only if the material is held in common. The Qurʾān is not made of some extraordinary substance; it is made of the Arabs' own letters, of sounds they themselves knew. The letters are set down first, then it is said "that is the Book": the material is yours, this is the result.

This reading is not certain; but unlike the others it rests on an order inside the text itself. I find it the most reasonable — as a preference, not as a ruling.

### What should not be done

Attempts to draw out hidden messages by numerical calculations on these letters (*abjad*, counting letters, extracting dates) have been tried again and again in the history of exegesis and have fallen into inconsistency again and again. The basic flaw of the method is this: given enough licence, any conclusion can be drawn from any text. That road is not taken in this commentary.

### A note

These letters count as a verse, they are recited in prayer, they are written in the muṣḥaf. That their meaning is unknown does not mean they are without function. That the text should have a side which does not open fully to the human being is itself a piece of knowledge: at the very first line the reader meets the fact that he will not solve everything.

---

## 2/2 — ذَٰلِكَ ٱلْكِتَٰبُ لَا رَيْبَ ۛ فِيهِ ۛ هُدًى لِّلْمُتَّقِينَ

*Dhālika'l-kitābu lā rayba fīh, hudan li'l-muttaqīn*
"That is the Book; there is no doubt in it; a guidance for those who guard themselves."

### ذَٰلِكَ — "that, yonder"

Arabic has two kinds of demonstrative: *hādhā* (this) for what is near, *dhālika* (that) for what is far. For the book we hold in our hands and are reading, the **far demonstrative** is used. Why?

The classical commentaries give several explanations:

- **Eminence.** In Arabic the far demonstrative can convey rank rather than distance. In English too, "that is the book" is not a statement about physical distance. The distance is the distance of respect.
- **A reference to what was promised.** There was a book announced beforehand, awaited; "that is it", it is said. The pointing is towards the past, towards the expectation.
- **A reference to the source.** What is meant is not the copy in our hands but the original.

Whichever it is, the shared effect is the same: in speaking of itself the text puts a distance in between. It builds not nearness but gravity.

### ٱلْكِتَٰب — book

Root *k-t-b*. We know it as "to write", but the primary sense of the root is **to bring together, to join, to sew**. The Arabs used this verb for stitching pieces of leather to one another. Writing, too, is the stitching of letters side by side. From the same root comes *katība*: a military unit that is gathered, not scattered.

"Book", then, means in essence **what has been collected, brought together**. The gathering of what was scattered.

The root has a second use as well, and it occurs often in the Qurʾān: *kutiba ʿalaykum* — "it was written for you", that is, it was made obligatory (*"Fasting was written for you"*, al-Baqara 2/183). In this use the book is not only what is read but what **brings obligation**. The word itself carries the fact that the text binds.

### لَا رَيْبَ فِيهِ — "there is no doubt in it"

**The difference between رَيْب and شَكّ.** Arabic has several words for doubt and they are not synonyms:

- **Shakk** — neutral uncertainty. Being unable to decide between two possibilities. It does not require discomfort.
- **Rayb** — the doubt that unsettles. There is disquiet and suspicion in it. From the same root comes *rība*: an ill opinion held against someone, the feeling that "there is something behind this".

The verse uses *rayb*, not *shakk*. What is said is therefore not only "it is logically certain" but also "it has no side that will trouble you inwardly". A trust is being given to the person approaching the text.

**The form of the negation.** The construction "lā rayba" is a pattern in Arabic that negates the whole genus — it means "no doubt of any kind". Not "there is a little doubt but it does not matter"; doubt itself is left entirely outside.

**But what of those who do doubt?** The objection is plain: there are, and have been, people who doubt this book. Is the verse denying that?

No. Note carefully: the verse does not say "no one doubts it"; it says **"there is no doubt in it"**. The place of doubt is not the book itself but the state of the one reading. It is saying that the fault is not to be looked for in the object. Indeed the same sūra addresses the doubters directly a few verses later and calls them to make the attempt (2/23).

### The small mark in the muṣḥaf

In this verse the classical copies carry two three-dotted marks in the form `ۛ` — one before "fīh", one after. This is called *muʿānaqa* (embracing), and it means this: **one may pause at either of the two places, but not at both.** For the two pauses give two different meanings:

- *"There is no doubt in it. It is a guidance for those who guard themselves."*
- *"There is no doubt in it. In it there is guidance for those who guard themselves."*

Both are correct; the two cannot be read at once. This mark in the muṣḥaf is a concrete example of how scrupulously the tradition of recitation has preserved differences of meaning.

### هُدًى — a guidance

The word comes indefinite: not "al-hudā" (the guidance) but "hudan" (a guidance). In Arabic indefiniteness sometimes conveys diminution and sometimes magnification; here it is the second — "such a guidance as this". It also comes in the form of a *verbal noun*: not "guiding one" but "guidance" outright. The Book is not something that *gives* guidance; it is as though it were guidance itself.

### ٱلْمُتَّقِين — those who guard themselves

Root *w-q-y*: **to protect oneself, to take cover, to put something over oneself.** From the same root comes *wiqāya* (protection); in Turkish the borrowed word *vikaye* is still used, and it lies behind the term for preventive medicine.

The usual renderings of *taqwā* — "piety", "God-fearing" — pull the word towards devoutness and religious scrupulosity. The sense of the root is different from this, and plainer: **to put oneself under protection, to place something between oneself and danger.** The muttaqī is not a coward; he is one who is careful. Like a person who watches where he treads as he walks.

### The question of the closed circle

A familiar objection arises here: **if the Book is guidance for the muttaqīn, how is someone who is not a muttaqī to find guidance?** If guidance requires taqwā and taqwā requires guidance, the circle never opens.

The answer lies in guidance having layers (I separated those layers at al-Fātiḥa 5):

The **call** of the Book is to everyone — the Qurʾān describes itself again and again as "a warning to all beings", "an exposition for people". But **actual benefit** depends on the person's stance. The sun rises on everyone; the one who shuts his eyes is not lit. The fault is not in the sun.

Moreover "the muttaqīn" here does not mean people who are finished, who have reached completion. Return to the sense of the root: **those who carry a concern to guard themselves.** That is, anyone who shrinks from doing a wrong, who has the intention of being careful. This is not a high threshold but a minimum openness. The Book gives benefit not to the one closed to it but to the one who cares about being guarded.

---

## 2/3 — ٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْمِنُونَ بِٱلْغَيْبِ وَيُقِيمُونَ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَمِمَّا رَزَقْنَٰهُمْ يُنفِقُونَ

*Alladhīna yuʾminūna bi'l-ghaybi wa yuqīmūna'ṣ-ṣalāta wa mimmā razaqnāhum yunfiqūn*
"Those who believe in the unseen, who keep up the prayer, and who spend of what We have provided them."

Who the "muttaqīn" named in the previous verse are is now described. Three items are listed and their order carries meaning: **inner world → body → property.** From belief to what is done with the body, and from there to letting go of what one owns. More concrete at every step, and harder.

### يُؤْمِنُونَ — faith

Root *ʾ-m-n*. The primary sense of the root is **trust and safety.** Look at what comes from the same root: *amān* (a guarantee of safety), *amāna* (a trust), *amīn* (trustworthy), *āmin* (one who is safe).

This separates *īmān* from the English "to believe". In English, believing has more to do with knowledge — accepting a proposition as true. The Arabic root, by contrast, sets up a **relationship**: to trust, to commit oneself, to feel safe.

Indeed one of the names of God is **al-Muʾmin**: the one who gives safety. The same word covers both the believer and the giver of security. Faith is not a one-way acceptance but a bond of mutual trust.

### بِٱلْغَيْب — in the unseen

Root *gh-y-b*: **to disappear from sight, to be covered.** The setting of the sun is called *ghaybūba*. The unseen is not what does not exist but **what is not visible**. This distinction is critical and is often confused.

One point must be said plainly: **knowledge that rests on the unseen is not something irrational; most human knowledge is of this kind.** No one has seen an electron; it is known from its effects. No one has gone down to the earth's core; it is inferred from seismic waves. No one sees the past; it is built from its traces. No one can directly observe another person's consciousness; it is inferred from behaviour.

The principle "I believe only what I see", applied consistently, takes away nearly everything a human being knows. Faith in the unseen is not a leap without evidence; it is trust in a domain reached by evidence but not directly observable. Nor is what the Qurʾān asks for an acceptance with the eyes shut — the same Qurʾān says constantly "look", "will you not think", "travel the earth and see".

I do not write this in the sense that the Qurʾān is doing science; I say only that the principle "trust in what is unseen" is not out of keeping with the general working of human knowledge.

### يُقِيمُونَ ٱلصَّلَوٰة — to keep up the prayer

The verse does not say "they perform the prayer". It says **"they keep the prayer standing"**, and the difference matters.

*Iqāma* is from the root *q-w-m* — the same root as *mustaqīm* in al-Fātiḥa. Its sense: **to raise something to its feet, to set it up, to make it straight, to hold it standing.** Like raising a post.

Arabic could have used the verb *adāʾ* for "to discharge the prayer"; it is not used. The difference is this: *adāʾ* is the discharging of a debt — it is done and finished. *Iqāma* is holding something upright — it requires continuity, it requires maintenance, one has to keep it from falling.

The meaning that follows: the prayer is not a duty struck off a list but a structure held standing. On time, according to its conditions, continuously and with its inside filled. Elsewhere the Qurʾān describes the reverse of this — those who pray and are heedless of their prayer (al-Māʿūn 107/4-5). That is exactly the difference between performing and keeping up.

The root of the word **صلاة** is disputed; some lexicographers attach it to the root meaning *to pray*, others to the *ṣalā* region on either side of the lower back (connecting it with the movement of bowing). The first is more widespread.

### وَمِمَّا رَزَقْنَٰهُمْ يُنفِقُونَ — they spend of what We have provided

There are three distinct subtleties here.

**First: "what We have provided as sustenance."** The sentence states from the outset the source of the property to be given. The one who spends is not giving out of his own property; he is giving out of what has been given to him. This cuts the sense of superiority in the act of giving off at the root — what you hold in your hand was never yours.

**Second: the particle "min".** The verse does not say "they spend what We have provided", it says "**of** what We have provided". Here *min* in Arabic states the partitive: a portion of it. It is not being asked that one distribute everything. Elsewhere the Qurʾān sets this balance out plainly: *"Do not keep your hand chained to your neck, nor open it out altogether"* (al-Isrāʾ 17/29).

**Third: the word *infāq* itself.** Root *n-f-q*. The first sense of this root is **to be used up, to run out, to pass away**; *nafaqa* is a thing's going out of one's hand, coming to an end. From this comes *nafaqa* — what is spent and consumed for maintenance.

The same root has one further interesting relative: *nafaq*, an underground tunnel. And from this, **nifāq** — hypocrisy. The field mouse has a burrow with two mouths; it enters by one and, when danger comes, leaves by the other. The hypocrite gets his name from here: the one with two exits.

The same root, then, names both the letting go of one's property and the keeping of a hidden way out for oneself. *Infāq* is the closing of one's exit — accepting that what is given will not come back. *Nifāq* is always keeping an escape tunnel in reserve. That the sūra will speak at length of the hypocrites in the verses ahead makes this contrast more meaningful still.

Note: *infāq* does not mean only *zakāt*; *zakāt* is the obligatory minimum portion, whereas *infāq* is spending in the way of God in general, in its entirety.

---

## 2/4 — وَٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْمِنُونَ بِمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ وَمَآ أُنزِلَ مِن قَبْلِكَ وَبِٱلْـَٔاخِرَةِ هُمْ يُوقِنُونَ

*Wa'lladhīna yuʾminūna bimā unzila ilayka wa mā unzila min qablika wa bi'l-ākhirati hum yūqinūn*
"And those who believe in what has been sent down to you and in what was sent down before you; and of the hereafter they are certain."

### The claim to be the continuation of a chain

This verse is one of the fundamental texts showing how Islam positions itself. What counts as a condition of faith is not only "what has been sent down to you" but **"what was sent down before you"**.

The text presents itself, then, not as a new religion but as the last link of a chain that had been interrupted. This is also why the history of the Children of Israel is recounted at such length in the sections ahead: what is recounted is not the story of a foreign nation but the earlier links of the same chain.

The verb **أُنزِلَ** comes in the passive: "it was sent down", the sender is not named. Using the passive where the agent is obvious is common in Arabic, and here it also quietly stresses that the text is not a human work — the book was not written, it was sent down.

### وَبِٱلْـَٔاخِرَة — the hereafter

Root *ʾ-kh-r*: **what comes after, what is left behind.** *Al-ākhira* = the last, the other one. The word's pair is *al-dunyā*, and that comes from the root *d-n-w*: **what is near, what is lower.**

The names the Qurʾān uses for the two lives, then, state not a judgement of value but a **position**: the near one and the one that comes after. This world is not named "dunyā" because it is bad, but because it is near. And in fact the human being's basic problem is described right here — it is easy to see what is near, and hard to take into account what comes after.

### يُوقِنُونَ — why not "faith" but "certainty"?

This is the striking side of the verse. At the head of the sentence the verb *yuʾminūn* (they believe) is used twice; when it comes to the hereafter the verb changes: **yūqinūn.**

*Yaqīn* is a word from the root *y-q-n*, and it means **certain knowledge from which doubt has been entirely removed**. It carries an association like sediment settling to the bottom, like turbidity clearing. It is a degree beyond faith: faith is to trust, whereas *yaqīn* is the closing of the argument.

Why for the hereafter in particular? Because throughout history this is the article of belief most objected to and most mocked. The objections the Qurʾān reports all gather at the same place: *"When we have become dust, shall we really be created anew?"* (al-Raʿd 13/5). The idea of God is found negotiable; the idea of rising after death is refused outright.

That the firmest word is used at the point most shaken is no coincidence.

**هُمْ يُوقِنُونَ** — there is also a pronoun in the sentence that looks superfluous: "they, they are certain". In Arabic this pronoun repeated after the subject conveys *takhṣīṣ* (specification). The meaning shifts to this: **it is they who are certain of the hereafter.** The emphasis implies that others are slack on this point.

---

## 2/5 — أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ عَلَىٰ هُدًى مِّن رَّبِّهِمْ وَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُفْلِحُونَ

*Ulāʾika ʿalā hudan min rabbihim wa ulāʾika humu'l-mufliḥūn*
"Those are upon a guidance from their Lord, and those are the ones who attain."

### عَلَىٰ هُدًى — "upon" guidance

A small particle, a large difference. The verse does not say "**in** guidance" (*fī hudan*); it says "**upon** guidance" (*ʿalā hudan*).

Look at which particle the Qurʾān uses for the exact opposite: for going astray it always uses *fī* — **fī ḍalālin mubīn**, "**in** a manifest straying". This contrast holds consistently through the text and has caught the attention of the classical exegetes:

| | Particle | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Guidance | *ʿalā* — upon | To stand on a ground; to be high up, to see the road, to have firm footing |
| Straying | *fī* — in | To be sunk inside a thing; to be surrounded, not to see the way out |

The one on the right road is **on top of the road** — he sees where he is going, his foot rests on ground. The one who strays is **inside** something — he is closed in on every side and cannot choose his direction. Two particles alone draw two distinct human conditions.

The clause **"min rabbihim"** is not idle either: the guidance is not their own achievement but a gift coming from their Lord. In al-Fātiḥa we saw that guidance comes from the same root as *hadiyya* (gift); here this is said outright. For all that they carry the five attributes listed, the source of the guidance is still not themselves.

### ٱلْمُفْلِحُون — those who attain

The root of this word carries one of the finest pictures in the sūra.

Root *f-l-ḥ*: **to split, to crack open, to cleave.** From the same root comes **fallāḥ** — the farmer. The farmer is given this name because he **splits the soil**. The plough's work is to break the crust on the ground.

*Falāḥ* — attainment — is therefore, within the word's own logic: **to break through an obstacle and come out, to crack the husk, to come to the point of bearing.**

The picture is consistent throughout. The seed is buried under the soil, left in darkness, looking as though it is rotting. Its deliverance is to split the layer above it and reach the surface — and only then does it yield a hundredfold. Deliverance is not ease; it is the overcoming of resistance.

The call **"ḥayya ʿala'l-falāḥ"**, repeated five times a day in the call to prayer, is with this word: "come to the splitting through".

### The function of the repetition

"Ulāʾika" (those are) occurs twice in the verse, and the second clause carries the pronoun "hum" as well. Three emphases piled on one another. This accumulation acts as a seal at the end of the five-verse opening: those who carry the attributes listed — **and only they.**

### The opening as a whole

The first five verses of the sūra do this:

1. They begin with letters that are not understood — at the very first line the reader meets his own limit.
2. They present the Book and place doubt as arising not from the book but from the reader.
3. They describe who will benefit from the Book: those who carry a concern to guard themselves.
4. They list five attributes of these people: faith in the unseen, prayer, spending, faith in all the revelations, certainty about the hereafter.
5. They state the outcome: they are on the road, and they will break through and come out.

Al-Fātiḥa had ended with "show us the straight path". Al-Baqara begins with "that is the Book" and in five verses gives the description of those standing on the road. The transition between the two sūras is the transition between a question and an answer.

The verses that follow will show the other face of the coin: first those who reject outright (6-7), then — at much greater length — those who appear to accept and do not (8-20).

---

## The three groups in the opening and their proportions

The opening of the sūra divides people into three, and the **measure** by which it divides them is as meaningful as the place where it divides:

| Group | Verses | Length |
|---|---|---|
| Those who guard themselves | 2-5 | 4 verses |
| Those who cover over (the unbelievers) | 6-7 | 2 verses |
| The two-faced (the hypocrites) | 8-20 | **13 verses** |

Two verses are given to the one who rejects openly, thirteen to the one who appears to accept and does not. The proportion is no coincidence. An enemy standing opposite you can be seen, taken into account, guarded against. But a person standing in the same rank who is not in the same place cannot be identified. On its very first page the Qurʾān turns attention from the outside inward.

---

## 2/6 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ سَوَآءٌ عَلَيْهِمْ ءَأَنذَرْتَهُمْ أَمْ لَمْ تُنذِرْهُمْ لَا يُؤْمِنُونَ

*Inna'lladhīna kafarū sawāʾun ʿalayhim a-andhartahum am lam tundhirhum lā yuʾminūn*
"As for those who have covered over the truth: it is the same for them whether you warn them or do not warn them, they will not believe."

### كَفَرَ — unbelief

There is a wide gap between the load the word carries in ordinary use and the sense of the root in Arabic, so the root must be put down first.

Root *k-f-r*: **to cover, to close over, to hide.**

Its derivatives show this plainly:

- **The farmer** is called *kāfir* in Arabic — because he covers the seed with earth. The Qurʾān makes this use itself: *"Like a rain whose growth delights the tillers (*kuffār*)"* (al-Ḥadīd 57/20). The same word, in the same sūra, in an agricultural sense.
- **The night** is called *kāfir*, because it covers everything over.
- Of one who puts on armour it is said "kafara dirʿahu" — he covered his body.
- **Kaffāra** is the amends that close over an offence committed.

The definition that follows from this is critical: **unbelief is not not-knowing; it is covering over what one knows.** Ignorance and unbelief are different things. The person the Qurʾān calls *kāfir* is not one who has failed to see the proof set before him but one who has chosen to cover what he saw. Indeed the Qurʾān says this outright through the mouth of Moses (Mūsā) against Pharaoh: *"You have certainly come to know that no one has sent these down but the Lord of the heavens and the earth"* (al-Isrāʾ 17/102).

And look at the word's opposite: in the Qurʾān what is set opposite *kufr* is most often not *īmān* but **thanks** (*"shākiran aw kafūrā"*, al-Insān 76/3). Thanks is to see and acknowledge what has been given; unbelief is to cover it over. Unbelief, then, is at bottom a notion of **ingratitude**.

This distinction takes the word out of being a term of abuse and makes it a description of a condition. It will be used that way in this commentary.

### إِنذَار — warning

Root *n-dh-r*. To warn, but specifically **to give advance notice of a bad outcome.** This is why the Qurʾān uses two words as a pair for the prophet: *bashīr* (bringer of good news) and *nadhīr* (warner).

From the same root comes *nadhr* — a vow. What the two have in common is the making binding, here and now, of something that has not yet come about.

### What the verse says and what it does not say

On the face of it the sentence carries a definite ruling: "whether you warn them or do not warn them, they will not believe". To draw from this a general conclusion such as **"unbelievers will never believe"** runs against both history and the Qurʾān itself.

For many people who were unbelievers in the years this verse came down believed afterwards; some of the best-known names in the history of Islam were still on the other side when this verse came down. The Qurʾān also keeps the door open: *"Say: O My servants who have gone beyond bounds against themselves! Do not despair of God's mercy"* (al-Zumar 39/53).

Most exegetes have therefore understood the verse thus: the ruling is for a particular group who have made unbelief their trait, whose stance before the proof has become fixed; it is not a general decree of destiny for every unbeliever. Indeed the verse does not say "the unbelievers", it says **"those who have covered over"** — those who have performed an act.

And look at whom the verse addresses: it is said to the one giving the warning. Its function is not to issue a ruling but **to set the warner at ease**: your task is to convey, not to produce the outcome. The Qurʾān says this plainly elsewhere: *"You cannot guide whom you love"* (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/56).

---

## 2/7 — خَتَمَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ قُلُوبِهِمْ وَعَلَىٰ سَمْعِهِمْ وَعَلَىٰٓ أَبْصَٰرِهِمْ غِشَٰوَةٌ وَلَهُمْ عَذَابٌ عَظِيمٌ

*Khatama'llāhu ʿalā qulūbihim wa ʿalā samʿihim, wa ʿalā abṣārihim ghishāwah, wa lahum ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*
"God has sealed their hearts and their hearing; and over their eyes there is a covering. For them there is a great punishment."

### خَتْم — seal

Root *kh-t-m*: to come to the end of a thing, and to set a seal. From the same root comes *khātam* — a signet ring; and this is also the word in *khātam al-nabiyyīn*, used of the Prophet.

A seal does two things: it **closes** and it **confirms.** When an envelope is sealed it both becomes impossible to enter and is declared to have its contents settled. The use here carries both: the situation has been closed and registered.

### Three organs, two different operations

There is a detail in the grammar of the verse that is lost in most translations. The sentence divides thus:

- **The heart and the hearing** → *khatm* (sealing). Both are attached to the same verb.
- **The eye** → *ghishāwa* (a covering). This is a separate sentence: "over their eyes there is a covering."

Two different operations, two different weights. The seal is lasting and closes from within; the covering is a cloth drawn over the top — lighter, giving the impression that it could be lifted. There is a logic in the order too: the centre is the heart, and that is where it begins; the ear is the channel nearest to it; the eye is the outermost.

**An observation about the language:** in the verse "samʿ" (hearing) comes **singular** and "abṣār" (eyes) **plural**. This is a consistent pattern across the Qurʾān — hearing is almost always singular, seeing always plural. The explanation the classical exegetes give for it is this: hearing is of one kind, everyone in the same place hears the same sound; seeing varies, each person looks in a different direction and sees from a different angle. In hearing there is unity, in seeing multiplicity.

### The real question: if God is the one who seals, who is responsible?

This verse stands at the centre of one of the heaviest debates in the exegesis of the Qurʾān, and it cannot be passed over. The question is this: **if God has sealed the heart, how can that person be held responsible?**

**The key to the answer is in the order.** Elsewhere the Qurʾān recounts this sealing not on its own but together with a cause, and it gives the sequence plainly:

- *"God sealed their hearts because of their unbelief"* (al-Nisāʾ 4/155). The seal is the consequence, the cause is the unbelief.
- *"When they swerved, God made their hearts swerve"* (al-Ṣaff 61/5). Their straying first, then its requital.
- *"No! What they were doing has laid rust over their hearts"* (al-Muṭaffifīn 83/14). Rust is not something applied from outside; it arises from the metal's own reaction.

The seal, then, is not a barrier placed in front of a person's choice, but a locking that comes as the consequence of choice. A person covers first; as the covering is repeated the cover thickens; in the end the possibility of return is effectively closed. The Qurʾān attributes this last stage to God, because it is He who creates outcomes.

**And this is something observable.** That repeated choices become character, and that character in time becomes unalterable, is one of the most familiar of human experiences. The longer a habit lasts, the harder it is to leave; the more a stance is defended, the more impossible it becomes to turn back from it — because turning back now means declaring the whole of one's past wrong. The locking the verse describes looks at the same place as this well-known mechanism of moral life.

**The theological debate.** The great dispute that runs through this verse — who creates a person's acts, how much power of choice a person has — is among the fundamental divisions between the Muʿtazila and Ahl al-Sunna, and it went on for centuries. The Muʿtazila gave priority to preserving the independence of the human will, the Ashʿarī line to preserving the dependence of everything on God's power; the Māturīdī line looks for a way between the two. I take no side in this commentary; but this has to be said: **none of the parties held that the human being is without responsibility.** The dispute is about *how* responsibility is established, not about whether it exists.

---

## 2/8-20 — The hypocrites

This is the longest section in the opening of the sūra. The Qurʾān does not dispose of this type with a ruling; it describes it step by step, then paints it with two long comparisons.

### 2/8 — وَمِنَ ٱلنَّاسِ مَن يَقُولُ ءَامَنَّا بِٱللَّهِ وَبِٱلْيَوْمِ ٱلْـَٔاخِرِ وَمَا هُم بِمُؤْمِنِينَ

*"Among the people are some who say 'we believe in God and in the last day'; yet they are not believers."*

The section begins with **"among the people are some"**. No name is given. This will be kept to the end of the sūra: the Qurʾān describes this group, it does not expose it. And there is a reason for this — a description lasts, an exposure passes. Had names been given, the text would have been buried in history along with those persons.

Note: the sentence they utter is in the form **"we have believed"**. Faith is given as a word. The verse's reply comes by way of their state: "they are not believers". This gap between word and condition is the subject of the whole section.

One further detail: they say "in God and in the last day" — the beginning and the end of the chain. What lies between, that is, the prophet and the revelation, is absent from their sentence. Some exegetes count this a fine indication: it is easy to believe in what is abstract and distant; the hard thing is to comply with the concrete call in front of you.

### 2/9 — يُخَٰدِعُونَ ٱللَّهَ وَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَمَا يَخْدَعُونَ إِلَّآ أَنفُسَهُمْ وَمَا يَشْعُرُونَ

*"They try to deceive God and those who believe; but they deceive only themselves, and they do not perceive it."*

The root of **خدع**: to hide, to conceal, to keep covered. Deception, in this root, is "to show a thing as other than it is". The verb is in the *mufāʿala* pattern (*yukhādiʿūna*) — it conveys reciprocity and effort: they *set about* deceiving.

The turn of the sentence is hard: the attempt at deception does not reach its target, it comes back on them. The reason is simple — deception requires a gap in the other party's knowledge. Since there can be no such gap before God, the only real effect of the attempt is that the person hides his own condition from himself.

**وَمَا يَشْعُرُونَ** — "they do not perceive". Root *sh-ʿ-r*: **to perceive the finest thing.** From the same root come *shaʿr* (hair) and *shāʿir* (poet) — the poet is given this name because he perceives what is fine. The verse does not say "they do not know"; it says **"they do not notice it even with the finest perception"**. That is the definition of self-deception: to be unable to perceive that one is being deceived.

### 2/10 — فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ فَزَادَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ مَرَضًا

*"In their hearts is a disease; and God has increased their disease."*

For the unbelievers the image was a **seal**; for the hypocrites it is **disease**. The change of metaphor is meaningful:

- A sealed vessel is closed — the situation is fixed.
- But a diseased body is **alive**; it is neither healthy nor dead. It functions, but it functions faultily. And by the definition of disease it has a direction: it either heals or advances.

The verse states the second direction: "He increased it". A disease left untreated does not stay where it is. This increase too, like the seal in the previous verse, is the consequence of choice.

### 2/11-12 — The one who spoils seeing himself as a repairer

*"When they are told 'do not spread corruption in the earth', they say 'we are only putting things right'. Take note! It is they who are the corrupters, but they do not perceive it."*

These two verses are the most modern-seeming place in the whole section.

What is said is this: **the corrupter does not define himself as a corrupter.** He defines himself as a reformer. The great majority of evil is done not with the intention of doing evil but with the claim of putting right. Almost every great destruction in history had a programme of reform.

The root of **فسد**: to be spoilt, to rot, to lose its balance — a thing's going out of its own order. **صلح**, by contrast: to be fitting, to be in its right place, to be repaired. From the same root come *ṣulḥ* (peace) and *ṣāliḥ*. Both notions presuppose that an order exists: *fasād* is the spoiling of an order, *iṣlāḥ* the setting of it back in place.

The answer is not "no, you are spoiling things"; it is **"it is they who are the corrupters, but they do not perceive it"**. The issue, that is, is not lying but **the inability to see one's own condition.** This is the heavier diagnosis: the liar knows, this man does not.

### 2/13 — Intellectual arrogance

*"When they are told 'believe as the people have believed', they say 'shall we believe as the fools believe?' Take note! It is they who are the fools, but they do not know."*

The root of **سفه**: **lightness.** To come out light on the scale, to have no weight. From the same root comes *safīh* — light of mind, unable to manage himself. The Qurʾān also uses this word as a legal term for a person unable to manage his own property (al-Nisāʾ 4/5).

The attitude here is familiar: to look down on the believers, to place oneself above them. The verse's answer turns the same word back.

**The fine difference at the end of the two verses.** Verse 12 ends with *"lā yashʿurūn"* (they do not perceive), verse 13 with *"lā yaʿlamūn"* (they do not know). The change of word is not idle:

- Corruption is something that **cannot be perceived** — because it presents itself in the dress of reform; diagnosing it requires a fine perception.
- Foolishness is something that **cannot be known** — because the instrument by which a person would measure his own mind is that same mind.

### 2/14-15 — Two faces

*"When they meet those who believe they say 'we believe'; when they are alone with their satans they say 'we are with you, we were only mocking'. God mocks them, and leaves them floundering in their excess."*

The portrait is completed here: two settings, two languages. The personality changes with the setting, because there is no fixed centre.

The root of **طُغْيَان (ṭughyān)** is *ṭ-gh-y*: **to overflow, to go beyond bounds.** The Qurʾān uses this word for the overflowing of water as well: *"When the water overflowed"* (al-Ḥāqqa 69/11). *Ṭughyān* is not fitting inside one's vessel.

**يَعْمَهُونَ** — root *ʿ-m-h*. This is not blindness (*ʿamā*); it is **to flounder having lost one's direction.** A blind man knows he does not see and acts accordingly; the *ʿāmih* supposes that he sees, and goes round in a circle. The second is more dangerous, because he asks for no help.

### 2/16 — The metaphor of trade

*"Those are the ones who have bought straying at the price of guidance. This transaction brought them no profit, and they did not find the way."*

Here the Qurʾān moves into Mecca's own language: buying, selling, profit, loss. Mecca was a merchant city; measure, the reckoning of profit, the notion of capital were part of daily speech. The Qurʾān uses this language often — the *khusr* (loss) of Sūrat al-ʿAṣr, the address in Sūrat al-Ṣaff, "shall I show you a trade that will save you", all come from the same vein.

The description of the transaction must be read carefully: **they gave guidance and took straying.** That is, they had something in their hands and they let it go. This shows that straying is placed not as "never having had" but as "trading away what one held".

---

## 2/17-20 — Two comparisons

The section closes with two long tableaux. Both are built on light and darkness, but they describe two different conditions.

### The first tableau: the fire that goes out (17-18)

*"Their case is like the case of one who kindles a fire: when it lit up what was around him, God took away their light and left them in darknesses, unable to see. Deaf, dumb, blind; they will not return."*

**The finest detail is here:** the man kindled a **fire**, but God took away their **light**. The text does not say "nārihim" (their fire), it says **"nūrihim"** (their light).

Fire has two properties: it **gives light** and it **burns.** The verse says that the illuminating side has been taken and the burning side has been left. They have fire in their hands — but it no longer serves, it only harms.

The concrete counterpart of this is: the person has acquired a religious belonging, and has for a moment seen its light — the phrase "when it lit up what was around him" says this, so there had been light for a moment. But once he is emptied out inside, what remains is only its obligation, its risk and its power to burn; the function of showing the way has gone.

**The darknesses are plural, the light is singular.** This too is a consistent pattern in the Qurʾān: *nūr* always comes singular, *ẓulumāt* always plural (*"He brings them out of the darknesses into the light"*, al-Baqara 2/257). The right is one, the wrong innumerable.

The triad **"deaf, dumb, blind"** corresponds to the three organs of verse 7 — but here the organs are sound and the function is missing. And the order is worth noticing: dumbness stands in the middle. One who does not hear cannot learn, one who does not learn cannot speak, one who cannot speak cannot see his way. When the chain closes, *"they will not return"*.

### The second tableau: walking in the storm (19-20)

*"Or like those caught in a downpour from the sky, in which there are darknesses, thunder and lightning. They thrust their fingers into their ears against the thunderbolts, in fear of death… The lightning all but snatches away their sight. Whenever it lights their way they walk on, and when darkness falls upon them they stand still."*

The second tableau describes a different type from the first. In the first, the light came once and went entirely; here the light comes and goes **at intervals**.

The portrait is extremely concrete: the man takes a few steps when the lightning flashes and freezes where he stands when the darkness comes down. The advance is broken, insecure, dependent on outward conditions.

This is the description of a second and more common type: the person has not broken away entirely. When a warning comes, in a moment of fear, at a funeral, in an illness, he moves; when conditions return to normal he stops. His walking is determined not by his own decision but by a shock arriving from outside.

The word **صَيِّب** is from the root *ṣ-w-b* — both **to pour down** and **to hit the mark**. From the same root comes *iṣāba*. A downpour is something that finds its target; it cannot be escaped.

**Their thrusting their fingers into their ears** is meaningful in its own right. This movement, made to protect against the thunder, is of no use — cutting off the sound does not stop the thunderbolt. Not hearing the warning does not remove the danger warned of.

The section closes thus: *"Had God willed, He would have taken away their hearing and their sight."* That is, what they have heard and seen up to now is itself an opening; it has not yet been taken from them. The section ends not with a ruling but with a door left open.

### What the two tableaux have in common

Both say the same thing: **the light comes from outside.** Neither can the man who kindles a fire hold the light in his hand, nor can the man in the storm summon the lightning. The one thing in a person's hands is to walk when the light comes.

And both tableaux are built on **movement**: in the first, movement has stopped altogether ("they will not return"); in the second it is intermittent. If one recalls the expression used of the muttaqīn in the fifth verse of the sūra — *to be upon the road* — the contrast here is complete: one is walking on top of the road, the others are standing in the dark.

---

## 2/21-22 — The first command

*"O people! Serve your Lord, who created you and those before you; perhaps you will guard yourselves. He who made the earth a bed for you and the sky a building; who sent down water from the sky and brought forth with it fruits as provision for you. So do not set up equals to God when you know."*

### The first command in the Qurʾān in the order of the muṣḥaf

The twenty verses up to here were depiction and description. The first sentence built in the imperative comes here, and the command is: **serve.**

### The scope of the address

**"O people"** — the whole of humanity is addressed. This is a choice that deserves notice. In the sūras that came down in the Medinan period the predominant address is *"O you who believe"*; the general address to humanity is more the character of the Meccan sūras. Al-Baqara, for all that it is a Medinan sūra, gives its first command to all people.

The logic of this within the sūra is plain: the opening section had divided people into three (those who guard themselves, those who cover over, the two-faced). Once the division is complete the call is made to all of them at once. The classification, that is, was not made in order to throw anyone outside the call.

### Not "God" but "your Lord"

The command does not come in the form "serve God"; it comes as **"serve your Lord"**. And immediately afterwards the ground of this name is enumerated: the one who created you, who created those before you, who prepared the earth and the sky, who sent down the water, who brought forth the fruit.

The demand for service, then, is grounded not on authority but on **nurture and care**. Not "I am powerful, obey"; but "I brought you up". In al-Fātiḥa we saw that the word *Rabb* carries ownership and nurture together; here that sense turns into a proof.

### Four favours and two words

**فِرَاش (firāsh) — the earth a bed.** Root *f-r-sh*: to spread, to lay out. From the same root comes *farsh* (a spread, a carpet).

A misunderstanding has to be shut off at the outset here: **this expression does not say the earth is flat.** A bed is not flat either — it is spread out, it folds, it takes the shape of the ground beneath it. What the word says is not shape but **function**: the earth has been made such that one can stand on it, live on it, rest on it. The Qurʾān names the same function with other words too — it describes the earth as *mihād* (a cradle) and as *dhalūl* (a docile mount) (al-Nabaʾ 78/6; al-Mulk 67/15). All three say the same thing: this ground has been set up to be lived on.

**بِنَاء (bināʾ) — the sky a building.** Root *b-n-y*: to build, to construct. The sky is named not as "emptiness" but as **a structure that has been built**. Elsewhere the Qurʾān opens this further: *"And We made the sky a guarded roof"* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/32).

The expression "guarded roof" has a side that overlaps with what is known today — the atmosphere really is a layer that filters harmful radiation and the greater part of incoming bodies. I do not write this as a claim that the verse announces a scientific discovery; the verse does not speak of the atmosphere. I say only this: the word chosen for the sky is not "emptiness" but "structure", and that choice is one that says the thing above us is functional.

### أَندَاد — equals

Root *n-d-d*. *Nidd*: **an equal, a match, a rival.** Association of partners is described here not as "making idols" but as **"taking equals"**, and that description is far wider. One does not have to put a thing in God's place; it is enough to put it on the same line.

**وَأَنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ** — "and that while you know". The sentence attaches once more to the finding of verse 6: the issue is not ignorance but the covering of what is known.

---

## 2/23-24 — The challenge

*"If you are in doubt about what We have sent down to Our servant, then bring a sūra like it; and call your witnesses besides God, if you are truthful. If you do not do it — and you never will — then guard yourselves against the fire whose fuel is people and stones."*

### The link with the second verse of the sūra

In the second verse it was said *"there is no **rayb** in it"*. Here the same word comes back: *"if you are in **rayb**"*.

Set the two sentences side by side and the structure the sūra builds appears: the doubt is not in the book, the doubt is in you. And the claim is not left standing empty — a **test** is proposed.

Note also: for doubt the particle *fī* (in) is used once more. The pattern we saw in the fifth verse continues — negative states are things one is "inside".

### عَبْدِنَا — "Our servant"

The word used in speaking of the Prophet is **"Our servant"**. This is a consistent choice of the Qurʾān; the same word is used when the Ascension is recounted: *"He who took His servant by night…"* (al-Isrāʾ 17/1).

Even where the highest stations are named, the title is "servant". Prophethood is not a rank above servanthood; it is a task within servanthood.

### نَزَّلْنَا — the manner of sending down

Arabic has two different patterns from the same root and the Qurʾān uses both: *anzala* and *nazzala*. The second (the one used here) states that the act is done **piece by piece, repeatedly**. That the Qurʾān came down spread over twenty-three years is recorded in the pattern of the word.

### The character of the challenge

The Qurʾān makes this challenge in more than one place and on different scales: bring a discourse (al-Ṭūr 52/34), bring ten sūras (Hūd 11/13), bring one sūra (al-Baqara 2/23; Yūnus 10/38). The classical reading takes this as a gradual narrowing — the demand was made smaller and smaller and was still not met. But since the order in which these sūras came down is not known with certainty, the chronological construction "much was asked first, then it was reduced" is a supposition; that has to be stated.

**Here one must be honest.** This claim is a literary claim, and judgements of literary superiority are in part subjective; it is not possible to prove by any laboratory measure that a text is "without equal". The Qurʾān's own proof is historical: this challenge was made to a community who were the most accomplished users of the language and who had every reason to refuse, and that community chose to meet the text not with imitation but with weapons.

This proof deserves to be taken seriously, but it does not close the matter on its own. In my judgement the most defensible side of the claim is not beauty of style but **wholeness**: that a text spread over twenty-three years, over continually changing conditions, over war and peace, victory and loss, should keep its language, its measure and its inner consistency. I write this not as a ruling but as my own assessment.

**وَلَن تَفْعَلُوا۟** — "you never will". In Arabic *lan* is a definite negation directed at the future. For a text to issue such a ruling about itself means leaving itself permanently open to a test.

---

## 2/25 — The other side: the good news

*"Give good news to those who believe and do righteous deeds: for them are gardens with rivers running beneath them…"*

Immediately after the verse of punishment comes the verse of good news. This is the general rhythm of the Qurʾān: threat and good news are never left alone.

**بَشِّرْ — give good news.** Root *b-sh-r*, and the primary sense of this root is **skin**: *bashara* is the outer skin of the face. Good news is given this name because good news **shows in the skin of the face** — when a person receives good news his face opens, his colour changes. From the same root comes *bashar* (human being): the creature whose skin shows, who is not covered with fur.

**جَنَّة — garden.** Root *j-n-n*: **to cover, to conceal.** The derivatives of this root are surprisingly wide, and all of them turn around invisibility:

- *janīn* — the one covered in the womb
- *jinn* — the unseen creature
- *majnūn* — one whose mind is covered over
- *mijann* — a shield (that one hides behind)
- *janna* — the garden that covers the ground with its trees

The word *janna*, then, carries both "the garden that covers with its greenery" and "what is hidden from sight". What is promised is, by its very definition, what cannot now be seen.

**مُتَشَٰبِهًا — "resembling one another".** A qualification used of the fruits of the garden in the sense of "like what is in this world, but not the same". This determines how all the descriptions of the hereafter are to be read: the account has to be made by way of things the hearer knows — because language can find no material other than human experience. That the descriptions are sensory follows not from what is described being sensory but from **there being no other way to tell it**.

---

## 2/26-27 — The gnat

*"God is not ashamed to strike a comparison with a gnat, or with what is above it. Those who believe know that it is the truth from their Lord; those who cover over say 'what did God mean by this comparison?' By it He leads many astray, and by it He guides many aright."*

### Context

It is transmitted in the classical commentaries that this verse came down after mockery was made of the Qurʾān's giving small and seemingly trivial creatures (the fly, the spider, the ant) as examples. The objection was: does an exalted book speak of things as small as this?

### The verse's answer

The answer is that the size of the example is immaterial: the value of a comparison comes not from the dimensions of the thing compared but from **the truth of what it shows**. Small is not low.

**فَمَا فَوْقَهَا** — the expression "what is above it" has been understood in two ways: above in size (something larger), or above in smallness (something smaller still). The second reading is possible in Arabic and appears better suited to the context: "a gnat, or even something smaller".

### The real question: one text, two outcomes

The most important sentence of the verse is its last: **the same example** leads many astray and guides many aright.

The text is one. The outcomes are two. What produces the difference between them is not the text but the way the reader approaches it. This completes the measure of the sūra's second verse: the book was a guidance "for those who guard themselves". The same water makes one plant grow and rots another; the difference is not in the water but in the seed.

---

## 2/28 — A proof in four stages

*"How can you cover over God? You were dead, and He gave you life; then He will make you die, then He will give you life again, then to Him you will be returned."*

In a short verse there is a piece of reasoning in four steps:

| | Stage | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | You were dead | The state of non-being |
| 2 | He gave life | The present life — **already realised** |
| 3 | He will make die | Accepted, not disputed |
| 4 | He will give life | **Objected to** |

The proof is this: the fourth step you object to is a repetition of the second step, which has already occurred once. Someone who has experienced being brought into being out of nothing cannot count being brought into being again impossible. The Qurʾān builds this proof still more plainly elsewhere: *"It is He who begins the creation, and repeating it is easier for Him"* (al-Rūm 30/27).

The force of the argument lies in its using the hearer's own existence as evidence — it asks for no proof from outside.

---

## 2/29 — Whom is the earth for?

*"It is He who created for you everything on the earth. Then He turned to the sky and ordered it into seven heavens. He is the knower of all things."*

### لَكُم — "for you"

These two letters are among the fundamental expressions that establish the human being's relation to nature, and they can be read in two directions:

- As a **right of use**: everything has been placed at your disposal.
- As a **trust**: everything has been handed over to you.

If we leave the verse on its own, the first comes forward. But the Qurʾān does not leave it on its own: **in the very next verse** the human being is called a "khalīfa" (2/30) — that is, a deputy, one who looks after in another's place. A deputy has no property of his own; he has an account to render.

The measure that emerges when the two verses are read together is this: the earth has been given over to the human being's **use**, not to his **ownership**. Elsewhere the Qurʾān attaches corruption directly to human action: *"Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what people's hands have earned"* (al-Rūm 30/41).

The weight this distinction carries today is plain. The sentence "everything was created for me", on its own, legitimises unlimited use; together with the clause "I shall render an account for it", it produces a responsibility. The Qurʾān has set the two side by side.

### سَبْعَ سَمَٰوَٰت — seven heavens

The number "seven" must be dwelt on, because this is very fertile ground for forced interpretations.

There are two readings, and both are present in the classical sources:

- **Seven as a count.** Seven distinct layers are meant; their natures have not been made known to us.
- **Seven as conveying multitude.** In Arabic — and in the Semitic languages generally — the numbers seven, seventy and seven hundred are often used not for a particular count but in the sense of "very many". The Qurʾān itself makes this use: *"Even if you were to ask forgiveness for them seventy times…"* (al-Tawba 9/80) — what is meant here is not exactly seventy but "however many times".

Which is meant is not certain. To close off this uncertainty and produce a modern list saying "the seven heavens are such and such layers" is not exegesis but forcing; that road is not taken in this text.

### A bridge to the next verse

This verse brings the human being onto the stage by saying "everything on the earth is for you". The section that follows immediately — the creation of Adam (Ādam), the question of the angels, the teaching of the names, the prostration and Iblīs — will unfold at length what this sentence means. The sūra first states what has been given to the human being, then begins to tell who that human being is.

---

# 2/30-39 — Adam

Here the Qurʾān moves into narrative. Up to this point there was description, proof and command; now a scene is set. And the scene that is set is the answer to the question of what the human being is.

---

## 2/30 — The khalīfa and the question of the angels

*"When your Lord said to the angels, 'I am going to place a khalīfa on the earth', they said, 'Will you place there one who will spread corruption in it and shed blood, while we glorify You with Your praise and hallow You?' He said, 'I know what you do not know.'"*

### خَلِيفَة — khalīfa

Root *kh-l-f*: **to come after, to take another's place behind him.** From the same root come *khalaf* (the one who comes after), *khilāfa*, and, interestingly, *ikhtilāf* (to be left behind → to fall into disagreement).

The word has two readings, and both are present in the classical sources:

| Reading | Meaning | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **Deputy** | One who acts on earth on God's behalf | The word's sense of "one who takes another's place"; the authority given to the human being |
| **Successor** | One who comes on earth after those before him | The places where the Qurʾān uses the word in the plural: *"It is He who made you successors on the earth"* (al-Anʿām 6/165) — here the sense is plainly "generations following one another" |

Some scholars have entered a reservation about the first reading, on this ground: a deputy stands where the principal is absent; no such absence can be conceived of God. The reservation is well placed. But the sense of **authority and responsibility** that the word carries is not lost in the second reading either: one who takes the place of the one before him is responsible for the trust he has received.

### The question of the angels

The angels make an extremely specific prediction about a creature not yet created: **corruption and the shedding of blood.**

How they knew this is not stated in the Qurʾān. The possibilities the exegetes have produced are these: that they had seen the fate of an earlier community on the earth; that they drew an inference from the word "khalīfa" itself (a creature ruling on earth and disposing by its own will over limited resources necessarily comes into conflict); or that it had been made known to them. The text is silent, and so I do not speak with certainty here.

**The question itself matters.** It is not a rebellion. The angels do not refuse the command, they are trying to understand — and when they receive the answer they accept it. That the Qurʾān has recorded this question is worth noticing: asking a question finds a legitimate place within the text. What is rejected is the stance of Iblīs, not the question of the angels.

### What each side sees

The angels also present their own ground: "we glorify and hallow You". That is: we are here for this task, why another?

The answer offers no proof: **"I know what you do not know."** But the verses that follow will show what this knowledge is.

The picture that emerges is this:

- **What the angels see is correct.** The human being really does corrupt and shed blood; history has confirmed this more than enough. The verse does not reject that observation.
- **What the angels do not see** is the human being's other end: the capacity to learn, to name, to go beyond himself.

The difference lies here: the angel is fixed, the human being variable. A fixed being neither falls nor rises. The human capacity for evil and the human capacity for rising are two faces of one and the same property — **the will.** It is not possible to take one and leave the other.

---

## 2/31-33 — The names

*"He taught Adam all the names, then presented them to the angels and said, 'Tell me the names of these, if you are truthful.' They said, 'Glory be to You; we have no knowledge except what You have taught us.'"*

### Where the human being's superiority is established

Note carefully: Adam's position before the angels is not established by **worship**. Nor could it have been — the angels already worship without interruption, and they say so themselves.

The position is established by **knowledge**. And specifically by one kind of knowledge: **naming.**

### الْأَسْمَاء كُلَّهَا — "all the names"

What is meant has been debated: the names of things, the faculty of language itself, the realities of beings, or the names of God. The preference of the majority is the first two — the names of things and the capacity to name them.

This preference is worth dwelling on, because **naming is no ordinary skill.**

To give a thing a name requires the following: to distinguish that thing from its surroundings; to gather its likes into a category; to attach the category to a sound; and to be able to use that sound when the object is not present. The last item is decisive — **to be able to speak about what is not there.** The past, the future, abstract concepts, things that do not exist, things that might: all rest on this capacity.

This is also the point to which modern research on language points when it separates the human being from other living creatures: many animals communicate, but producing an unlimited number of new sentences and referring to what is not present appears to be peculiar to the human being. That the verse should choose exactly this faculty as the human being's distinguishing attribute is worth noticing.

I do not write this as a claim of miracle — the verse is not doing linguistics. I say only this: with dozens of properties available for defining the human being (power, reason, feeling, worship), what is chosen is naming, and that is a well-aimed choice.

### The angels' answer

**"We have no knowledge except what You have taught us."**

This sentence sets out the real difference between the two kinds of being. The angels' knowledge is **given and closed**: as much as has been given, that much. The human being's knowledge is **open-ended**: he can learn, err, correct, accumulate.

This openness is a risk as much as a superiority. A creature that can learn can also learn wrongly; a creature that can accumulate accumulates error too. The angels' concern and the human being's superiority are two outcomes of the same capacity.

---

## 2/34 — The prostration and Iblīs

*"When We said to the angels, 'Prostrate yourselves to Adam', they all prostrated; except Iblīs. He refused, was arrogant, and became one of those who cover over."*

### To whom is this prostration?

There is wide agreement that this prostration is not a prostration of worship — because worship is offered to God alone, and it is He who commanded this. The classical explanations: prostration is a form of respect and greeting (bowing before an elder was a widespread expression of reverence in ancient societies); or Adam is a point of orientation to turn towards, and the prostration is to God.

In any case it is God who gives the command; obedience or rebellion is therefore directed at Him. What Iblīs refused is not Adam but the command.

### أَبَىٰ وَٱسْتَكْبَرَ — two verbs

The verse uses two distinct verbs, and their order is meaningful:

**أَبَىٰ (abā)** — he refused, he would not come near. The act itself.
**ٱسْتَكْبَرَ (istakbara)** — he was arrogant. The ground of the act.

The pattern of the second word matters. The *istifʿāl* form conveys **wanting something or counting it so**. *Istikbār*, that is, is not to **be** great but to **count oneself** great. The word itself says that arrogance is not a reality but a supposition.

The Qurʾān gives Iblīs's ground plainly elsewhere: *"I am better than he; You created me from fire and created him from clay"* (al-Aʿrāf 7/12).

The finding that follows is important: **arrogance is born of a comparison.** Iblīs makes no claim of value on his own account; he measures himself against another and comes out superior. Arrogance is not seeing oneself as high but **seeing another as low**; its measure is always someone else.

And the comparison itself is faulty: to compare fire with earth and declare fire superior is an assumption. Fire burns and consumes; earth carries and brings forth. Iblīs invented a hierarchy and put himself at the top of it.

### وَكَانَ مِنَ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ — "he became one of those who cover over"

This expression confirms the definition we set up in the sixth verse and offers a very clear example.

**Iblīs does not deny the existence of God.** On the contrary: he speaks with Him, hears His command, asks for respite until the day of resurrection, and his request is granted. He believes, that is, both in God and in the hereafter. He is not an "atheist" in the modern sense; theoretically there is nothing missing from his system of belief.

For all that, he is declared one who covers over. Because **unbelief is not a lack of knowledge but the covering over of what is known** — what is covered over here is the doing of what the truth he sees requires. Iblīs knows and refuses all the same.

This says something fundamental about the Qurʾān's understanding of faith: faith is not assent to a list of propositions. Iblīs assents to the whole list and still stands on the other side.

**Is Iblīs an angel?** The arrangement of the verse ("We said to the angels… except Iblīs") appears to count him among the angels, but the Qurʾān says plainly elsewhere: *"He was of the jinn, and he departed from his Lord's command"* (al-Kahf 18/50). The majority of exegetes, relying on the second verse, say that he was a jinn and that he fell within the scope of the command because he was present with the angels.

---

## 2/35-36 — The prohibition and the slip

*"We said, 'Adam, dwell, you and your wife, in the garden, and eat freely from where you wish; but do not approach this tree, or you will be among the wrongdoers.' Satan made their feet slip from there and brought them out of the state they were in."*

### Which tree?

The Qurʾān does not say. Guesses such as wheat, grapes and figs are transmitted in the classical commentaries; none has support from the text, and in all likelihood they come from the surrounding cultures.

Where the text is silent, one must be silent. The species of the tree has no effect anywhere in the story — because the issue is not the tree but **the boundary itself.** The subject of the prohibition is arbitrary; what is tested is obedience.

### لَا تَقْرَبَا — "do not approach"

A small but important detail. The prohibition is not framed as "do not eat" but as **"do not approach"**.

The boundary, that is, begins not with the act itself but **with the distance that leads to the act**. This is one of the textual grounds for the principle known in Islamic law as "closing the means": if a thing is forbidden, the road leading to it is put under precaution too. The Qurʾān uses the same pattern elsewhere — *"Do not approach fornication"* (al-Isrāʾ 17/32); it is not the act but the coming near that is forbidden.

The practical logic is simple: a person cannot stand firmly right at the edge of a boundary. Standing at the edge is another name for falling.

### Whose fault? — a point that must be dwelt on

One must attend to the Qurʾān's grammar in this narrative, because there is a widespread cultural misunderstanding.

**All the verbs are in the dual (two-person) form:** "dwell", "eat", "do not approach", "made slip", "brought out". There is **no expression anywhere in the Qurʾān** that separately blames Eve (Ḥawwāʾ), positions her as a temptress, or loads the responsibility onto her.

In Sūrat Ṭāhā the narrative is given in slightly more detail, and there the one addressed by the whispering is Adam directly: *"Satan whispered to him"* (Ṭāhā 20/120), and then *"they both ate of it"* (20/121). The woman is not presented as an intermediary.

This has not always been read so in the history of exegesis — narratives from pre-Islamic and neighbouring cultures have seeped into some classical commentaries. But **the text itself** does not permit that reading. To stay faithful to the text of the Qurʾān requires taking as one's basis here the grammar of the verse, not the widespread cultural interpretation.

### أَزَلَّهُمَا — "made their feet slip"

Root *z-l-l*: **to slip, a stumbling of the foot.** From the same root comes *zalla* — a slip, a mistake made without intending it.

The choice of word sets the tone of the narrative. The story of the human being begins not with a **rebellion** but with a **slip**. Iblīs "refused and was arrogant" — a conscious, insistent, reasoned refusal. Adam "slipped". The difference between the two verbs also explains the difference in the fate of the two beings: one was driven out, the other forgiven.

---

## 2/37 — Repentance

*"Adam received certain words from his Lord, and God accepted his repentance. He is the one who accepts repentance again and again, the merciful."*

### تَلَقَّىٰ — "received, met with"

The finest point of the verse is here. Adam does not find the words of repentance himself; he receives them from his Lord.

The possibility of return, then, is a gift given afterwards to the one who wants to return. A person feels remorse, but he has to be taught even how to express his remorse. The door of repentance is not opened by the effort of the one repenting; it has already been left open.

What the words were the Qurʾān gives elsewhere: *"Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we shall surely be among the losers"* (al-Aʿrāf 7/23).

Note the structure of this prayer: the taking on of the fault ("we have wronged ourselves"), the production of no excuse, and an unconditional request. Compared with the stance of Iblīs the difference is clear — Iblīs spoke too, but instead of accepting his fault he justified it, and even loaded the fault onto God (*"Because You have led me astray…"*, al-Aʿrāf 7/16).

**The two beings were in the same situation: they broke the command. What makes the difference is what they did afterwards.**

### تَابَ — to turn

Root *t-w-b*: **to turn, to come back.** And what is striking is this: the same root is used both of the servant and of God.

- The servant *tāba ila'llāh* — he turns **towards** God.
- God *tāba ʿalayh* — He turns **upon** the servant.

The turning, then, goes in two directions. The Qurʾān says this very plainly in one place: *"Then He turned to them, that they might turn"* (al-Tawba 9/118) — God's turning comes **before** the servant's turning. The servant is able to turn because he has already been turned to.

The pattern **تَوَّاب** also conveys intensity: one who accepts again and again, continually. Repentance is not a one-time affair.

### A comparison

This verse has a heavy consequence for how the human being sees himself. In the Qurʾānic narrative the fall:

- **Has been forgiven.** Adam comes down to the earth as one already pardoned; not as one in debt.
- **Is not inherited.** The Qurʾān establishes this principle generally as well: *"No bearer of burdens bears the burden of another"* (al-Anʿām 6/164). Later generations are not held responsible for Adam's slip.
- **Is not a loss of status but the beginning of a task.** The descent is framed not as a punishment but as the actual beginning of the office of khalīfa — indeed that office had already been announced in verse 30, before the fall. The earth was the goal; the garden was a preparation.

I write this not in order to criticise other traditions of belief but to show the Qurʾān's own inner logic. These three items mean that the human being sees himself not as guilty by birth but as responsible by birth.

---

## 2/38-39 — The descent and the promise

*"We said, 'Go down from it, all of you.' 'When guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance — no fear shall be upon them, nor shall they grieve.'"*

### The venture on earth does not begin without a guide

Immediately after the command to descend comes a promise: **guidance will come.**

This sentence is the starting point of the chain of revelation. The human being is not abandoned to the earth; he is sent to the earth, and it is said to him that a guide will be sent after him. The Qurʾān positions itself as the last link of this promise.

And here a circle closes back to al-Fātiḥa. In al-Fātiḥa the servant had asked *"guide us to the straight path"*. In this verse of al-Baqara it is said that the answer to that request was promised from the very beginning. The request is not new; nor is the answer.

### لَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ

This formula, repeated across the Qurʾān, names the human being's two basic pains, and the two look to different times:

| | Direction | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| **Khawf** (fear) | **The future** | What has not yet happened, but might |
| **Ḥuzn** (grief) | **The past** | What has happened and cannot be taken back |

The human being's unease comes from these two directions: either he grieves for what he has lost or he fears for what he will lose. The present moment, meanwhile, is mostly squeezed between the two and passes unlived.

What is promised is not that events will change — the person will lose again, will meet danger again. What is promised is the knowledge that both these directions have an owner. If the past can be forgiven, grief loses its ground; if the future is in the hands of a Lord, fear loses its ground.

### Where the narrative leaves things

In ten verses the following has been said about the human being: he holds authority on the earth; he has a capacity for corruption and bloodshed; he has a capacity for naming and learning; he can transgress a boundary; but he can return, and his return is accepted; and he will not be left without a guide.

This is not praise or blame — it is a **description**. And the rest of the sūra is the long history of communities that have and have not matched that description.

---

# 2/40-46 — The address to the Children of Israel begins

From here the sūra enters a long section. For some eighty verses one single community will be addressed: those who had been given a book before.

**Why is this section so long?** Because what is recounted is not the story of a foreign nation. It is the earlier link of the same chain — in the fourth verse of the sūra it was said "they believe in what was sent down before you". And more importantly: what is recounted here is the list of **what can happen to a community that has received a book**. A community receiving a new book is reading what a community that received a book before it went through.

This has to be said at the outset, because the most widespread misreading of this section is to suppose it to be about somebody else. The Qurʾān's own record prevents this: *"They are not all alike; among the People of the Book there is … an upright community"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/113). Attributes are being enumerated, not an identity — and whoever carries the attribute, the ruling looks to him.

---

## 2/40 — The covenant

*"Children of Israel! Remember My favour that I bestowed on you, and fulfil your covenant with Me and I shall fulfil My covenant with you. And fear Me alone."*

### إِسْرَٰٓءِيل — Isrāʾīl

It is the second name of the prophet Jacob (Yaʿqūb); the Qurʾān plainly intimates this in one place (Āl ʿImrān 3/93). "Banū Isrāʾīl" = the sons of Jacob, that is, the name of a lineage.

The origin of the word is not Arabic. In Islamic sources it is commonly explained as "ʿAbdullāh" — *isrā* (servant) + *īl* (God). In the Hebrew tradition another meaning is given to the word. In this commentary I transmit the explanation found in the Islamic sources and make no claim to a settled etymology; that the word is of foreign origin makes a definite solution difficult.

### نِعْمَتِى — "My favour", singular

The favours to be counted are many: deliverance, a book, prophets, a land. For all that, the word comes **singular**. In Arabic this use states the genus — the kind of favour, not its individual items. Elsewhere the Qurʾān says why: *"If you tried to count God's favour you could not number it"* (Ibrāhīm 14/34). What cannot be counted can only be named in the singular.

### عَهْد — covenant

Root *ʿ-h-d*. Its primary sense is **to guard, to watch over, to look back at again and again.** From the same root come *taʿahhud* (to undertake), *muʿāhada* (a treaty) and *maʿhad* (a known, familiar place).

The sense of "looking back" at the core of the word matters: a covenant is not a word given once and forgotten but a bond **that must be continually remembered and watched over**. Left untended, it decays.

**أَوْفُوا۟** — root *w-f-y*: to complete, to pay in full. The Arabic *wafāʾ* comes from it, and Turkish has borrowed the same word as *vefâ*, "faithfulness". The verse does not say "keep your word", it says "**pay your word in full**". Partial fulfilment does not count as fulfilment.

### The surprising structure of the sentence

**"Fulfil your covenant with Me and I shall fulfil My covenant with you."**

A mutual contract is set up here, and it has two parties. This shows that the divine–human relationship is not framed as a mere relation of command and obedience: a bond is described in which both sides have an obligation.
