This too must be added: this reciprocity does not mean an equality between the parties. The one who sets the terms of the contract and the one who gives the power to fulfil it are the same party. But that the Qurʾān has chosen this language is meaningful — it addresses the human being with a notion by which he can grasp his obligation: a contract.

**وَإِيَّٰىَ فَٱرْهَبُونِ** — "fear Me alone". Note: *iyyāya* has been brought forward. It is the same structure as *iyyāka naʿbudu* in the fourth verse of al-Fātiḥa: bringing forward conveys restriction.

Fear with **رَهْبَة**: root *r-h-b*. This is not sheer terror; it is a shrinking mixed with respect, a keeping of distance. From the same root comes *rāhib* (monk) — one who has devoted himself and withdrawn.

---

## 2/41 — Do not be the first to reject it

*"Believe in what I have sent down confirming what is with you; and do not be the first to reject it. And do not trade My verses for a small price."*

**مُصَدِّقًا لِّمَا مَعَكُمْ** — "confirming what is with you". The new book is presented not as the rival of the old but as its **confirmer**. This is the same line as the condition of faith in the fourth verse of the sūra: all the revelations are links of one chain.

**أَوَّلَ كَافِرٍۭ بِهِ** — "the first to reject it". At first sight this expression looks strange, because historically there had already been those who rejected — the mushrikūn of Mecca.

The classical explanation is this: being "first" is a matter not of sequence but of **position**. The rejection of one who does not know binds only himself. But the rejection of one who knows **sets a precedent** — it produces a ground for those who come after. When a person of the Book says "this is not true", the word is carried as though it were a verdict coming from knowledge. Their rejection is therefore first in effect, even if it comes later in sequence.

**ثَمَنًا قَلِيلًا** — "a small price". To sell the verses: to turn knowledge into advantage, to state the ruling according to one's interest, to keep silent where one need not and to speak where one should not.

The adjective "small" is worth dwelling on. The price may not really be small — position, wealth, security are large things. The adjective belongs not to the amount of the price but to **the nature of the exchange**: anything that can be received in return for what is sold is small in comparison with what is sold. Every price of a thing of infinite worth is low.

The first addressees of this verse are people who possess knowledge. The real warning therefore looks to everyone who holds religious knowledge in his hands — including the Muslim scholar reading this section. Indeed in the classical exegetical tradition this verse has been one of the warnings most often recalled to the people of learning.

---

## 2/42 — Two distinct offences

*"Do not clothe the truth in falsehood, and do not conceal the truth while you know."*

The verse forbids two distinct acts, and they are different things.

### لَا تَلْبِسُوا۟ — "do not dress"

Root *l-b-s*: **to wear, to clothe.** From the same root comes *libās* (garment) — the famous comparison the Qurʾān uses for spouses is in this word too (al-Baqara 2/187).

When the verse says "do not mix", the word it uses says **"do not dress falsehood in the garment of truth"**. This is something different from concealing, and more dangerous:

| | What it does | Its result |
|---|---|---|
| **Concealing** (*katm*) | Makes the true invisible | The person is left without knowledge — but he knows he does not know |
| **Dressing** (*talbīs*) | Gives falsehood the dress of truth | The person knows wrongly — and supposes he knows rightly |

The second is heavier, because it leaves no gap. A person without knowledge may search; a person wrongly informed does not.

Its method is familiar too: no lying is required. To give correct information incompletely, to put a true sentence in a false context, to use a genuine piece of evidence for a conclusion it does not support — all of these fall under this description. The material is truth; the structure built is falsehood.

**وَتَكْتُمُوا۟ ٱلْحَقَّ** — the second offence: concealing. The silence of one who knows.

**وَأَنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ** — "and that while you know". This clause sets the definition of unbelief we established in the sixth verse of the sūra to work here as well: the issue is not ignorance but what is done with what is known.

---

## 2/43 — Three commands

*"Keep up the prayer, give the alms, and bow with those who bow."*

The first two commands are familiar; the third is striking. It does not say "bow" — it says **"bow with those who bow"**.

What is asked, that is, is not only that the act of worship be performed but that it be **performed together**. At the beginning of the sūra the prayer was to be "kept up" (held standing); here a communal dimension is added to that. The measure we saw in the plural of al-Fātiḥa's *"You alone do **we** serve"* turns here into a command.

The meaning of this command within its context also matters: the ones addressed are a community that has received a book but is divided. That the command given to a divided community should be "bow together" is no coincidence.

---

## 2/44 — Forgetting oneself

*"Do you enjoin righteousness upon people and forget yourselves? And you recite the Book! Will you not use your reason?"*

This verse is the most often quoted sentence in the whole section, and most of those who quote it use it against some other congregation. Yet the structure of the verse turns it back on **the one reading it**.

### بِرّ — birr

Root *b-r-r*. Its primary sense is **breadth**: from the same root comes *barr* — dry land, that is, the wide expanse set over against the sea. *Birr* is this breadth: goodness, in the word's own logic, is **the opposite of narrowness**. Miserliness narrows, goodness widens.

The Qurʾān defines this word plainly in one place, and the definition is surprisingly wide: *"Righteousness (birr) is not that you turn your faces to the east and the west. Righteousness is that of one who believes in God and the last day… and gives of his wealth, though he loves it, to relatives, orphans and the poor…"* (al-Baqara 2/177). *Birr*, then, is not a ceremony but a sum of conduct.

### تَنسَوْنَ أَنفُسَكُمْ — "you forget yourselves"

The verb is not "you do not do" but **"you forget"**. This is a finer diagnosis: the person is not deliberately leaving himself outside; while giving the counsel he simply **does not bring to mind** that he is among those addressed.

The mechanism is familiar. To explain a truth to someone else gives a person the feeling of possessing that truth. Explaining takes the place of doing. Indeed the better the explaining is done, the stronger the substitution becomes.

**وَأَنتُمْ تَتْلُونَ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ** — "and you go on reciting the Book". The verb is in the form conveying continuity: the reciting goes on. Contact with the text has not been broken; what has been broken is the bond between the text and the person.

### أَفَلَا تَعْقِلُونَ — "will you not use your reason?"

The sense of the root gives the end of the verse a force of its own.

Root *ʿ-q-l*: **to bind.** *ʿIqāl* is the rope tied round a camel's knee to stop it running off. Arabic has given the mind this name — reason, in the word's own logic, is **not a store of knowledge but a tether.** The thing that holds a person, that reins him in, that keeps him from being swept away.

That the verb in this verse comes from this root completes the sentence: you have knowledge, you recite, you even teach — but if the knowledge does not **bind** you, you have not used your reason. That is the difference between knowing and using reason: one is acquiring, the other is being bound.

---

## 2/45-46 — Patience and prayer

*"Seek help through patience and prayer. And it is a heavy thing indeed, except for those who are humble — those who know that they will meet their Lord and that to Him they are returning."*

### صَبْر — patience

Root *ṣ-b-r*: **to hold, to restrain, to confine.** To keep something in a place by force.

This differs from "putting up with". Putting up with is passive — you bear what comes upon you. Patience is active: **to hold the soul**, not to let it go where it wants to go. This is why patience is asked for not only in affliction but in worship and in refraining from sin; in all of them the work done is the same — holding.

In al-Fātiḥa it was said *"from You alone do we seek help"*. Here it is said by what means the help is to be sought: one from within (patience: holding oneself), the other from without (prayer: a regular, bodily, repeated bond).

### خُشُوع — humility

Root *kh-sh-ʿ*: **to soften, to become low, to bend.** The Qurʾān uses this root of the earth as well: *"You see the earth barren (khāshiʿa); when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells"* (Fuṣṣilat 41/39).

This use opens the spirit of the word. *Khushūʿ* is the breaking of hardness, the softening of the crust, coming to a state in which one can take in what arrives. Dry, hard ground does not absorb water, it lets it run off the surface. The same water, in softened ground, turns into a plant.

The logic of the verse is built from here: the prayer "is heavy for those who are not humble" — because for what is hard, every bending is difficult. For what has softened, the same movement is no burden.

### يَظُنُّونَ — "suppose" or "know"?

The word is from the root of **ẓann** and is often rendered "to suppose". But in Arabic this root, according to context, can carry both a doubtful guess and **a settled conviction** — in language this is called contrariety of sense.

That what is meant here is certain knowledge is plain; for the verse says it as praise, and no one is praised for doubting that he will "meet his Lord".

This two-sided character of the word is meaningful in itself: knowledge of the hereafter is not of the kind verified in a laboratory; but it can turn into a conviction that leaves no doubt in the one who holds it. To name this intermediate position the Qurʾān uses exactly the word that carries both senses at once.

### What the section ties together

In seven verses the following has been said: pay your word in full; do not deny what you know; do not sell your knowledge; neither conceal the truth nor disguise it; perform your worship together; say to yourself what you say to another; and let your support be patience and prayer.

None of this is peculiar to one community. All of it is the list of **the risks that come with having received a book** — and those risks hold for whoever has received the book.

---

# 2/47-61 — The favours and what was returned for them

This section proceeds like a list: each favour is named, and immediately after it comes the return made for that favour. The structure is itself an argument — what is being recounted is that ingratitude was not a single event but **a repeated pattern**.

---

## 2/47-48 — Preferment and the solitude of the reckoning

*"Children of Israel! Remember My favour that I bestowed on you, and that I preferred you above all beings. And guard yourselves against a day when no soul can pay anything for another; when no intercession is accepted from it, no ransom taken, and they are not helped."*

### فَضَّلْتُكُمْ عَلَى ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ — "I preferred you above all beings"

This sentence must be read carefully, because it can easily be turned into a claim of racial superiority — and the Qurʾān itself prevents that turn.

Three qualifications are needed:

**First, the context.** The verses that follow immediately after this sentence enumerate the repeated failings of the same community. Praise and criticism stand side by side. Had the thing praised been the community itself, the eighty verses that follow would make no sense.

**Second, the subject of the preferment.** The majority of the classical exegetes understand it as the **opportunities given**: that a book was given to them, that a great many prophets arose among them, that they were delivered from slavery. The preferment, that is, is a preferment **in task and in opportunity**, not a preferment of kind. If what is given is more, what will be asked is more too.

**Third, the Qurʾān's own measure.** The text sets out the measure of honour plainly elsewhere and closes off every claim of descent: *"The most honoured of you with God is the one who guards himself most"* (al-Ḥujurāt 49/13).

And the very next verse already cuts this claim off at the feet.

### Four negations

Verse 48 lists four things that will not work on that day:

| | The road closed |
|---|---|
| *lā tajzī nafsun ʿan nafsin* | No soul can pay for another |
| *wa lā yuqbalu minhā shafāʿatun* | No intercession is accepted |
| *wa lā yuʾkhadhu minhā ʿadlun* | No ransom is taken |
| *wa lā hum yunṣarūn* | They are not helped |

All four together say one thing: **the reckoning cannot be transferred.** To a community told in the previous verse "you were preferred", it is announced immediately afterwards that this is no guarantee. Descent, belonging, privilege in the past — none of these works on that day.

The word **عَدْل** here means not "justice" but **ransom**; the primary sense of the root is "what is equal" (the equivalent given in return for something). The same root names justice too, as "equivalence".

**A qualification concerning intercession.** Read on its own, this verse looks like an absolute rejection of intercession. But elsewhere — indeed within this same sūra — the Qurʾān accepts intercession as tied to permission: *"Who is there that can intercede with Him except by His permission?"* (al-Baqara 2/255). The picture that emerges when the two verses are read together is this: **there is no intercession that stands of itself, by right, that a person could count as a guarantee;** what is tied to permission is a separate matter. This subject is among the disputed headings in the history of theology; here I confine myself to setting the two verses side by side and state no side.

---

## 2/49-50 — Pharaoh and the sea

*"We delivered you from Pharaoh's people, who were visiting the worst torment upon you, slaughtering your sons and sparing your women… And We parted the sea and delivered you, and drowned Pharaoh's men before your eyes."*

**يَسُومُونَكُمْ** — root *s-w-m*: to put a good up for sale, to press something insistently, to keep placing it before someone. The verb states that the torment was not a single event but a state **continually pressed** upon them. The commercial root of the word also gives a sense that people were being disposed of like goods under negotiation.

**The killing of the male children and the sparing of the women** is named separately as a form of oppression. This is not a massacre born of rage but a calculated policy: to dry up the capacity for resistance at its source, to reduce a community to a workforce that goes on reproducing but cannot defend itself. That the Qurʾān records this detail is meaningful — the most dangerous form of oppression is not the enraged one but **the planned one**.

**بَلَآءٌ مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ عَظِيمٌ** — "a great trial from your Lord". In Turkish the borrowed word *belâ* has narrowed to calamity alone, and the usual English renderings show the same drift — "trial" and "affliction" both pull towards misfortune. In Arabic the root *b-l-w* means **to test, to try by wearing down**, and it covers testing by good as well. The Qurʾān says this outright: *"We test you with evil and with good, as a trial"* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/35).

Which of the two the "balāʾ" here points to has been debated: the oppression itself, or the deliverance? Both are defensible and both come out at the same principle — hardship is a test and so is deliverance. Indeed the rest of this section will recount how the second test turned out.

---

## 2/51-54 — The calf

*"We appointed forty nights for Moses (Mūsā). Then, after he had gone, you took the calf as a god…"*

Note the timing: the span between the parting of the sea and the making of the calf is forty days. That the narrative sets these two events one on top of the other is no coincidence — having seen the greatest of miracles is no guarantee for forty days later.

The finding that follows joins with the two comparisons at the beginning of the sūra (2/17-20): **seeing does not keep one on the road.** One had to walk when the light came; that the light came once is not enough.

**عِجْل (ʿijl)** — the calf. The classical commentaries transmit a play resting on the sound-kinship of the word with the root *ʿ-j-l* (haste): they hurried and did not wait for Moses, and their haste brought them an *ʿijl*. I transmit this as a point about the language; that the word really derives from this root is not certain, and it is no basis for a ruling.

### تُوبُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰ بَارِئِكُمْ — "turn back to your Maker"

The finest point of the verse is here, and in most translations it becomes invisible.

When the command to repent is given, it does not say "turn back to your Lord"; it says **"turn back to your Bāriʾ"**. *Bāriʾ* is from the root *b-r-ʾ*: to bring out of non-being into being, to create by separating and by making flawless. The same root is present in the words *barāʾa* (being cleared) and *bariyya* (created things).

Now consider the situation: these people **have made a statue.** They have cast metal, given it a shape, produced a form. And that God should be named, in the call to repentance, precisely by the name meaning **"the one who truly gives form, who brings into being from nothing"**, is a name set directly opposite the offence. What they have done is recalled to them by the name inside the call.

### فَٱقْتُلُوٓا۟ أَنفُسَكُمْ

The command "kill yourselves" is the heaviest sentence in the section, and the exegetes have differed over it:

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **Actual retaliation** | It is a real sentence of death passed upon those who worshipped the calf; it is peculiar to that law and produces no ruling for later communities |
| **In the sense of "one another"** | *Anfusakum* in Arabic can also mean "those among your own number"; it is a command to carry out the sentence upon the guilty |
| **Figurative** | What is meant is the killing of the self, that is, the ending of the ego that inclined to the calf |

The majority gathers around the first two views. I state no preference here; but this qualification has to be entered: this is the transmission of a ruling given to a particular community upon a particular event — it is not a general rule and produces no ruling for today.

---

## 2/55-56 — "Let us see God plainly"

*"You said, 'Moses, we will not believe you until we see God plainly', and the thunderbolt struck you."*

This verse stands in **exact opposition** to the third verse of the sūra, and the opposition looks deliberate.

| | |
|---|---|
| **al-Baqara 2/3** | Those who guard themselves believe in the *unseen* — they trust what is not visible |
| **al-Baqara 2/55** | *"We will not believe until we see God plainly"* |

In describing the person who would benefit from the book, the sūra had counted trust in the unseen as the first attribute. Here its exact contrary is staged: what is demanded ceases to be faith and becomes a report of observation.

The inconsistency inside the demand is plain too. These people **had seen** the sea parted. The issue, that is, is not a lack of proof; it is that after every proof another is asked for. The demand to see is not a demand that can be satisfied — because it does not wish to be satisfied.

---

## 2/57 — Shade, manna and quails

*"We made the cloud a shade over you and sent down to you manna and quails… They did not wrong Us, but they were wronging themselves."*

What exactly **مَنّ and سَلْوَىٰ** were is not known with certainty; the classical commentaries explain them as a sweetish substance and a kind of bird. No definite identification follows from the text, and I do not guess. What matters for the function of the narrative is a provision that came in the desert without labour.

### وَمَا ظَلَمُونَا وَلَٰكِن كَانُوٓا۟ أَنفُسَهُمْ يَظْلِمُونَ

This sentence is repeated many times in the Qurʾān and fixes the place of the notion of **wrong-doing** (*ẓulm*).

The root *ẓ-l-m* has two wings of meaning: **darkness** (*ẓulma*) and **putting a thing somewhere other than where it belongs.** The second is decisive for the definition: wrong-doing, in the word's own logic, is a work of **displacement**. To give a right to someone other than its owner, to direct worship to other than God, to transfer property to one who has not earned it — all are the same act.

And what the sentence says is this: the thing displaced is, in the end, the doer himself. No harm reaches God; the one who spoils the order loses his own place.

---

## 2/58-59 — The word that was changed

*"We said, 'Enter this town, eat freely from where you wish, enter the gate bowing low and say "ḥiṭṭa", and We shall forgive you your faults.' But those who did wrong changed the word said to them for another word."*

**حِطَّة (ḥiṭṭa)** — root *ḥ-ṭ-ṭ*: **to bring down, to set low, to unload a burden.** The word is a request for forgiveness in the sense of "take down our load, let our sin fall". The same root is used for the lowering of a price.

The command has two parts, and both convey lowering: **entering bent low** (the body) and **saying ḥiṭṭa** (the tongue). The one thing asked is that there be no arrogance at the moment of entry.

### Changing the word

The verse recounts what was done in a very narrow expression: *"they changed the word said to them for another word."* What they said is not stated — the only thing stated is that **another word was put in place of the word given.**

Why this should be counted so heavy an offence may not be clear at first sight. But the mechanism is familiar: to refuse an obligation openly is costly — you cross over to the other side. To **keep the formula and empty it out** instead is costless: an onlooker sees that it has been complied with, and the one complying has not complied.

This is of the same family as the *talbīs* of verse 42 of the sūra — there falsehood was dressed in the garment of truth, here the appearance of compliance with a command is set up. In neither is there any need to lie; the form is preserved, the function is cancelled.

---

## 2/60 — Twelve springs

*"Moses asked for water for his people. We said, 'Strike the rock with your staff.' Twelve springs gushed out of it; each tribe knew its own drinking place."*

The detail matters: the number of springs is **twelve**, and the number of tribes is twelve too. The water flows not from one place but from a mouth belonging to each community.

This is a **measure of governance** set inside the miracle. Had there been a single source in a waterless desert, the source would itself have become a cause of conflict — a fight over turns, over precedence, over control. At the same time as the water is given, the quarrel that would arise from sharing the water is solved.

What the Qurʾān records here is that meeting the need is not enough on its own: **a favour given without an order of sharing is a source of new trouble.**

---

## 2/61 — Onions and garlic

*"You said, 'Moses, we cannot endure one kind of food. Pray to your Lord to bring forth for us from what the earth grows — its greens, its cucumbers, its garlic, its lentils, its onions.' He said, 'Would you exchange what is lower for what is better?'"*

### أَدْنَىٰ — "lower" or "nearer"?

This is the key word in the verse, and it carries both senses at once.

Root *d-n-w*: **to be near** and **to be low.** From the same root comes **dunyā** — as we saw in the fourth verse, the name of this life is "the near one".

There is thus a second reading of the sentence, and it does not conflict with the first:

> "Would you exchange what is **nearer** for what is better?"

The language itself passes a judgement: **the near and the low are named by the same word.** What can be held, reached at once, tasted now is for that very reason low in value. The name the Qurʾān chooses for "this world" already carries this assessment within it.

The demand itself is also striking. What is asked for is no luxury — onions, garlic, lentils. Nothing better is being asked for; **something more familiar** is being asked for. These were the things eaten in Egypt.

The inclination here is the best-known state of a community that has come out of slavery: the uncertainty of freedom is more disturbing than the predictability of bondage. Provision that comes without labour but is unfamiliar is preferred less than food obtained by labour but familiar. A person can prefer an order he knows to be bad to an order he does not know to be good.

The whole pattern of this section is summed up here too: the problem is not, as the word "ingratitude" suggests, a lack of thanks. The problem is **the loss of the measure by which what is given would be valued** — and when the measure is lost, a person always compares what he has with what he has left behind.

---

## 2/62 — A label does not save

*"Surely those who believe, and the Jews, and the Christians, and the Sabians — whoever believes in God and the last day and does righteous work, they have their reward with their Lord; no fear shall be upon them, nor shall they grieve."*

This verse is among the most debated in the Qurʾān. To read it rightly one must first look at **how the sentence is built**.

### The structure of the sentence

The verse consists of two parts:

**The first part — four names are listed:** those who believe, the Jews, the Christians, the Sabians.

**The second part — a condition comes:** *"whoever believes in God and the last day and does righteous work…"*

And the ruling is attached not to the first part but **to the second**. The verse does not list four groups and say "these are saved"; it lists four groups and then sets down **a criterion**. Names are mentioned, but the reward is attached to the criterion, not to the names.

Its function within the context is plain. The preceding fifteen verses had been dismantling, step by step, a community's reliance on its own chosenness — in verse 47 it was said "I preferred you", and in verse 48 it was enumerated in four items that this gave no guarantee whatever. This verse completes the same work: **belonging is no security.** And the criterion is the same for everyone.

### هَادُوا۟ and ٱلصَّٰبِـِٔين

**هَادُوا۟** — root *h-w-d*: to turn, to come back, to repent. The Qurʾān does not use the noun "yahūd" here but the **verb** form: "those who became Jews". The same root occurs in the sense of repentance at al-Aʿrāf 7/156 (*"innā hudnā ilayk"* — "we have turned to You").

**ٱلصَّٰبِـِٔين** — root *ṣ-b-ʾ*: to leave one religion and go over to another. Who they were is not known with certainty; several possibilities are listed in the classical commentaries. The Qurʾān gives no description, and I do not guess.

### What does the verse say? — the views

| View | Explanation | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| **The criterion reading** | The verse lists the group names precisely to show that they carry no security value. Salvation is tied to the condition, not to the name | The reading most consonant with the context; the grammar of the sentence supports it too |
| **Historical scope** | What is meant are those who lived and died following their own prophets before Muhammad's message reached them | Widespread in the classical commentaries; there is no clause in the verse narrowing it so |
| **It has been abrogated** | Āl ʿImrān 3/85 (*"whoever seeks a religion other than Islam…"*) has lifted this verse | It meets a serious objection on grounds of method — see below |

**The objection raised against the claim of abrogation** is technical but important: according to the majority of the scholars of legal method, abrogation occurs in **rulings**, not in **statements of fact**. This verse is not a command but a declaration — it says "those who do thus have their reward". To abrogate a declaration would mean saying that it was false. Many scholars have therefore not accepted the claim of abrogation.

There is also a datum within the text: the same expression is repeated **twice more** in the Qurʾān (al-Māʾida 5/69; al-Ḥajj 22/17). A ruling said to have been abrogated would not be expected to be repeated in different sūras.

**The stance in this commentary:** I find the criterion reading the strongest, because it fits both the grammar of the sentence and the context it stands in. But I must state that the verse has been the subject of a wide debate in the history of theology and law, and that the preference given here is not binding. What the verse says **with certainty** is this, and there is no disagreement about it: **no community obtains a guarantee of salvation by being a member of it.** This was said to the first addressees as much as to the Muslim reading these lines.

---

## 2/63-66 — The mountain and the sabbath

*"We took a covenant from you and raised the Mount above you: 'Hold fast to what We have given you.'… You know well those among you who transgressed the sabbath. We said to them, 'Be apes, despised.'"*

**خُذُوا۟ مَا ءَاتَيْنَٰكُم بِقُوَّةٍ** — "hold **fast** to what We have given you". It is said that holding the book requires not a loose contact but a firmness of grip. What is held loosely does not stay in the hand.

### The matter of the sabbath and its place in this sūra

The detail of the event is given in Sūrat al-Aʿrāf (7/163): a coastal town where the fish appeared only on the sabbath; the prohibition forbade fishing on the sabbath. The solution transmitted is this: nets and pools are prepared on the sabbath, the fish go into them, and they are gathered on the Sunday. **No fishing was done on the sabbath** — technically.

This is the exact instance of what we saw at verse 59 of the sūra: there another word was put in the place of the word given; here the letter of the prohibition is preserved and its purpose cancelled. The prohibition appears to have been obeyed; the prohibition does not work.

And the mechanism is the same as the *talbīs* of verse 42 of the same sūra: there is no need to lie, the form is preserved, the function is emptied out. The Qurʾān marks this behaviour in three separate places with three different examples — which means that what is being described is not a single event but a **type**.

### قِرَدَةً خَٰسِـِٔينَ — "apes, despised"

Whether this expression is a real transformation or a figure is a classical disagreement:

- **A real transformation** — the view of the majority; this is the outward sense of the expression.
- **A figure** — some exegetes have understood that hearts and behaviour became ape-like, that is, fell into imitation and trickery. This view was voiced in the early period as well.

I state no preference. But this should be recorded: the animal chosen is not arbitrary. The ape is the animal that **imitates** — it copies the appearance and does not understand the function. And that is exactly what had been done: the appearance of the prohibition was preserved, its meaning imitated. The punishment is named in the language of the offence itself.

**خَٰسِـِٔين** — driven off, kept at a distance; from the root used for shooing an animal away.

---

# 2/67-74 — The cow

The sūra takes its name from here. That the longest sūra of the Qurʾān should be named after this short and strange story is worth dwelling on.

## 2/67-73 — The story of the cow

*"Moses said to his people, 'God commands you to slaughter a cow.' They said, 'Are you mocking us?' He said, 'I take refuge with God from being among the ignorant.'*

*They said, 'Pray to your Lord for us to make clear to us what it is.' He said, 'He says: a cow neither too old nor too young, but middling between the two. Now do what you are commanded.'*

*They said, 'Pray to your Lord for us to make clear to us what its colour is.' He said, 'He says: a yellow cow, bright in colour, giving joy to those who look at her.'*

*They said, 'Pray to your Lord for us to make clear to us what she is like; cows look alike to us. If God wills, we shall surely be guided.' He said, 'He says: a cow not broken to the yoke, that does not plough the soil or water the field, sound, with no spot on her.' They said, 'Now you have brought the truth.' And they slaughtered her; and they very nearly did not do it.*

*And when you killed a soul and fell out with one another over it — while God was going to bring out what you were hiding. We said, 'Strike him with a part of her.' Thus does God bring the dead to life and show you His signs — that perhaps you may use your reason."*

### The order of the telling is built backwards

The most striking side of the story is the order in which the events are recounted.

In the order of time the **murder** was committed first, then the killer could not be found, and then the command to slaughter the cow came. But the text does not follow this order. The command is given first (67), questions and answers run on for five verses (68-71), and only after these are finished does the answer to the question **why** arrive: *"And when you killed a soul…"* (72).

The reader, that is, reads the command without knowing its ground, exactly as the first addressees did. The telling makes the reader live through the state of knowledge of the ones addressed.

**I record this as an observation about narrative technique,** because its consequence attaches directly to the subject of the story: had the ground of the command been given at the outset, the questions would have looked meaningful — one could have said, "since a murder is to be solved, which cow it is matters". Because the ground is left to the end, the questions appear naked: there is nothing there that is not understood, only a thing not done.

And when the ground is disclosed, the questions turn retrospectively into something else. For among those asking the questions there is also someone who does **not want** the murder brought to light. The text does not say this; but by setting the order thus it leaves room for the reader to construct it himself.

### بَقَرَة — cow

Root **ب-ق-ر**: **to split, to open, to divide down the middle.** The lexicographers connect the animal's name with this root: the cow, when yoked to the plough, is the animal that **splits the soil**. From the same root come *bāqir* (splitter) and *tabaqqur* (to pick a matter apart and open it up).

That the word is from this root is striking when read together with verse 74, which comes immediately after: there the rock's **splitting** and giving water will be recounted, and the hardness of the heart will be described as a failure to split. The word that carries the name of the sūra attaches to the same idea.

**And there is a fine point here. I record it as my own reading:** of an animal whose name is "the splitter of soil", verse 71 asks specifically for the one that has **never split soil** — *lā dhalūlun tuthīru'l-arḍa wa lā tasqi'l-ḥarth*. An animal is sought that has never done the work its name describes.

**ذَبْح** — root **ذ-ب-ح**: to slaughter. The lexicographers record that the root's original picture is **splitting**, and that slaughtering is the splitting of the throat. The verb of the command comes from the same field of meaning.

**أَتَتَّخِذُنَا هُزُوًا** — "are you mocking us?" The first reaction is not even a question; it is a **refusal to take the command seriously**. *Huzuʾ* — to make a thing an object of amusement. The people object not because they do not understand the command but because they do not count the command a command.

Moses's answer is not a defence either but a taking of refuge: *aʿūdhu bi'llāhi an akūna mina'l-jāhilīn* — "I take refuge with God from being among the ignorant." That is, "mockery is ignorance"; and *jahl* here means not lack of knowledge but **unseriousness, levity** — this is one of the widespread uses of the root in Arabic.

### The questions and the narrowing command

The beginning of the command is extremely wide: **a cow.** Any one. But the command is not carried out; instead the questions begin, and each question narrows the description:

| Question | The answer given | What is left |
|---|---|---|
| — (the first command, 67) | *baqaratan* — "a cow" | Every cow |
| "Make clear what it is" (68) | *lā fāriḍun wa lā bikrun, ʿawānun bayna dhālik* | The range of age narrowed |
| "What colour is it?" (69) | *ṣafrāʾu fāqiʿun lawnuhā* | The colour fixed as well |
| "Make clear what she is like" (70) | *lā dhalūlun… musallamatun lā shiyata fīhā* | Very nearly a single animal |

And the story ends thus: *"And they slaughtered her; and they very nearly did not do it."*

**This is the main idea of the story.** What made the obligation heavy was not the one who gave the command but the one who received it. The command at the start was easy; the only cause of its becoming difficult is the questions.

There is one further detail: at the end of verse 68, immediately after the first answer, comes a warning — **فَٱفْعَلُوا۟ مَا تُؤْمَرُونَ** — *"now do what you are commanded."* The narrowing, that is, could have been stopped at the second question; the door was left open. The questions go on all the same.

### لَا فَارِضٌ وَلَا بِكْرٌ — neither old nor young

**فَارِض** — root **ف-ر-ض**: **to notch, to cut a groove, to fix by cutting.** From the same root come *farḍ* and *farīḍa*: an obligation definitely determined, with its boundary drawn. Used of an animal it means **advanced in age, past bearing**.

**بِكْر** — root **ب-ك-ر**: **to be early, to be first.** From the same root come *bukra* (the early part of the morning) and *ibkār*. Of an animal: one that has not yet borne, a young one.

**عَوَان** — between the two, middle-aged.

Something here is worth recording: the first answer gives a **range** — neither this nor that, but between the two. The answer, that is, is still wide; it points to no single animal. The narrowing hardens not at this step but at the ones that follow.

And the words *fāriḍ* and *bikr* both have to do with time: one the end of a span, the other its beginning. The animal sought is neither at the beginning nor at the end of its life. The two senses the word **wasaṭ** carries at verse 143 of the sūra — being in the middle and being choice — appear here in advance, in the description of an animal.

### صَفْرَآءُ فَاقِعٌ لَّوْنُهَا — bright yellow

**صَفْرَآء** — root **ص-ف-ر**: yellow. This root was treated in the discussion of **al-Ḥadīd 57/20**; it was recorded there that in the word *muṣfarr* the *ifʿilāl* pattern conveys that the colour is thoroughly settled. Here the pattern is different — *ṣafrāʾ* is an adjective of the *ṣifa mushabbaha* type and states the colour itself; the intensification comes not from the pattern but from the word placed beside it.

**فَاقِع** — root **ف-ق-ع**. In Arabic each colour has an intensifier of its own, and these are not interchangeable: *aḥmar qāniʾ* (bright red), *aswad ḥālik* (pitch black), *abyaḍ nāṣiʿ* (pure white) — and for yellow, *aṣfar fāqiʿ*. The expression is thus not "yellowish" but **the most intense degree of the colour**.

**تَسُرُّ ٱلنَّٰظِرِينَ** — "giving joy to those who look at her." In a description given for an animal to be slaughtered, a clause with no bearing whatever on the function: **that it should please the one who sees it.** The detail is itself the product of the questions — had they not been asked, this condition would not exist either.

### مُسَلَّمَةٌ لَّا شِيَةَ فِيهَا — sound, without a spot

**لَا ذَلُولٌ تُثِيرُ ٱلْأَرْضَ** — the word *dhalūl* and the root **ذ-ل-ل** were treated in detail in the discussion of **al-Mulk 67/15**, and this verse was already cited there for comparison; I do not repeat it. Its summary: *dhalūl* is an animal brought under the yoke, broken in to work. What is sought here is the exact opposite.

**تُثِيرُ** — root **ث-و-ر**: to raise, to turn the underside up, to stir dust into the air. To plough the soil is to bring the underside of the soil to the top.

**مُسَلَّمَة** — root **س-ل-م**. At verse 131 of the sūra we saw the sense of this root, "to be sound, flawless, without defect", in the verbs *aslim / aslamtu*. *Musallama* — one having no fault, free of blemish.

**لَا شِيَةَ فِيهَا** — "there is no spot on her." Root **و-ش-ي**: to add a mark of another colour to something, to make it dappled. The lexicographers connect it with *wishāya* (informing against someone) from the same root in this way: the informer too is one who adds his own colour to what is said. **I transmit this connection as the lexicographers make it**, not as a settled etymological ruling.

The condition is that the colour be **single**: it is to be yellow, but with not one blot of another colour in it. At the last step the description comes down from colour to texture.

**قَالُوا۟ ٱلْـَٰٔنَ جِئْتَ بِٱلْحَقِّ** — *"now you have brought the truth."* The sentence gives itself away without noticing: it means that the earlier answers were not counted true. Yet they had all come from the same source. What changed is not the source of the answer but **that there was no question left to ask**.

### فَذَبَحُوهَا وَمَا كَادُوا۟ يَفْعَلُونَ

*"And they slaughtered her; and they very nearly did not do it."*

The closing sentence of the story ends the event not with a success but with **an outcome by a hair's breadth**.

The verb **كَادَ** in Arabic conveys "to come close, to all but happen". Together with its negative — *mā kādū yafʿalūn* — it means "they very nearly did not do it": the thing was done, but it came close enough to touch the boundary of not being done.

The placement of the sentence matters too. The verse could have ended by saying "in the end they carried out the command"; the command was, after all, carried out. But the text does not leave the **quality** of the obedience unrecorded. The deed is counted, but how it was done is written down as well.

**I record this as my own reading:** the story draws no sharp line between obedience and disobedience. It shows that there is a wide field in between — a field in which the command is not refused but its carrying out is delayed to the last moment. The subject of the story is that in-between.

### Is asking questions always good?

One has to be careful here, because this story is very open to being wrongly generalised — as though asking questions were bad.

The same sūra had recorded the opposite as well: **in verse 30 the angels asked a question**, and that question was neither condemned nor punished; it was answered. The question itself, that is, is not the issue.

The difference lies in **what the question does**:

| | The angels' question (2/30) | The cow questions (2/68-71) |
|---|---|---|
| When it was asked | **Before** the command, in order to understand | **After** the command, instead of doing |
| Its effect | It brought knowledge | It made the obligation heavier |
| What followed | They accepted | "They very nearly did not do it" |

What is condemned, then, is not curiosity but **postponement**. The question here is not an instrument of knowledge but an instrument of delay — that a new question is produced as each answer arrives shows this too. The Qurʾān warns against this attitude directly elsewhere: *"Do not ask about things which, if made clear to you, would trouble you"* (al-Māʾida 5/101).

And the sūra will return to this matter in a later verse: *"Or do you wish to question your messenger as Moses was questioned before?"* (2/108). The story, that is, is not left as a tale belonging to the past; the measure is applied to the new addressee too.

### The murder and the shifting of the offence

The second half of the story explains why the cow was required: there is **an unsolved murder**.

**فَٱدَّٰرَ ْٔتُمْ فِيهَا** — root *d-r-ʾ*: **to ward off, to push, to throw off oneself.** The pattern conveys reciprocity: **you threw it on to one another.** In a single sentence the social scene of an unsolved murder is drawn — everyone pushes the offence on to someone else.

**وَٱللَّهُ مُخْرِجٌ مَّا كُنتُمْ تَكْتُمُونَ** — "God is the bringer-out of what you were hiding." The sentence is built as a **nominal sentence** and its agent is given with an active participle (*mukhrij*); in Arabic this structure states the matter not as a one-off act but as **a fixed attribute**. Not "He will bring out", but "He is the one who brings out."

And the verb *katm* (concealing) is not here by accident. This stretch of the sūra is about concealment from beginning to end: at verse 42 the concealing of the truth, at verse 140 the concealing of testimony, at verse 146 concealing while knowing, at verse 159 the making good of concealment. The murder set inside the story of the cow is the **most concrete** instance of that pattern: what is hidden here is not a piece of knowledge but a corpse.

Now the two halves of the story join: on one side there is a command that will bring the truth to light, on the other the questions produced in order not to carry out that command. Asking after details is not an innocent scrupulousness; it can also be read as **an effort to delay the arrival of the result**. For someone who wants to cover the killer, the best strategy is to drown the investigation in a debate about procedure.

### ٱضْرِبُوهُ بِبَعْضِهَا — "strike him with a part of her"

*"We said, strike him with a part of her. Thus does God bring the dead to life."*

The verse does not recount **how** the event happened. Which part, whom it was struck on, what the murdered man said — none of it is there. Detailed reports filling these gaps are transmitted in the classical commentaries; **I do not transmit them as certain knowledge,** because the text has chosen not to give them.

What the text does give is **what** the event is recounted for, and this is written plainly:

- **كَذَٰلِكَ يُحْىِ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمَوْتَىٰ** — "thus does God bring the dead to life."
- **وَيُرِيكُمْ ءَايَٰتِهِۦ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَعْقِلُونَ** — "and He shows you His signs; that perhaps you may use your reason."

The story, then, does not end with the solving of a murder investigation; it closes as a **proof of resurrection**. The making of a dead man to speak is presented as a small-scale demonstration of the raising of the dead.

This is a structure repeated in the later parts of the sūra: at verse 243 those who went out in fear of death are brought to life, at verse 259 the man who waited a hundred years, at verse 260 Abraham's four birds. The sūra treats the subject of resurrection not by argument but **by scenes set one after another**. The story of the cow is the first of that series.

**لَعَلَّكُمْ تَعْقِلُونَ** — "that perhaps you may use your reason." The last word of the story is from the root *ʿaql*, and at verse 44 we saw that this root means "to bind". The closing asks for exactly that binding: the bond between a slaughtered animal and the raising of the dead.

### Why is the sūra named after this story?

The longest sūra of the Qurʾān is named not after its longest narrative (the history of the Children of Israel) nor after its most famous verse (the Verse of the Throne), but after this short story.

No definite ground is transmitted. But there is a side of it that fits the sūra as a whole: from beginning to end this sūra is about **the distance between a command and a person.** In the section on the hypocrites there were those who appeared to comply with the command; at verse 59 those who changed the word; at verse 65 those who went round the prohibition; here those who wear the command down with questions. The story of the cow is the plainest and most visible instance of that pattern.

---

## 2/74 — Harder than stone

*"Then after that your hearts hardened; like stone, or harder still. For there are stones from which rivers gush; there are stones that split and water comes out of them; and there are stones that fall down in fear of God."*

### قَسْوَة — hardness

Root *q-s-w*: to harden, to grow hard, to lose elasticity.

This word is the exact opposite of the **khushūʿ** of verse 45 of the sūra. There we saw that the root of *khushūʿ* is "to soften" and that the Qurʾān uses it of dry ground as well (*"you see the earth barren; when We send down water it stirs"*, Fuṣṣilat 41/39). Now its contrary comes: what does not soften, does not take in water, has formed a crust.

Together the two verses set a measure: the question is not whether the knowledge arrives but whether the place it arrives at takes it in. The same water does two different things in two soils.

### The comparison turning to the stone's advantage

The real force of the verse lies in its setting up a comparison and then **breaking it in favour of the thing compared to.** Had it said "like stone" and stopped, that would already be a heavy verdict. But it goes on: *"or harder still."*

And the ground is enumerated — the three states of stone:

1. **Stone from which rivers gush** — giving abundantly and continuously
2. **Stone that splits and lets water out** — giving little, but giving
3. **Stone that falls down in fear of God** — giving nothing, but moving

All three show a response. Even the most barren stone at least **falls** — that is, it does not stay fixed. This is how the heart's being harder than stone is to be understood: even stone does something.

**يَتَفَجَّرُ** — root *f-j-r*: to split open and gush. This root has occurred at several earlier points in this commentary, and in all of them the same idea was present: the splitting of a husk and the coming out of what is inside. The words the Qurʾān chooses for fruitfulness come insistently from the root of **splitting** — *falaq* (the splitting of the dawn), *falāḥ* (the farmer who splits the soil), *fajr*, *shaqq*, *faṭr*. The hardening of the heart is exactly the reverse: what does not split, does not open, and lets nothing out.

At the fifth verse of the sūra those who attain were called **mufliḥūn** — "those who split through and come out". Seventy verses later the opposite tableau is complete: the heart that does not split.

---

# 2/75-86 — What was done to the text

This section carries the subject of the previous section one step further. There, commands were being gone round; here it is recounted what was done **to the text itself**.

---

## 2/75 — تَحْرِيف

*"Do you hope that they will believe you? A group of them used to hear the word of God and then, after they had understood it well, distort it knowingly."*

The root **حرف**: **an edge, a tip, a border.** Not the middle of a thing but its margin. From the same root come *ḥarf* (the smallest unit of writing) and *inḥirāf* (swerving from the road). Elsewhere the Qurʾān uses this word very concretely: *"Among the people is one who serves God **upon an edge** (ʿalā ḥarfin)"* (al-Ḥajj 22/11) — that is, standing not at the centre of it but at its margin.

On this, **taḥrīf** is, in the word's own logic: **to bend a thing from its edge.** Not to tear the text down the middle; to draw it slightly aside from its margin, to shift its direction. A small crookedness produces a great deviation further off.

In the classical commentaries this is divided into two kinds, and the division matters:

| | What is done | How it is detected |
|---|---|---|
| **Distortion of the wording** | The text itself is altered | It can be seen by comparing copies |
| **Distortion of the meaning** | The text stays, its interpretation is shifted | Far harder to see |

The second is the more widespread and the more dangerous, because it leaves no trace at all. The text stands there; what has changed is the ruling drawn from it.

**مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا عَقَلُوهُ** — "**after they had understood it well**". The verse records this deliberately. The verb is from the root *ʿ-q-l* — as we saw at verse 44, "to bind". They understood, they grasped, they made the connection; and then they bent it.

This is a place where the definition set up in the sixth verse of the sūra works once more: **the issue is not ignorance but what is done with what is known.**

---

## 2/76-77 — Behind a closed door

*"When they meet those who believe they say 'we believe'. When they are alone with one another they say, 'Do you tell them what God has opened to you, so that they may argue against you with it before your Lord? Will you not use your reason?' Do they not know that God knows what they hide and what they make public?"*

### وَإِذَا خَلَا بَعْضُهُمْ إِلَىٰ بَعْضٍ

**خَلَا** — root **خ-ل-و**: **to be emptied, to withdraw into privacy, to be left alone together.** From the same root come *khalwa* (being alone) and *khālī* (empty). The picture in the verb is very concrete: the place **empties**, that is, third parties withdraw and only they themselves are left.

This pattern had occurred earlier in the sūra, with almost the same sentence structure:

| | On meeting | When alone together |
|---|---|---|
| **2/14** (the hypocrites) | *"we believe"* | *"we are with you, we are mocking them"* |
| **2/76** (this verse) | *"we believe"* | *"why are you telling them?"* |

The two verses set up the same structure: **two settings, two sentences.** The difference is in the content of what is said in the second setting. The hypocrites there declared their allegiance; here a piece of **information management** is being discussed.

**And this resemblance is itself worth recording.** The sūra thereby refrains from making hypocrisy peculiar to one group: the same pattern of behaviour is described for the community at the head of this section too. The measure looks not at who anyone is but at what is said when the door closes.

### أَتُحَدِّثُونَهُم بِمَا فَتَحَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْكُمْ

**فَتَحَ** — root **ف-ت-ح**: **to open.** The word's use here describes the knowledge given as an **opening**: what was closed has been opened and shown to them. The same root is used in the Qurʾān both for "to judge" (*fattāḥ*) and for "victory"; the shared idea is the opening of something closed.

The logic of the objection is this: the knowledge was opened to us, it belongs to us; to pass it to the other side is to give the other side a **card** they can play against us.

**لِيُحَآجُّوكُم بِهِۦ عِندَ رَبِّكُمْ** — "so that they may use it as an argument against you before your Lord?" The root *ḥ-j-j* — to bring a proof, to prevail in an argument; *ḥujja* is from the same root. What is feared, then, is not the spreading of the knowledge but the knowledge's **becoming an argument against themselves**.

**The stance here must be read carefully.** What is said is not "this knowledge is false". On the contrary, it is accepted as true — indeed it can only serve as an argument against them if it is true. The objection is not to its truth but to its **being shared**.

This stands in the same place as "clothing the truth in falsehood and concealing it knowingly" at verse 42 of the sūra; but it is a step beyond. There concealment was an act; here the **ground** of concealment is discussed and proposed to a group as good sense: *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn* — "will you not use your reason?"

**I record this as my own reading:** this is the heaviest detail in the sentence. Hiding knowledge is presented here not as a weakness or a cowardice but as **intelligence**. At verse 44 of the sūra the word for reason had occurred in such a place too — there those who forget themselves were asked "will you not use your reason?" The same question comes here, turned inside out, from the mouths of those who defend concealment.

### مَا يُسِرُّونَ وَمَا يُعْلِنُونَ

The answer of verse 77 is short, and it voids the whole calculation.

**سِرّ** — root **س-ر-ر**: to hide, to hold within. **إِعْلَان** — root **ع-ل-ن**: to bring out into the open, to announce.

The two words form a **pair** and often come side by side in the Qurʾān. What they do is this: they leave no third field between them. Every word spoken is either hidden or open; both belong to the same knowledge.

This is the continuation of the same principle as *wa'llāhu mukhrijun mā kuntum taktumūn* at verse 72. There what was hidden was a murder, here what is hidden is a piece of knowledge. In both the verse says not that concealment is **technically impossible** but that it **produces no result**: what is hidden can be hidden, but it cannot stay outside the reckoning.

And its link with the subject of the section is plain. At verse 75 the bending of the text is recounted, at verse 78 the not knowing of the text, at verse 79 the fabricating of the text. These two verses give the link in between: **the hiding of the text.**

---

## 2/78 — أُمِّيُّون and أَمَانِىّ

*"Among them are unlettered ones who do not know the Book; all they know are wishful fancies. They only conjecture."*

**أُمِّىّ** — root *ʾ-m-m* (mother). The classical explanation of the word: one who has remained in the state in which he was born of his mother, that is, who has not learned to read and write. Here it means specifically **one who does not know the Book**.

**أَمَانِىّ** — this word has two senses, and both fit the context:

- **Fancy, wish.** Root *m-n-y*: to wish, to hope. That is, a conviction resting not on reality but on wish.
- **To read through.** One use of the same root also covers voicing a text without understanding it.

Read together, the two senses give a familiar picture: the text is **recited** but not **known**; and what fills the gap is what the person wishes were so. Religion is derived not from the text but from wishing.

**وَإِنْ هُمْ إِلَّا يَظُنُّونَ** — "they only conjecture". This word occurred at verse 46, and there it meant **certain knowledge**. Here it is the exact reverse: guesswork without ground. That the same word is used in two verses in two opposite senses shows the word's contrary character — and it is always the context that determines which sense is meant.

---

## 2/79 — Writing with one's own hand and ascribing it to God

*"Woe to those who write the Book with their own hands and then say 'This is from God', in order to sell it for a small price!"*

The plainest form of distortion. And the verse enumerates three stages separately: **writing** (production), **ascribing** (fabricating a source), **selling** (advantage).

**بِأَيْدِيهِمْ** — "with their hands". A detail that looks superfluous; everyone knows that writing is done by hand. It is placed there for emphasis: the source is known, the agent is known. The sentence sets a human hand directly opposite the claim "from God".

The first addressees of this verse were a particular community; but the act it describes is peculiar to no community. Anyone who presents a religious ruling, his own opinion, his own preference as "God's ruling" falls under this description — those who fabricate hadith, those who bend a verse to their own advantage, those who make their personal judgement speak as though it were revelation. In the classical tradition this verse is among the warnings most often recalled in the matter of fabricated hadith.

---

## 2/80-82 — "A counted number of days"

*"They said, 'The fire will not touch us except for a counted number of days.' Say: Have you taken a covenant from God?… No! Whoever earns an evil and is encompassed by his sin — those are the companions of the fire."*

The claim is this: even if there is punishment it is **limited**, it has an end, it can be borne. This is the last stronghold of the "guarantee of chosenness" dismantled in verses 47-48 of the sūra — if there is no guarantee of salvation left, at least the shortness of the punishment is guaranteed.

The answer comes in two steps. First **a proof is demanded**: *"Have you taken a covenant from God?"* That is, what is the ground of this claim? Then the measure is set down.

**وَأَحَٰطَتْ بِهِۦ خَطِيٓـَٔتُهُۥ** — "if his sin has **encompassed** him". Root *ḥ-w-ṭ*: to surround, to enclose, to shut in. From the same root comes *ḥāʾiṭ* (a wall).

This word is chosen very carefully. The measure is not **the number of sins**; it is whether the sin has **closed round the person.** A person may err and his error may remain one part of him; or the error may grow and wall him in on every side, so that he now lives inside it. The verse describes the second.

The difference is this: in the first case the person still has a place from which he can look at his error — he can repent, he can turn back. In the second, the outside has become invisible. It comes out at the same place as the "seal" of the seventh verse of the sūra: the closing happens not in a moment but with the completion of the encirclement.

---

## 2/83 — A covenant in seven items

*"We took this covenant: serve God alone; be good to parents, to kin, to orphans and to the poor; speak good words to people; keep up the prayer, and give the alms."*

This verse is the summary of the covenant given to the earlier prophets, and what is striking is its **order**:

1. Service to God alone
2. Goodness to parents
3. To kin
4. To orphans
5. To the poor
6. A good word to people
7. Prayer and alms

After tawḥīd comes directly **the parents** — not worship. Then the circles widen: kin, orphan, poor, and finally **all people.** The acts of worship come last of all.

This order is a pattern repeated elsewhere in the Qurʾān as well (al-Isrāʾ 17/23 and what follows it is the closest to it). What it says is plain: the first concrete return of service to God is not ritual but **how one treats the nearest to hand**.

**وَقُولُوا۟ لِلنَّاسِ حُسْنًا** — "speak good words to people". The word here is not "to the believers" but **"al-nās"** — all people. There is no narrowing clause in the verse. This stands in the same line as verse 62 at the head of the section: the measure does not change according to who the other person is.

---

## 2/84-85 — Believing in part of the Book

*"We took a covenant from you that you would not shed one another's blood nor drive one another from your homes… Then you kill one another and drive a part of you from their homes… And when they come to you as captives you ransom them — though it was already forbidden you to drive them out."*

The verse stages an extremely concrete inconsistency. The same community, at the same time:

- **Drives** someone from his home — this was forbidden.
- Then, when that person falls captive, **ransoms and frees him** — this was obligatory.

One of two rulings is kept, the other broken. And the ruling kept is the one whose keeping is **costless** or brings prestige; the one broken is the one that runs against interest.

The answer is in the nature of a summary of the whole section:

> **أَفَتُؤْمِنُونَ بِبَعْضِ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ وَتَكْفُرُونَ بِبَعْضٍ**
> *"Do you believe in part of the Book and reject part of it?"*

This sentence names all the patterns treated so far in this commentary. Changing the word (2/59), going round the prohibition (2/65), wearing the command down with questions (2/67-71), bending the text from its edge (2/75) — all did the same work: **taking the part of the text that suits.**

And note: this attitude is not leaving religion. On the contrary, it requires staying inside religion — because only someone who stays inside can believe in "part of it". The person the verse describes is a person who counts himself religious. And the measure of the selection is not the text but the person's own interest; the text is used only as an endorsement.

This is a permanently open door for everyone who possesses religious knowledge. The only practical sign of believing in the whole of the Book is **keeping the ruling that goes against you too** — because keeping the ruling that favours you requires no faith.

---

## 2/86 — Trade again

*"Those are the ones who have bought the life of this world at the price of the hereafter."*

The metaphor of trade used of the hypocrites at verse 16 of the sūra comes back here — there "straying" was bought at the price of "guidance", here "this world" at the price of "the hereafter".

What the two transactions have in common is that both were made **with something in hand**. What is lost is not something never possessed; it is something held and given away.

And the name of what is taken attaches to the same root as the word we saw at verse 61: **dunyā**, that is, *adnā* — the near, and at the same time the low. The evil of the transaction lies not in the intention but in **the price**: what is near has been exchanged for what lasts.

---

# 2/87-96 — The measure of rejection

## 2/87 — The chain and hawā

*"We gave Moses the Book and sent messengers after him… Whenever a messenger brought you something your souls did not like, you were arrogant; some you called liars, and some you killed."*

**وَقَفَّيْنَا** — root *q-f-w*: **the nape, the back.** The verb means "to come at someone's nape", that is, to send immediately after. The line of prophets is described as a caravan walking one at the other's nape. The idea of the chain in the fourth verse of the sūra takes concrete form here.

### The criterion of rejection

The crucial place in the verse is this: **no lack of proof is given as the cause of rejection.** What is given is this:

> **بِمَا لَا تَهْوَىٰٓ أَنفُسُكُمُ**
> *"whenever he brought something your souls did not like"*

The measure is not whether the claim is true; it is **whether it pleases**.

**هَوَىٰ** — root *h-w-y*: **to fall, to go downward.** From the same root comes *hāwiya* (an abyss, the place one falls into); it occurs in Sūrat al-Qāriʿa as a name of the fire. *Hawā* is also akin to "air" — an emptiness with nothing to hold on to.

Arabic has given desire this name, then: **what pulls a person downward.** The word states a direction. Indeed the Qurʾān uses this word in the context of making something a god as well: *"Have you seen the one who takes his hawā as his god?"* (al-Furqān 25/43) — that is, a person's making what he wants into the measure.

This verse gives the diagnosis of the whole section. Believing in part of the Book and rejecting part of it (2/85), bending the word (2/75), going round the prohibition (2/65) — the mechanism common to them all is this: **the ruling is first put to hawā, and only then is one turned back to the text.**

**رُوحِ ٱلْقُدُسِ** — the supporting of Jesus (ʿĪsā) with this spirit. The majority of the classical commentaries explain it as Gabriel (Jibrīl); the root of "quds" (*q-d-s*) conveys purity and cleansing.

---

## 2/88 — "Our hearts are wrapped"

*"They said, 'Our hearts are in a wrapping.' No! God has cursed them for their unbelief."*

**غُلْف** — root *gh-l-f*: **a wrapping, a sheath.** From the same root comes *ghilāf* (a case, a covering). Like the scabbard on a sword: the contents are protected but cannot be used.

This is not a confession but an **excuse.** What is said is: "your word does not work on us, because our heart is closed." Responsibility is taken from the person and loaded onto his constitution. Its present-day counterpart is more than familiar: "this is how I am", "it is not for me", "my nature is not suited to this".

The answer comes in two words: **"bal"** — no, it is not so. And the cause is reversed: they do not reject because the heart is closed; **the heart has closed because they reject.** The order we set up for the seal at the seventh verse of the sūra is confirmed once more here: the closing is the consequence of choice, not its cause.

---

## 2/89 — Though they recognised it

*"Formerly they used to pray for victory by it against those who covered over; but when what they recognised came to them, they rejected it."*

**يَسْتَفْتِحُونَ** — root *f-t-ḥ*: to open. This pattern means "to seek an opening", that is, to ask for victory and the upper hand. According to what is transmitted, they claimed that they would prevail with the coming of the awaited prophet.

And then:

> **فَلَمَّا جَآءَهُم مَّا عَرَفُوا۟ كَفَرُوا۟ بِهِۦ**
> *"when what they recognised came to them, they rejected it"*

The choice of word is exact: **"what they recognised"**. There is knowing, there is recognising, there is no acceptance. The definition of unbelief in the sixth verse of the sūra — *covering over what is known* — is seen here at its plainest. The Qurʾān sharpens this further a few verses later: *"They recognise it as they recognise their own sons"* (al-Baqara 2/146).

The fulfilment of the expectation did not satisfy the expectation. Because what was expected was not really a prophet but **preferment**; when the prophet came and the preferment passed to another, the expectation lost its meaning.

---

## 2/90 — Selling themselves

*"How evil is that for which they sold themselves! They rejected what God has sent down, begrudging that God should bestow of His bounty on whichever of His servants He wills."*

**بِئْسَمَا ٱشْتَرَوْا۟ بِهِۦٓ أَنفُسَهُمْ** — the metaphor of trade in the sūra reaches its furthest point here. At verse 16 guidance was sold, at verse 86 the hereafter; here what is sold is **the person himself.**

**بَغْيًا** — root *b-gh-y*: to pass the limit, transgression; and **envy.** The verse names the cause of the refusal explicitly: *"begrudging that God should bestow of His bounty on whichever of His servants He wills."*

The objection, then, is not to the content of the revelation but to **its recipient.** The matter is not "this is not true" but "why was this given to him". It is the same structure as the ground of Iblīs (2/34): there too the matter was not the command itself but whom it favoured. Like pride, envy too is born of a **comparison.**

---

## 2/91 — "We believe in what was sent down to us"

*"When they are told 'believe in what God has sent down', they say, 'we believe in what was sent down to us', and they reject what is beyond it — though it is the truth confirming what is with them."*

The structure of the sentence is striking: faith is professed, but the object of the faith is defined not as a **truth** but as a **possession** — "what was sent down to us". The measure is not whether the word is true but whose it is.

And the verse shows that this stance contradicts itself: what they reject is the thing that **confirms** what they accept. Someone who rejects what corroborates his own book is attached not to his book but to his ownership of it.

The answer targets this contradiction too: *"Then why did you kill God's prophets before — if you were believers?"* It is recalled that the inheritance claimed in word was not kept in deed.

---

## 2/92 — The claim tested against its own history

*"Moses brought you clear proofs; then after him you took the calf — and you were wrongdoers."*

In the previous verse it had been said: *"we believe in what was sent down to us."* Allegiance, that is, had been reserved to their own prophets and their own books.

This verse does not reject that claim; it **tests it with its own material.**

### How the test is built

The sentence consists of three parts, and their order sets up a time-line:

| Part | What is said |
|---|---|
| *wa la-qad jāʾakum Mūsā bi'l-bayyināt* | The proof came — and it was **clear** |
| *thumma'ttakhadhtumu'l-ʿijla min baʿdih* | The calf was taken — and **right afterwards** |
| *wa antum ẓālimūn* | And this was done knowingly |

The side that says "we believe in what was sent down to us" turned to something else while its own prophet was still among them, immediately after the clearest of proofs. **The claim is refuted by the record of the one making it.**

This is a method repeated in the sūra, and it will be seen once more at verse 94: there the claim about the hereafter will be tested by the attitude towards death. In neither is the counter-proof brought from outside; it is taken **from the life of the one addressed**.

### ثُمَّ and مِنۢ بَعْدِهِۦ

The conjunction **ثُمَّ** in Arabic conveys *tarākhī*: "then", with an interval entering in between. Its use here shows that some time passed between the proof and the calf.

But the **مِنۢ بَعْدِهِۦ** that comes immediately after narrows that interval: "after him" — that is, right behind Moses's departure. At verse 51 of the sūra it was said how long this span was: **forty nights.**

Set the two notes side by side and what emerges is this: not enough time has passed for forgetting. The absence is short, the proof fresh, the turning quick.

### ٱلْبَيِّنَٰت

Root **ب-ي-ن**: to separate two things, to distinguish. A *bayyina* is the proof by which truth and falsehood are **separated**; *bayān* and *tabyīn* are from the same root.

That the word comes in the plural matters: not a single proof but **proofs.** The sūra had enumerated them one by one in verses 49-60 — the parting of the sea, the shade, manna and quails, the twelve springs, water from the rock. The verse recalls this whole list with one word.

### وَأَنتُمْ ظَٰلِمُونَ

This clause at the end of the sentence is another form of the sūra's most frequently repeated clause: *wa antum taʿlamūn* — "and that while you know" (2/22, 2/42, 2/75, 2/188).

Here the word **wrongdoing** is put in the place of knowledge. In this sūra the two are bound to one another: what is done knowingly is no longer an error but an injustice. And the sense of the root *ẓ-l-m*, "to put a thing somewhere other than its place", occurred at the fifty-fourth verse — the setting of the calf in God's place is the word's exact counterpart.

And a nominal sentence is used: *wa antum ẓālimūn* — not "you did wrong" but "you were wrongdoers". In Arabic this structure states the act not as a one-off event but as **the character of the condition at that moment**.

---

## 2/93 — The calf made to be drunk into the heart

*"…and the calf was made to be drunk into their hearts because of their unbelief."*

One of the most striking expressions in the Qurʾān.

**وَأُشْرِبُوا۟** — root *sh-r-b*: **to drink.** The verb is passive: "they were made to drink". Love of the calf was given to the heart **as a drink**.

The force of the comparison lies in what drinking is: what is eaten goes to the stomach, what is drunk mixes with the blood. What is drunk cannot be taken out — it spreads through the body and becomes part of the tissue. With this verb the verse tells how deeply an attachment can work into a person's constitution.

And there is one further detail: it says **the calf itself** was made to be drunk in, not "love of the calf". Such abbreviations are a known style in Arabic; but here the effect looks deliberate — the distance between the object and the attachment felt for it has been removed.

The cause is again stated: **"because of their unbelief".** The being made to drink, that is, is no arbitrary intervention; it is the consequence of choice. The seal (2/7), the wrapping (2/88) and this verse tell the same principle in three different pictures.

---

## 2/94-96 — The test of the claim

*"Say: If the abode of the hereafter with God is yours alone and not for other people, then wish for death — if you are truthful. But they will never wish for it, because of what their hands have done."*

Here the sūra proposes a **test**, and the logic of the test is flawless.

The claim was: the hereafter belongs to us, the fire will touch us for a counted number of days (2/80). If that is true, then death is not a loss but a gain — waiting is wearisome for one whose waiting is for something good. They should therefore wish for death.

And the answer is given: **"they will never wish for it."**

The method here resembles a method we meet at several points in this commentary: **testing by conduct a claim advanced in words.** Sūrat al-Māʿūn gave treatment of the orphan as the proof of denying the religion; here the attitude towards death is given as the proof of the claim about the hereafter. In both, the measure is looked for not in the declaration but in the deed.

**وَلَتَجِدَنَّهُمْ أَحْرَصَ ٱلنَّاسِ عَلَىٰ حَيَوٰةٍ** — "you will find them the greediest of people for life". And a striking detail follows: *"each of them would like to live a thousand years."*

The figure **a thousand years** matters. What is wanted is not immortality — that is already known to be impossible. What is wanted is **the longest of what is possible.** The demand, that is, is made by a realistic calculation: the greatest span that can be held on to.

This measures exactly the distance between claim and condition. A person may say he believes in the hereafter and may even be sincere in it; but the force of his clinging to life shows where his belief is lodged. **The verse does not call anyone's word a lie; it sets the word beside the conduct.**

And this measure joins with verse 62 at the head of the section: belonging does not save, and neither does a claim. The only thing left is whether faith and deed hold together.

---

# 2/97-112 — Proof, abrogation and the criterion

## 2/97-98 — Enmity towards an angel

*"Say: Whoever is an enemy to Gabriel — it is he who brought it down upon your heart, confirming what came before it… Whoever is an enemy to God, His angels, His messengers, Gabriel and Michael, let him know that God is an enemy to those who cover over."*

The objection has slipped to a strange place here: not to the content of the revelation but to **its carrier.** According to what is transmitted, the identity of the angel who brought the revelation had been put forward as a ground for objection.

The answer comes in two steps. First it is recalled **what the work is**: the angel does not speak on his own account, he carries — and what he carries is what "confirms what came before it". Then the real address of the enmity is set down: to be an enemy to a messenger because of his errand is to be an enemy to the one who sent him.

The logic here is the same as the general pattern of the section: the stated reason for the objection and its real reason are different. At verse 90 the cause of the refusal was named as **envy**; here too the ground looks technical, but its function is the same — to find some support for not accepting.

---

## 2/99-100 — Clear signs and the covenant thrown aside

*"We have sent down to you clear signs; none reject them but the transgressors. Is it not so that whenever they made a covenant, a group of them threw it aside? Indeed most of them do not believe."*

These two verses look as though they were placed in between, closing the previous section (enmity towards an angel) and passing to the next (sorcery and Solomon). In fact they are the link joining the two: they record the **groundlessness** of the objection and the fact that it is **not new**.

### وَمَا يَكْفُرُ بِهَآ إِلَّا ٱلْفَٰسِقُونَ

The concrete sense of the root **ف-س-ق** has been treated at several points in this commentary — at verse 26 of the sūra and in the discussions of al-Ḥadīd and al-Ḥujurāt: **a date's coming out of its skin**, that is, overflowing its own vessel. I do not repeat it.

The word's place here is striking. The sentence does not call those who reject the signs "those who do not know" or "those who do not understand"; it calls them **those who have come out of their skin**. The cause of the rejection, that is, is not a lack of knowledge but **an overstepping of the boundary one stands in**.

This continues the finding built up from the beginning of the section: at verse 89 "though they recognised it", at verse 90 "out of envy", at verse 91 "we believe in what was sent down to us" — in all of them the rejection is given not as a problem of knowledge but as a problem of position.
