**The pattern of the sentence reinforces this too:** *mā… illā* — in Arabic this is the style of **qaṣr** (restriction). First there is a general negation, then a single exception is left. Not "those who reject them are generally transgressors"; but **"none reject them but the transgressors."**

### أَوَكُلَّمَا عَٰهَدُوا۟ عَهْدًا نَّبَذَهُۥ فَرِيقٌ مِّنْهُم

**نَبَذَ** — root **ن-ب-ذ**. This root was treated in the discussion of al-Humaza (104/4); I do not repeat it. The summary of the finding there was this: *nabdh* is to throw a thing away **because one counts it worthless** — not every throwing is *nabdh*; *nabdh* is the counting of the thing thrown as useless.

The choice of word matters for that reason. The covenant is not **broken**, not **transgressed**, not **forgotten** — it is *thrown away*. The verb carries within it the value placed on the contract.

The same root is used of this community once more in the Qurʾān, and even more concretely: *"they threw it behind their backs"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/187). There the act of throwing acquires a direction as well: backwards, to the side that is not seen.

**كُلَّمَا** — "whenever". The word takes the event out of being a single occurrence and makes it **a repeated pattern**. That the question comes in the form of a question (*a-wa kullamā*) reinforces this: no answer is expected, something known is being recalled.

And the sūra does not leave this finding hanging; the word "covenant" forms a chain in this sūra:

| Verse | What is said about the covenant |
|---|---|
| **2/27** | Those who break God's covenant after making it firm |
| **2/40** | *"Fulfil My covenant and I shall fulfil yours"* |
| **2/83-84** | A covenant in seven items had been taken |
| **2/100** | At every covenant a group has thrown it aside |
| **2/124** | *"My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers"* |

Five occurrences draw a course: the covenant is taken, its keeping is asked for, it is thrown away — and finally it is said whom the covenant will not reach.

### فَرِيقٌ مِّنْهُم and بَلْ أَكْثَرُهُمْ

Two clauses stand side by side, and both are limiting: the ones who throw it are **a group**, the ones who do not believe are **most of them**. The ruling, that is, is not passed wholesale.

This is the sūra's general scrupulousness in language, and it is seen elsewhere too: *"a group of them"* (2/75), *"most of them"* (2/109), *"they are not all alike"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/113). The text builds what it describes by way of **attributes**; it does not close it over a whole community.

That this distinction is preserved here in particular is meaningful: the rest of the sentence is extremely hard — "at every covenant", "most of them do not believe". Even inside the hardness the distinction is preserved.

---

## 2/101-103 — Sorcery and Solomon

*"…They followed what the satans recited about the kingdom of Solomon (Sulaymān). Solomon did not cover over the truth; but the satans did: they were teaching people sorcery…"*

This verse is one of the hardest passages in the sūra and has to be handled carefully.

### The first thing the verse does: clearing a prophet

At the centre of the sentence stands this: **وَمَا كَفَرَ سُلَيْمَٰنُ** — "Solomon did not cover over the truth."

There is, that is, a narrative ascribing sorcery to the name of a prophet, and the verse **rejects** it. Solomon's extraordinary kingdom had been explained by sorcery; the Qurʾān returns that explanation to those who made it.

This is another form of the subject of "what was done to the text" treated through the section: what is altered here is not a text but **a prophet's record.**

### Hārūt and Mārūt

*"…and what was sent down to the two angels at Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt. Yet the two taught no one anything without saying, 'We are only a trial, so do not cover over the truth.'"*

This expression has been the subject of long debates in the classical commentaries, and some of them are unresolved:

| Debate | Views |
|---|---|
| The reading of **مَلَكَيْن** | The widespread reading is "the two angels"; some sources also transmit the reading "the two kings" |
| The scope of "what was sent down" | Was sorcery sent down, or should the sentence be understood as "sorcery was not sent down" — the lexicographers differ |
| The angels' teaching this | As a trial, or in order to show how sorcery is to be recognised |

Most of these uncertainties the classical commentaries could not resolve either, and a significant part of the narratives circulating around the subject rests on reports coming from pre-Islamic sources. I state no definite preference here and do not transmit those narratives.

### The boundary the verse sets for itself

In the midst of the uncertainties there are things the verse says **plainly**, and these are what matter:

**First:** *"They harm no one except by God's permission."* Any independent power ascribed to sorcery is thereby refused. The authority to harm does not lie in sorcery itself.

**Second:** *"They were learning what harms them and does not profit them."* The claim of gain is brought down as well.

**Third, and most important:** *"They **certainly knew** that whoever bought it would have no share in the hereafter."* The same expression once more. The finding that turns on every page of this section holds here too: **the knowledge was there.**

Nothing will be said in this commentary about the nature, kinds or practices of sorcery; that is not the subject of the verse. The subject of the verse is what it means to leave the book in one's hand and turn to another source of power — and the sentence ends thus: *"If only they had known."*

---

## 2/104 — The abandoning of a word

*"You who believe! Do not say 'rāʿinā', say 'unẓurnā', and listen."*

A verse that looks small, but is extremely interesting.

**رَاعِنَا** — root *r-ʿ-y*: to watch over, to shepherd. In Arabic it is an entirely innocent expression: "watch over us", "attend to us", in the sense of slowing down while speaking. The Companions used this word in addressing the Prophet.

The trouble is this: the same sequence of sounds, said a little differently or in another language, could carry a demeaning sense. According to what is transmitted, some began to use the word for exactly that reason, with a slight bending — an insult for the speaker, an ordinary request for the hearer.

**The solution is striking: the word is forbidden.** No culprit is found and punished, no intention is examined; instead **a synonym** is proposed: *unẓurnā* — "look at us, attend to us". The meaning is the same, the sound different, the possibility of abuse gone.

The measure that follows is this: **how a word is understood also falls within the speaker's field of responsibility.** An expression may be correct in itself; but if it is open to being misunderstood or misused, its correctness does not make it defensible. And the solution is not to blame the other side but to change the expression.

This sets up an interesting symmetry with verse 59 of the sūra: there the word given was **changed** in bad faith; here it is **changed** in good faith. The same act, two directions.

---

## 2/105 — "They do not wish"

*"Neither those of the People of the Book who cover over the truth nor the mushrikūn wish that any good should be sent down to you from your Lord. But God singles out for His mercy whom He wills. God is the possessor of great bounty."*

### مَّا يَوَدُّ

**Root: و-د-د.** This root was treated in detail in the discussion of al-Mumtaḥina; the summary of the finding there is this: the root carries both **to love** and **to wish, to desire**, and the two are inseparable — a person loves what he wants and wants what he loves.

The word's function here is striking for that reason. The verse does not say "they hinder" or "they refuse"; it says **"they do not wish"**. What is described, that is, is not an act but an **inward inclination**.

And the object of the inclination is specially chosen too: what is refused is not a ruling or a person but *"that any **good** should be sent down to you from your Lord"*. The matter, then, is not even the content of what comes; it is **that it has come to someone else**.

This is the description of what will be named a few verses later: **حَسَد** — at verse 109 it will be said *"out of the envy within them"*. Here the feeling is described; there it will be named.

### The naming of two groups in the same sentence

**مِنْ أَهْلِ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ وَلَا ٱلْمُشْرِكِينَ** — those of the People of the Book who cover over the truth, and the mushrikūn.

Up to this point the sūra had always handled these two groups separately: the mushrikūn in the first twenty verses, the People of the Book from the fortieth verse on. Here for the first time they become **the subject of the same predicate**.

What joins them is not a belief but an **attitude**: not wishing for the good that comes. Two groups whose beliefs are diametrically opposed come into line at this point.

**And the clause in the verse's language is preserved:** *"those of the People of the Book **who cover over the truth**"* — not the whole of the People of the Book. The scrupulousness of distinction seen in verses 99-100 of the sūra continues here; indeed at Āl ʿImrān 3/113 it will separately be recorded that there are those within the same community who stand apart.

### يَخْتَصُّ بِرَحْمَتِهِۦ مَن يَشَآءُ

The root **خ-ص-ص**: to **set something apart and give it to one alone**, to single out. Its opposite is *ʿāmm* (general). *Khuṣūṣī*, *ikhtiṣāṣ*, *khāṣṣ* are from the same root.

The structure of the answer is worth recording. The objection took the form "why was it given to him?"; the answer does not say "because he deserved it", it says **"it is He who singles out"**. The debate, that is, is turned not to the quality of the recipient but to **the authority of the giver**.

This is a redirection seen elsewhere in the sūra too. At verse 124 the leadership Abraham (Ibrāhīm) asks for on behalf of his offspring will be answered with "My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers": there too the debate was carried from descent to **the measure**. Here it is carried to the point of ownership.

**And a qualification is needed:** the expression "whom He wills", read in the sense of arbitrariness, would contradict the Qurʾān's own language — for the same text describes again and again where mercy is directed (*"My mercy encompasses all things; I shall write it for those who guard themselves…"*, al-Aʿrāf 7/156). The expression states not that the choice is **without ground** but that it is **made without rendering account to anyone**. **I record this distinction as my own reading.**

**ذُو ٱلْفَضْلِ ٱلْعَظِيمِ** — *faḍl*: what is added **on top of** what is deserved. The word will occur at verse 237 as well: *"do not forget the faḍl between you"* — there it was used of the field beyond the share the law assigns. The same word is set down here as the name of the thing objected to: what is envied is not a right but a bounty.

---

## 2/106 — Abrogation

*"If We remove the ruling of a verse or cause it to be forgotten, We bring one better than it or its like. Do you not know that God has power over all things?"*

This is the foundational verse of the most disputed concept in Islamic legal method.

**نَسَخَ** — root *n-s-kh*: **to remove a thing, to erase**; and at the same time **to copy, to transfer** (*nuskha*, *istinsākh* come from here). The two senses together are interesting: both to lift and to transfer. What is lifted does not vanish; another takes its place.

### The scope of the concept

How widely the word "abrogation" is to be understood is one of the most exaggerated matters in the history of exegesis.

In the early period the word "naskh" was used very widely: one verse's **specifying** another (narrowing its scope), **qualifying** it, or the limiting of a general principle by a particular ruling were all called "naskh" as well. Because of this wide use, some old lists counted hundreds of "abrogated verses".

As later scholars narrowed the concept, these numbers dropped rapidly. The numbers reached by scholars who use the term in its technical sense — a ruling being wholly lifted and another put in its place — are very small; some have brought them down below twenty, some to the fingers of one hand.

**The stance adopted in this commentary:** a claim of abrogation can be advanced only where two verses are genuinely irreconcilable and where an order in time can be established between them. If two verses can be reconciled, reconciling them is preferred to claiming abrogation. This is also the measure I relied on in assessing the claim of abrogation at verse 62 of the sūra.

### The qualification the verse gives itself

The verse does not present the change of ruling as an arbitrary intervention; it sets down a **qualification**: *"We bring one better than it or its like."* The change, that is, is always towards what is equal or better.

The principle underlying this is one that appears elsewhere in the sūra: a ruling is adjusted to what the one addressed can bear. The gradual prohibition of wine and the gradual granting of permission to fight are always explained by this logic. A doctor's changing the dose in the course of a treatment is required by the process, not by the illness.

---

## 2/107-108 — Sovereignty and "as Moses was questioned"

*"Do you not know that to God belongs the sovereignty of the heavens and the earth? And you have, besides God, neither protector nor helper. Or do you wish to question your messenger as Moses was questioned before? Whoever exchanges faith for unbelief has strayed from the level road."*

### The attaching of verse 107 to the discussion of abrogation

This verse comes immediately after verse 106 and sets down the **ground** of the ruling there.

At 106 a change of ruling was spoken of. The lifting of a ruling and the putting of another in its place is possible only for one who is the **owner** of what is set down. Verse 107 says exactly this: **لَهُۥ مُلْكُ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ.**

The answer, that is, is given not by a theoretical argument but by a **declaration of ownership**. The same structure will be repeated in the later parts of the sūra: at verse 255 it will be said *lahū mā fi's-samāwāti wa mā fi'l-arḍ*, and at verse 284 the same sentence will close the section on property. Each time it comes after a question of authority.

**أَلَمْ تَعْلَمْ** — "do you not know?" The interrogative is used not to give information but **to recall something already known**. In this use, called *istifhām taqrīrī* in Arabic, the answer expected from the hearer is "yes".

### وَلِىّ and نَصِير

Two words come side by side, and they often occur together in the Qurʾān. They have a distinguishing side:

| Word | Root | What it conveys |
|---|---|---|
| **walī** | و-ل-ي | **Continuity** — a nearness with no gap in between, a relation of taking charge |
| **naṣīr** | ن-ص-ر | **The moment** — help that arrives at the point of pressure |

The root **و-ل-ي** was treated in detail in the discussion of al-Mumtaḥina; the summary of the finding there: at the centre of the root are **nearness and leaving no gap in between** — two things being adjacent in such a way that no third can come between them.

The negation of the two words together leaves no gap: neither a lasting back nor a passing rescue. And this is not a sentence of threat but a **description of position**; the verse takes as its subject what a person leans on.

### أَمْ تُرِيدُونَ أَن تَسْـَٔلُوا۟ رَسُولَكُمْ

Verse 108 is one of the plainest internal references in the sūra: **"as Moses was questioned."**

What was asked is not stated. But the sūra had already given two examples:

- **2/55** — *"We will not believe you until we see God plainly."*
- **2/67-71** — the chain of questions in the story of the cow.

Both are of the same kind: instead of accepting the command or the report, producing a **condition** to be met before it. In the story of the cow the result of this had been recorded — the obligation had grown heavier.

And now the measure is applied to the new addressee. The sūra does not content itself with recounting the story of past communities; each time it turns back to the reader. At verse 118 this work will be summed up in a sentence: *"their hearts have become alike."*

**A boundary has to be drawn here.** The verse does not forbid asking questions; indeed the same sūra is full of the question-and-answer pattern: *"they ask you about the new moons"* (189), *"they ask you what they should spend"* (215), *"they ask you about wine and gambling"* (219). None of these is condemned, all are answered.

The difference is the same as the difference recorded in the story of the cow: **the question that seeks knowledge and the question that postpones an obligation.** What the verse opposes is not the question itself but the work the question is made to do.

### وَمَن يَتَبَدَّلِ ٱلْكُفْرَ بِٱلْإِيمَٰنِ

**تَبَدُّل** — root **ب-د-ل**: to exchange one thing for another. Form V (*tafaʿʿul*) adds an element of **intention and effort** here: not a change that happens of itself but an exchange that is made.

The language of trade is a language repeated in this part of the sūra: at verse 16 those who buy straying with guidance, at verse 86 those who exchange the hereafter for this world, at verse 90 those who sell themselves, at verse 175 those who buy straying at the price of guidance. Each time a picture of an **exchange** is set up — and each time what is given is worth more than what is taken.

**سَوَآءَ ٱلسَّبِيلِ** — root **س-و-ي**: to be level, equal, balanced. *Sawāʾ al-sabīl* is the **level middle** of the road: the line where there is no swerving. The word pictures going astray not as "entering a wrong road" but as **slipping from the middle of the right one**.

The same root had been used for a different work at verse 6 of the sūra: *sawāʾun ʿalayhim* — "it is the same for them". The two uses show the centre of the root: an equality that leaves no difference.

---

## 2/109 — Pardon in the face of envy

*"Many of the People of the Book, out of the envy within them, would like to turn you back into unbelief after your faith, even after the truth has become clear to them. So pardon and overlook until God brings His command."*

That the two sentences stand side by side is striking. The first sentence gives an extremely hard diagnosis: **envy**, and moreover *"after the truth has become clear"* — knowingly once again.

In the second sentence what one would expect is a counter-move. What comes is: **فَٱعْفُوا۟ وَٱصْفَحُوا۟** — "pardon and overlook".

**عفو** — root *ʿ-f-w*: to erase, to wipe out the trace of something. It is used of the wind's erasing tracks on sand. Pardon, then, is not "I accept it but I do not forget"; it is **to erase the trace**.

**صفح** — root *ṣ-f-ḥ*: **a page, a surface.** The verb means "to turn the page", or "to turn one's face". That is, to close the matter over and move on.

The distance between the hardness of the diagnosis and the mildness of the command balances the general tone of the section: to **see** what the other side is doing and to know how to **behave** towards it are two different works. Seeing does not require retaliating.

---

## 2/110 — What is sent on ahead

*"Keep up the prayer and give the alms. Whatever good you send on ahead for yourselves, you will find it with God. God sees what you do."*

### The placing of the verse

Where this verse is placed cannot be read apart from its content.

The previous verse had given a very hard diagnosis and then said *"pardon and overlook"*. The other side's envy was recounted, but the road of retaliation was closed. After such a command a gap remains: what, then, is to be done?

The answer is this verse: **prayer, alms, the good sent on ahead.** The energy is directed not at the other side but at one's own work.

**I record this as my own reading:** the sūra is teaching a stance here — instead of occupying oneself with the one who bears the hostility, to go on doing the work that is the object of the hostility. The command *"race one another to good things"* at verse 148 is the plainer form of the same redirection; there too, in the middle of a dispute, the subject will be turned to the deed.

### وَمَا تُقَدِّمُوا۟ لِأَنفُسِكُم مِّنْ خَيْرٍ تَجِدُوهُ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ

This sentence occurs once more in the Qurʾān in exactly the same wording — **al-Muzzammil 73/20** — and was treated there; I do not repeat it. The summary of the two findings there:

- **تُقَدِّمُوا۟** (root **ق-د-م**, Form II): to send on ahead. The picture is that of a person about to travel sending his goods on before him.
- **لِأَنفُسِكُم**: the verse does not say "send for God", it says **"for yourselves"**. The act of giving is named as a transaction in the giver's favour.

There are two differences here, and both come from the context of this sūra.

**First, the end of the sentence.** In al-Muzzammil the sentence continued *"better and greater in reward"* — that is, the **increase** of the return was stressed. That part is absent here; the sentence says *"you will find it with God"* and stops.

**تَجِدُوهُ** — root **و-ج-د**: to find. The verb states the outcome of a search. And its object is a pronoun: *tajidū**hu*** — "you will find **it**". What is found, then, is not its return; it is **the thing sent itself.**

This is a fine distinction. The sentence does not describe a payment; it describes **the taking back of a deposit**. The thing sent is standing there.

**Second, the closing attribute.** In al-Muzzammil it said *"God is forgiving, merciful"*. Here: **إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ بَصِيرٌ** — *"God sees what you do."*

*Baṣīr* — root **ب-ص-ر**: to see, but specifically to see **discerningly**; *baṣīra* is from the same root. The placing of this attribute here connects with the previous verse: the other side's hidden envy (109) and what a person sends on ahead (110) fall within the same seeing. The work supposed to go unrewarded because it is unseen is in fact seen.

### The coming together of prayer and alms

**أَقِيمُوا۟ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَءَاتُوا۟ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ** — this pair occurs for the third time in the sūra: at verse 43 as a command, at verse 83 as an item of the covenant, here as a command again.

At verse 43 it was recorded that the verb *iqāma* means "to set up, to hold standing", and that what is asked for is not the prayer's being "performed" but its being "raised upright". The same distinction holds here.

And the word order is worth recording: after the two obligations are commanded, the sentence does not close with them but passes into an open-ended field with **"whatever good you send on ahead"**. The two obligations listed, that is, are set down not as a ceiling but as a floor. This is the same structure as the definition of *birr* at verse 177, which lists prayer and alms and does not stop there.

---

## 2/111-112 — "Bring your proof"

*"They said, 'None will enter the garden but those who are Jews or Christians.' That is their wishful fancy. Say: **Bring your proof, if you are truthful.**"*

### تِلْكَ أَمَانِيُّهُمْ

The word used is the same as the word at verse 78: **amānī** — wishful fancy, a conviction drawn from wishing. There it was used of those who do not know the Book; here of a claim about salvation.

The link is plain: a religion that does not rest on knowledge rests on **wishing**. And the natural form of wishing is to reserve salvation for one's own group.

### هَاتُوا۟ بُرْهَٰنَكُمْ

The answer itself is more important than the content of the answer. The Qurʾān does not content itself with refusing the claim; it **asks for proof.**

**بُرْهَان** — root *b-r-h*: certainty, clarity. A *burhān* is a conclusive proof about which no doubt is possible; the term is used in the same sense in logic.

This demand is one repeated in the Qurʾān, and it is striking: what is asked of a religious claim is not a louder repetition but **evidence**. The text calls its own addressees to the same measure — indeed the same expression occurs at al-Anbiyāʾ 21/24 and al-Naml 27/64 as well.

### The criterion again the same

And immediately afterwards, a criterion is put in the place of the claim:

> **بَلَىٰ مَنْ أَسْلَمَ وَجْهَهُۥ لِلَّهِ وَهُوَ مُحْسِنٌ فَلَهُۥٓ أَجْرُهُۥ عِندَ رَبِّهِۦ**
> *"No! Whoever submits his whole self to God while doing good, his reward is with his Lord."*

This sentence repeats the structure of verse 62 exactly: a group's claim is advanced, refused, and **a personal criterion** put in its place. And the criterion is again in two parts: **submission** (inward) and **iḥsān** (outward).

**أَسْلَمَ وَجْهَهُۥ** — "he submitted his face". In Arabic *wajh* (face) can stand for the whole — the face is a person's most visible and most recognisable part. That submission is with the whole and not with a part is said by this word.

**مُحْسِن** — root *ḥ-s-n*: beauty. *Iḥsān* means both "to do good" and "to do a work beautifully". The criterion, that is, does not stop at doing good; it includes **doing the work as it should be done.**

The sūra thus repeats fifty verses later the measure it set up in the sixties. All the historical narrative in between is there to show why that measure is needed: everywhere belonging has been counted a security, after a while the security itself has turned into the religion.

---

# 2/113-129 — Direction, place and Abraham

## 2/113 — Mutual annulment

*"The Jews said, 'The Christians stand on nothing'; and the Christians said, 'The Jews stand on nothing' — and that while they recite the Book. Those who do not know said the like of their word."*

The verse builds two sentences symmetrically: the same expression, in two directions. And both sides have a **book** in hand.

The really striking sentence is at the end: **كَذَٰلِكَ قَالَ ٱلَّذِينَ لَا يَعْلَمُونَ مِثْلَ قَوْلِهِمْ** — *"Those who do not know said the like of their word."*

The word of those who know and the word of those who do not know have **become the same.** Possessing knowledge did not, in the end, change the sentence spoken. This is one of the hardest criticisms in the section: if reading a book does not keep a person from annulling the other side wholesale, what is the function of that reading?

A measure can be built from here: the way to tell whether a view rests on knowledge or on belonging is to see whether someone **without knowledge** who shares the same belonging defends it too. If the answer is yes, the carrier is not knowledge.

---

## 2/114 — Barring a place of worship

*"Who is more unjust than one who bars God's name from being remembered in God's places of worship and strives for their ruin?"*

The verse attaches its heaviest adjective (*aẓlam* — "more unjust") to a single act: **barring God from being remembered in a place of worship.**

What must be noticed is that the expression is **general**. The text does not say "your places of worship"; it says *"God's places of worship"*. In the classical commentaries it is said that this verse points to particular historical events — the destruction of al-Masjid al-Aqṣā, or the Meccans' keeping the Muslims from the Kaʿba; but the wording of the ruling has not been confined to these.

**سَعَىٰ فِى خَرَابِهَآ** — "**strove** for their ruin". The verb states effort: the destruction here is not an incidental outcome but a targeted work.

---

## 2/115 — "Wherever you turn"

*"To God belong the east and the west. Wherever you turn, there is the face of God. God is all-encompassing, all-knowing."*

One of the widest-horizoned verses in the sūra, and it needs careful reading, because it is open to two misunderstandings at once.

**فَثَمَّ وَجْهُ ٱللَّهِ** — "there is the face of God". The word *wajh*, as we saw at verse 112, though it means "face", can stand for the whole and for turning towards. The widespread understanding here is "the direction to which God's good pleasure is turned" or "the side of turning to Him".

**What the verse says:** God cannot be confined to a place. A direction is not holy in itself. Between east and west there is no difference in respect of nearness to God; both are His.

**What the verse does not say:** that direction has no importance at all. Indeed the same sūra will give the command of the qibla a few verses later (2/144) and require the turning to the Kaʿba in prayer.

The two do not conflict, because they speak of two different things. The qibla is not the direction in which God is — it is **an arrangement that makes unity visible**. Millions of people turning to the same point is not because there is a holiness at that point but because scatteredness is itself a problem. This verse does not make the qibla meaningless; it says **what the qibla is not**.

Indeed this distinction is made by the Qurʾān itself immediately after the qibla verses: *"Righteousness is not that you turn your faces to the east and the west"* (al-Baqara 2/177).

---

## 2/116-117 — He says "Be" and it is

*"They said, 'God has taken a son.' Far be it from Him! To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth… He is the originator of the heavens and the earth. When He decrees a matter, He only says to it 'Be', and it is."*

**بَدِيع** — root *b-d-ʿ*: **having no precedent, done for the first time.** From the same root come *bidʿa* (something brought in afterwards) and *ibdāʿ* (original production). A *badīʿ* is one who brings forth something without looking at a model, something with no antecedent.

The word's place here is meaningful: where the claim of taking a son is refused, **creation without a model** is stressed. For taking a son is the way of beings that multiply by reproduction — producing a like of oneself. A being who creates without a model has no need of this. The same logic as *"He has not begotten, and He has not been begotten"* in Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ is built here with a different word.

**كُن فَيَكُونُ** — "He says 'Be', and it is". The expression pictures creation not as a **process** but as a **word**. Material, tool, labour, time — none of them is named.

A qualification has to be entered here: this expression says not that creation is in fact instantaneous but that **it carries no difficulty whatever for God**. Indeed the same Qurʾān speaks of the stages of creation too — the human being's being shaped stage by stage, the heavens and the earth being created "in six days". The two accounts do not conflict: one says *how* the process works, the other says *whose* the command is.

---

## 2/118 — The same objection repeated

*"Those who do not know said, 'If only God would speak to us, or a sign would come to us!' Those before them said the same thing. **Their hearts have become alike.**"*

**تَشَٰبَهَتْ قُلُوبُهُمْ** — "their hearts have become alike".

This short sentence may be taken as the summary of the whole section. For fifty verses the sūra had recounted the story of an old community; here it is said why that story was recounted: **because the same objection is repeated in the same form.**

The demand is the same too: at verse 55 it was said *"we will not believe until we see God plainly"*; here it is said *"if only God would speak to us"*. Centuries have passed, the addressee has changed, the language has changed — the demand has not.

And the character of the demand is the same: to ask for a direct, unmediated, personal proof. Such a demand is not impossible to meet; but when it is met, a new one is asked for — because what is really wanted is not the proof but **the postponement that demanding a proof provides**.

The verse had given its answer in the preceding sentence: *"We have made the signs plain for a people who want to know with certainty."* The sign, that is, is already there; what is missing is the intention to look.

---

## 2/119 — The definition of the task

*"We have sent you with the truth, as a bringer of good news and a warner. You are not answerable for the companions of the blaze."*

This verse is placed between 118 and 120 and binds the two together. The sequence works thus:

| Verse | What it does |
|---|---|
| **118** | The demand: *"If only God would speak to us, if only a sign would come to us."* |
| **119** | **The definition of the task:** to bring good news and to warn |
| **120** | The limit of expectation: *"they will not be satisfied."* |

The middle verse, that is, says **where responsibility ends** after a demand that cannot be met.

### بَشِير and نَذِير

The pair is the most frequently used description of the prophetic task in the Qurʾān.

**بَشِير** — root **ب-ش-ر**. The relation of this root to *bashara* (skin, the surface of the body) was treated at verse 25 of the sūra: good news is news that shows in the face — the skin of one who receives good news changes colour. *Bushrā*, then, is news that **leaves a mark on the person**.

**نَذِير** — root **ن-ذ-ر**. This root was treated at verse 6 of the sūra and in many sections; I do not repeat it. Its summary: *indhār* is to give notice of an approaching danger **in time**; at the centre of the root lies the idea of being "beforehand" (*nadhr* — a word bound in advance, a vow).

The two words together cover **the whole** of the work: what is good and what is bad in what lies ahead are both announced. And both are verbs of announcing. In the definition of the task there is no **persuading, turning, obtaining a result**.

**And the order is always the same:** good news first, warning after. The Qurʾān rarely sets this pair in the reverse order. The same arrangement was present in the first verses of the sūra too — first the deliverance of those who guard themselves (2/5), then the condition of those who cover over (2/6).

### وَلَا تُسْـَٔلُ عَنْ أَصْحَٰبِ ٱلْجَحِيمِ

**A variant reading must be recorded, and it changes the meaning:**

| Reading | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **لَا تُسْـَٔلُ** (*lā tusʾalu*) — passive, a declarative sentence | "You **will not be held answerable** for them" — a statement |
| **لَا تَسْـَٔلْ** (*lā tasʾal*) — active, a prohibition | "Do **not ask** about them" — a command |

Both readings are transmitted. The widespread one is the first, and it fits the context better: the sentence lays down no prohibition; it announces that **a burden has been lifted**. **I do not impose a preference;** but it should be recorded that the second reading comes out at the same place — in both, something is taken from the shoulders of the one addressed.

**أَصْحَٰبِ ٱلْجَحِيمِ** — the word *aṣḥāb* does not mean "friends" but **those who are continually together with something**; it is from the root of *ṣuḥba*. The Qurʾān uses this word insistently for the garden and for the fire: a person is named not as a convict placed there but as **the people of that place**.

**ٱلْجَحِيم** — root **ج-ح-م**: the fierce heat of a fire, the blazing of embers. This root was treated in the discussions of al-Infiṭār and al-Nāziʿāt; I do not repeat it.

### The limit of responsibility

What this verse says is repeated at several points in the Qurʾān, and all of them look the same way: *"You cannot guide whom you love"* (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/56); *"You are not one to compel them"* (al-Ghāshiya 88/22).

The practical consequence of this is that it changes the language of conduct. A person responsible for the result turns to pressure in order to obtain the result; **a person responsible for conveying tells and leaves it.** The ruling *"there is no compulsion in religion"* at verse 256 of the sūra is the natural continuation of this definition of the task.

And this note is completed together with the next verse: at verse 120 it will be said that the other side will not be satisfied. Read together, the two verses yield a measure — **neither is their satisfaction the goal, nor is their outcome a burden.**

---

## 2/120 — "They will not be satisfied"

*"Neither the Jews nor the Christians will be satisfied with you until you follow their religion."*

This verse requires care, because it can easily be turned into a general declaration of enmity.

What the sentence says is that **an expectation is unrealistic**: that the search for agreement cannot be tied to the aim of satisfying the other side completely. For the condition of complete satisfaction is to give up one's own position entirely.

What the sentence does **not** say is this: that no relationship can be established with them, that no good can be done to them, that one cannot deal justly with them. The Qurʾān regulates all of this plainly elsewhere: *"God does not forbid you to be good and to deal justly with those who have not fought you over religion and have not driven you from your homes"* (al-Mumtaḥina 60/8). And the same sūra does not neglect to distinguish those who are good (Āl ʿImrān 3/113).

The verse, then, is a **warning about strategy**, not a rule of conduct: one's position cannot be determined by chasing approval. The relationship itself is a separate subject and is regulated by other verses.

---

## 2/121-123 — Reciting it as it should be recited, and the closing of the circle

*"Those to whom We have given the Book recite it as it should be recited; those are the ones who believe in it. And whoever rejects it — those are the losers. Children of Israel! Remember My favour that I bestowed on you, and that I preferred you above all beings. And guard yourselves against a day when no soul can pay anything for another; when no ransom is accepted from it, no intercession profits it, and they are not helped."*

### يَتْلُونَهُۥ حَقَّ تِلَاوَتِهِۦ

The root **ت-ل-و** was treated in the discussions of al-Bayyina (98/2) and al-Jumuʿa (62/2); I do not repeat it. The summary of the finding there: the primary sense of the root is not to read but **to come after, to follow, to go along behind**. Arabic also has *qirāʾa* for reading (root **ق-ر-أ**: to gather); the difference is this — *qirāʾa* emphasises the **gathering** of letters, *tilāwa* the **following** of the text. The one who recites is not the one in front but the one who comes behind.

Now put this into the verse. **حَقَّ تِلَاوَتِهِ** — "giving its tilāwa its due."

The sentence does not say "the due of reading", it says **"the due of following"**. To give the following of a thing its due is really to follow it — to walk behind it where the road is easy and part from it where the road is hard is not to give following its due.

**I record this in this commentary as my own reading,** because it follows from the sense of the word itself: the verse's measure lies not in pronunciation or in memorisation but in **going where the text goes**.

And this measure stands as the exact opposite of the attitudes the section criticises. Up to this point the sūra had listed four broken relations with the book:

| Verse | The relation established with the Book |
|---|---|
| **2/75** | **Bending** it after understanding it (*taḥrīf*) |
| **2/78** | **Voicing** it without knowing, on wishful fancy |
| **2/79** | Writing it by hand and **ascribing** it |
| **2/85** | Believing in part of it and **leaving** part |
| **2/121** | **Following it as it should be followed** |

The fifth is the answer to all the rest. And note: the verse does not reserve this to a group — it says *"those to whom We have given the Book"* and separates out those among them who recite it as it should be recited. The same scrupulousness of distinction was seen at verses 99-100 and 105.

**أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ يُؤْمِنُونَ بِهِۦ** — "those are the ones who believe in it." The order of the sentence is worth recording: reading first, faith after. Faith, that is, is set down not as the **condition** of the reading but as its **result**.

### 122-123: the circle within the sūra

These two verses are the **exact repetition** of verses 47-48 of the sūra.

This is an arrangement that cannot be explained as coincidence. The section on the Children of Israel had **opened** at verse 47 with these two verses, had run for seventy-five verses, and now **closes** with the same two verses at 122-123. In Arabic, and in literature generally, this is called **ring structure**: a section is ended with the sentence it began with.

If one looks at what has been placed inside the ring, the function of the repetition becomes clear:

- **At the opening (47-48):** "I preferred you" — and immediately after it, in four items, that this is no guarantee.
- **Inside (49-121):** the record of the community to whom the preferment was given — the calf, the sabbath, the cow, distortion, concealment, the throwing away of the covenant.
- **At the closing (122-123):** the same two sentences.

A sentence that on first reading was a reminder comes back, after the seventy-five verses in between, **with a different weight**. The words are the same; the reader is not.

**I record this as an observation about the structure.** The sūra uses this technique once more: at verses 134 and 141 the sentence *"that was a community that has passed away"* is likewise repeated word for word twice, and in both cases it comes after a list of ancestors.

### What comes after the repetition

And immediately after the ring closes, the sūra changes its subject: with verse 124 the section on **Abraham** begins.

This transition is meaningful. The section on the Children of Israel had shown, through the record of one lineage, that belonging is no guarantee. Immediately afterwards it goes back to the name on which that lineage itself rests — and what will be said there is the most concentrated expression of the same thing: *"My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers"* (2/124).

At 47-48 a community's chosenness was not counted a guarantee; at 124 **a prophet's lineage** will not be counted a guarantee. The closing of the ring and the opening of the new section say the same measure at two levels.

There is also a small difference of word order between verses 48 and 123, and it must be recorded: the order at 48, *"no intercession is accepted… no ransom is taken"*, is exchanged at 123 into *"no ransom is accepted… no intercession profits"*. In the classical commentaries such exchanges are explained as a feature of style that keeps repetition from going dull. **I load no meaning on it beyond that.**

---

# 2/124-129 — Abraham

Here the sūra moves onto new ground. Up to this point Moses and the Children of Israel had been recounted; now it turns **further back**, to the forefather common to everyone.

Its function within the dispute is plain: both sides counted their own tradition the condition of salvation (2/111). But Abraham comes before both — and is therefore in the monopoly of neither. The Qurʾān will say this plainly a few sūras later: *"Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he was a ḥanīf, one submitted"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/67).

## 2/124 — "My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers"

*"His Lord tested Abraham (Ibrāhīm) with certain words, and he fulfilled them. He said, 'I am making you a leader for people.' Abraham said, 'And of my offspring?' God said, **'My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers.'**"*

This short dialogue contains one of the Qurʾān's most decisive sentences on politics and authority.

**إِمَامًا لِّلنَّاسِ** — "a leader for people". *Imām*, root *ʾ-m-m*: the one in front, the one followed (from the same root come *umma* and *amām* — front). And note: it does not say "for those who follow you" but **"for people"**. The scope of the task is open not to a group but to humanity.

**And then Abraham makes a request:** *"And of my offspring?"* This is an entirely human request — a father wanting what has been given to him to continue in his children.

**The answer does not refuse the request; it lays down a condition:** *"My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers."*

The consequences of this sentence are large:

- **Descent does not produce authority.** Being a prophet's child does not secure the inheriting of leadership.
- **The measure is moral.** What distinguishes is not lineage but whether one does wrong.
- **And this was said to a prophet who was asking.** The rule, that is, was not bent for anyone.

The idea of "the guarantee of belonging", dismantled from the beginning of the sūra, is cut once more here at its highest point: at verses 47-48 a community's chosenness was not counted a guarantee; here **a prophet's lineage** is not counted a guarantee.

This sentence stands against every form of religious authority built on a claim of descent throughout history. And its addressees are not only the old communities — claims of superiority based on lineage have not been lacking in Muslim history either.

## 2/125 — The Kaʿba: a refuge and a place of return

*"And when We made the House a **place of return** for people, and a **place of safety**."*

**مَثَابَة** — root *th-w-b*: **to turn, to come back.** From the same root comes *thawāb* (the return that comes back for what one has done). A *mathāba* is a place to which one returns again and again. The word itself describes not a single visit but a centre **returned to over and over**.

**أَمْن** — safety. The root *ʾ-m-n*; at the third verse we saw that the word *īmān* comes from this root. The name of the place and the name of the belief are thus from the same root: **safety.**

And note once again: *"for people"*. Not for a particular community.

## 2/126-129 — Abraham's prayers

Three prayers of Abraham come one after another, and their order is meaningful:

**The first — for the city:** *"My Lord, make this a secure town and provide its people with fruits."* Safety first, provision after. And the prayer is met elsewhere in the Qurʾān with the record that this city was in fact so: *"who has fed them against hunger and secured them against fear"* (Quraysh 106/4) — the same two needs, in the same order.

One detail: while Abraham qualifies his prayer with *"those of its people who believe"*, the answer removes the qualification — *"and the one who covers over I shall let enjoy a little."* Worldly provision, that is, is not conditional on faith; this distinction will be preserved in the later parts of the sūra too.

**The second — for acceptance:** *"Our Lord! Accept from us."* The prayer said while raising the walls of the Kaʿba is not for the soundness of the building but for its **acceptance**. The value of the work done is seen not in the work itself but in its being accepted.

**The third — for the future:** *"Our Lord! Send them a messenger from among themselves."* The prayer is not for himself but for what comes after him.

These three prayers complete the picture at the head of the section. The attitude criticised throughout the sūra was leaning on a privilege given in the past. Abraham's prayers look in exactly the opposite direction: he does not count what he has as a guarantee, he asks for it to be **accepted**; and his eye is not on himself but on those who will come after.

---

# 2/130-152 — The qibla, the middle community and the race

## 2/130-132 — "Submit"

*"Who turns away from the religion of Abraham but one who makes a fool of himself?… His Lord said to him, 'Submit', and he said, 'I have submitted to the Lord of all the worlds.'"*

**سَفِهَ نَفْسَهُۥ** — "he made himself a fool". We saw the sense of the root *safah*, "lightness, having no weight in the scale", at verse 13. There, there were those who counted the believers light; here the same word is used of the person who takes lightness upon himself by his own choice.

**أَسْلِمْ — أَسْلَمْتُ** — here *Islām* occurs not as the name of a religion but as a **verb**: "submit" and "I have submitted". Root *s-l-m*: to be sound, to be flawless, to be in wholeness. From the same root come *salām* (peace) and *salīm* (flawless).

This shows how the word is to be understood. *Islām* is to submit — but the submission here is not a defeat; it is **becoming whole and sound**. The same root carries both bending the neck and wholeness.

---

## 2/133 — The question on the deathbed

*"Or were you present when death came to Jacob? When he said to his sons, 'What will you serve after me?' They said, 'We will serve your God and the God of your fathers Abraham, Ishmael (Ismāʿīl) and Isaac (Isḥāq), one God; and to Him we are submitted.'"*

### أَمْ كُنتُمْ شُهَدَآءَ

The verse opens with a question, and the question changes the ground of the dispute.

The dispute was: to whom do Abraham and his line belong? Both sides were placing them inside their own tradition — this claim will be reported plainly a few verses later (2/140).

The verse's answer is not a counter-claim but a **question of testimony**: *"were you present?"*

The root of the word **شَهِيد** is **ش-ه-د**: to be present, to see with one's own eyes. English covers the two ends of this root with two unconnected words — *witness* and *martyr*; in Arabic *shāhid* and *shahīd* belong to one family, and what they have in common is **having been there**. The question, then, interrogates the source of the knowledge: what is the ground of this claim?

This is another form of the demand *"bring your proof"* at verse 111 of the sūra. There proof was asked for a claim about salvation; here eyewitness testimony is asked for a claim about history.

**And the verse answers the question it asks** — by recounting the scene. It does not say "you do not know" and stop; it says what happened.

### مَا تَعْبُدُونَ مِنۢ بَعْدِى

The scene itself is worth recording. This is the question a father on his deathbed asks his sons.

What is not asked is striking: how the property is to be divided, who will take charge, to whom the flocks will fall. The one thing asked is **whom they will serve**.

And the question is not "what will you believe" but **"what will you serve"**. The verb is from the root *ʿ-b-d*, and this root was treated in the discussion of al-Fātiḥa: *ʿabd* is one who belongs to his owner; service is a relation of belonging. What is asked, then, is not an opinion but an **allegiance**.

This verse can be read together with a ruling in the later parts of the sūra: at verse 180 the writing of a **bequest** will be commanded when death draws near. Here too there is a scene of bequest — but its content consists not of property but of service. **I record this as my own reading:** there is no conflict between the two verses but an order — in regulating the bequest of property, the Qurʾān shows in a scene what the real subject of inheritance is.

### ءَابَآئِكَ إِبْرَٰهِـۧمَ وَإِسْمَٰعِيلَ وَإِسْحَٰقَ

Three names are listed in the answer, and all three are named together as **"your fathers"**.

A note about the language is needed here: Ishmael is not Jacob's father or grandfather but his **uncle** (Jacob is the son of Isaac). That the word *ābāʾ* in Arabic is used collectively for grandfathers, uncles and remoter ancestors is a known usage, and the lexicographers record it. The expression is therefore not an error of lineage but the wide use of the word.

**And the naming of the names together does the verse's work.** Each side was separating out its own branch; the sentence gathers the two branches under a single heading, "fathers", and attaches them all to a single God.

**إِلَٰهًا وَٰحِدًا** — "one God." All the names in the sentence come together in these two words. What is recounted, then, is not a chain of descent but **a chain of one sentence**: not who came from whom, but that everyone said the same thing.

**وَنَحْنُ لَهُۥ مُسْلِمُونَ** — "and to Him we are submitted." The word is again from the root *s-l-m* and, as treated at verse 131, is used not as the name of a religion but as **the result of the verb**. And a nominal sentence is preferred: not "we shall submit" but "we are submitted" — not a promise about the future but a statement of a state.

One further detail: this answer was asked for the time **after** Jacob's death (*min baʿdī*), but the answer given is in the present. The question asks about the future, the answer states a condition. **My own reading:** the sentence intimates that an attitude in the future can be promised only as the continuation of the present state.

---

## 2/134 and 2/141 — The same sentence, twice

*"That was a community that has passed away. What they earned is theirs, what you earn is yours. You will not be asked about what they did."*

This verse occurs in the sūra **twice in exactly the same wording**: at 134 and at 141. A repetition on this scale is striking in the Qurʾān.

Both occurrences do the same work: each comes immediately after a chain of descent has been listed, and cuts the chain. In the first, Abraham and his sons had been named; in the second the same names are listed again. Each time, when the list ends, the same sentence comes down.

What it says is plain: **forebears leave an inheritance, they do not leave a record.** To be descended from Abraham does not write Abraham's deeds to a person's account. And the reverse is true too — "you will not be asked about what they did", that is, the fault of one's forebears is not loaded onto anyone's back either.

This is the generalised form of the principle *"My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers"* at verse 124 of the sūra. There a prophet's lineage was no guarantee; here no lineage at all enters the reckoning, either for or against.

**The repetition is itself a method.** The Qurʾān does not say this sentence once and pass on; it repeats it after both lists. It is as though it were saying: whenever you read this list, at the end of it you will recall the same thing.

---

## 2/135-137 — "No, the religion of Abraham"

*"They said, 'Be Jews or Christians and you will be guided.' Say: 'No! We follow the way of Abraham, a ḥanīf; and he was not of the mushrikūn.' Say: 'We believe in God and in what has been sent down to us; and in what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and his descendants; and in what was given to Moses and Jesus; and in what was given to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them. And to Him we are submitted.' If they believe as you have believed, they are guided. If they turn away, they are only in a breach. God will suffice you against them. He is the hearing, the knowing."*

### The form of the invitation

**كُونُوا۟ هُودًا أَوْ نَصَٰرَىٰ تَهْتَدُوا۟** — "**be** Jews or Christians and you will be guided."

The structure of the call is striking: guidance is placed behind an **identity**. First be this, then you will have found the way. The order, that is, begins not with conduct but with **membership**.

This is exactly the understanding dismantled at verses 62 and 111 of the sūra. There too salvation had been reserved to a group; here the same understanding meets us in the form of an **invitation**.

That the answer begins with **بَلْ** matters: in Arabic *bal* is a particle correcting the preceding sentence — it says "no, not that; this". The offer is refused, but no third identity is put in its place. What is put there is **an older line**.

### مِلَّةَ إِبْرَٰهِـۧمَ حَنِيفًا

**مِلَّة** — root **م-ل-ل**. The lexicographers relate the word to the sense "to dictate, to have written down" (*imlāʾ*): a *milla* is a way conveyed by a prophet, **spoken and set down**. The word differs slightly from "religion": *dīn* is more the road a person keeps, while *milla* is **the conveyed whole** of that road. This distinction is transmitted as the lexicographers record it.

**حَنِيف** — root **ح-ن-ف**: **to bend, to incline in one direction.** The lexicographers attach the word to a concrete use having to do with the foot: an *aḥnaf* is a person whose feet turn inward. The idea that follows is this — a *ḥanīf* is one who **departs from what is common and inclines in another direction**.

The word's function here is exactly that. What is described is a person who turns alone in another direction while everyone around him goes one way. And the description is given to Abraham as an attribute: what distinguishes him is not that he joined a group but that **he joined none of them**.

**وَمَا كَانَ مِنَ ٱلْمُشْرِكِينَ** — "and he was not of the mushrikūn." This clause placed at the end of the sentence may look superfluous; but it has a function. Being a *ḥanīf*, that is, departing from the majority, is no value on its own — one who departs may fall into another error. The clause fixes the direction of the departure.

### قُولُوٓا۟ ءَامَنَّا — the scope of the declaration of faith

Verse 136 gives a **formula of faith**, and the most striking side of the formula is its length.

What is listed: God, what was sent down to us, what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, his descendants, what was given to Moses, what was given to Jesus, and what was given to **all the prophets**.

The declaration, then, begins with one's own book and does not stop there. And the list is not closed: by saying *"and in what was given to the prophets from their Lord"*, a nameless field is left open.

**لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّنْهُمْ** — "we make no distinction between any of them." The root *f-r-q*: to separate, to divide; *tafrīq* — to set a distinction, to open a gap between.

This sentence is the direct counterpart of the criticism at verse 85 of the sūra: there it was said *"do you believe in part of the Book and reject part of it?"* Here the opposite stance is formulated. And the same sentence will occur once more in the last verses of the sūra (2/285) — the text repeats this expression both in the middle of the section and at the sūra's close.

**A grammatical detail:** the word *bayna* (between) normally requires at least two things; here it is used with *aḥadin* (one person). The lexicographers explain it thus: *aḥad* acquires an **inclusive** sense in a negative context — "none of them". The sentence therefore does not mean "we do not separate two of them" but **"we make no distinction among any of them"**.

**And note: the sentence does not say "they are all the same."** The Qurʾān says plainly that there is a difference of degree among the prophets (2/253). What is refused is not **degree** but **accepting one and rejecting another by discrimination**. The two are different works.

### فَإِنْ ءَامَنُوا۟ بِمِثْلِ مَآ ءَامَنتُم بِهِۦ

Verse 137 applies the measure to the other side too: *"if they believe **as** you have believed, they are guided."*

What is offered, then, is not joining a group but **believing with the same scope**. The measure differs from the form of the offer at verse 135: there it was "be this", here it is "believe this". One asks for membership, the other for content.

**شِقَاق** — root **ش-ق-ق**: **to split, to divide in two.** This root was treated in the discussions of ʿAbasa and al-Ṭāriq; the finding there holds here too: *shiqq* — a half, a piece; *mashaqqa* — the hardship the lexicographers attach to the idea of "what splits a person in two".

The pattern *shiqāq* (*mufāʿala*) conveys reciprocity, and its picture is very concrete: **each standing on one side of the split**. The division here, that is, is not a difference of opinion but the two sides' **falling on opposite banks**.

The word will come once more in the sūra with a still harder adjective: *shiqāqin baʿīd* — a far-off breach (2/176).

**فَسَيَكْفِيكَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ** — "God **will suffice** you against them." The root *k-f-y*: to be enough, to take a matter upon oneself and exempt the person from it.

The placing of the sentence is worth recording. After the breach is established, nothing is said about what is to be done — no strategy, no counter-move is given. The one thing said is that the matter does not rest on the shoulders of the one addressed. This looks the same way as the clause *"you are not answerable for the companions of the blaze"* at verse 119.

**وَهُوَ ٱلسَّمِيعُ ٱلْعَلِيمُ** — "He is the hearing, the knowing." The choice of these two attributes here fits the context: throughout the section **words spoken** had been reported — "be Jews", "we believe in what was sent down to us", "were you present". The closing records that all these words are heard.

---

## 2/138 — The dye of God

*"The dye of God! And who is better than God at dyeing?"*

**صِبْغَة** — root *ṣ-b-gh*: **to dye, to steep in colour.** It is used of a cloth being dipped into a dye vat.

The classical commentaries widely note that this expression was used in answer to a religious practice of the period — the registering of children into a religion by dipping them in water. On this, the sentence says: religion is not something **applied on top** by an operation performed once.

The word itself carries this difference. Dyeing is not smearing: what is smeared stays on the surface and wipes off; **dye works into the fibre.** The colour of a dyed cloth is not a coating but the cloth's own state.

This comparison belongs to the same family as the pictures we have seen elsewhere in the sūra: the calf's being **drunk into** the heart (2/93), sin's **encompassing** a person (2/81), *khushūʿ*'s **softening** of the soil (2/45). In all of them the measure is the same — what stays outside, and what works inside.

---

## 2/139-141 — The dispute, the concealed testimony and the repetition

*"Say: Do you argue with us about God, when He is our Lord and your Lord? Our deeds are ours and your deeds are yours. And we are sincerely devoted to Him. Or do you say that Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and his descendants were Jews or Christians? Say: Do you know better, or God? Who is more unjust than one who conceals a testimony he has from God? God is not heedless of what you do."*

### أَتُحَآجُّونَنَا فِى ٱللَّهِ

The root **ح-ج-ج**: to bring a proof, to prevail in an argument; *ḥujja* is from the same root. The pattern is **mufāʿala** (*muḥājja*) — that is, a two-sided, reciprocal dispute.

The objection within the question is to the **subject** of the dispute: *fi'llāh* — "about God". What is being disputed, that is, is not a ruling, a practice or a question of history; it is **whose God is**.

And the answer takes aim at exactly that point: **وَهُوَ رَبُّنَا وَرَبُّكُمْ** — "He is our Lord and your Lord." The premise of the dispute is refused. What was supposed to be indivisible is the very thing both sides already share.

**وَلَنَآ أَعْمَٰلُنَا وَلَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلُكُمْ** — "our deeds are ours, yours are yours." The sentence turns the dispute from belonging to **deed**: what cannot be shared is not the Lord but each person's own work.

This is the same pattern as the sentence *"what they earned is theirs, what you earn is yours"* repeated at verses 134 and 141 of the sūra. The same measure, in three separate places: once for forebears, once for those addressed.

**وَنَحْنُ لَهُۥ مُخْلِصُونَ** — root **خ-ل-ص**: a thing's being **pure, unmixed**; its state with nothing else blended in. The verb is used for the separating out of butter from milk. *Ikhlāṣ* is to mix no other intention into a work while doing it.

The placing of this word here completes the sentence as a whole: after the claims of belonging have been refused on both sides, the measure that remains is **mixing nothing else in**.

### أَمْ تَقُولُونَ إِنَّ إِبْرَٰهِـۧمَ… كَانُوا۟ هُودًا أَوْ نَصَٰرَىٰ

Verse 140 reports the concrete claim in the dispute: the chain of prophets is assigned to one of two groups.

The answer comes in two steps.

**The first is a question:** *"Do you know better, or God?"* The claim, that is, is interrogated as to its source of knowledge — a continuation of the question *"were you present?"* at verse 133.

**The second is a historical note** — and this note is made plainly elsewhere in the Qurʾān: *"The Torah and the Gospel were only sent down after him. Will you not use your reason?"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/65). The claim, that is, is impossible in point of time: the names mentioned lived before the names ascribed to them came into being.

**The measure these two answers set up together is worth recording:** if a claim of ownership over the past can be supported neither by testimony nor by chronology, its only remaining ground is **wish.** The sūra had already given this condition a name: *amānī* (2/78, 2/111).

### مَنْ أَظْلَمُ مِمَّن كَتَمَ شَهَٰدَةً عِندَهُۥ

This is the heaviest sentence of the verse and must be read carefully.

**أَظْلَم** — "more unjust". This elative pattern had occurred once before in the sūra: at verse 114, *"who is more unjust than one who bars God's name from being remembered in God's places of worship?"* In both places the heaviest adjective is attached to the act of **cutting something off**: there, worship; here, knowledge.

**كَتَمَ شَهَٰدَةً** — "he concealed a testimony." The sūra's chain of **katm** (concealment) comes here to a knot:

| Verse | What is concealed |
|---|---|
| **2/42** | The truth itself — knowingly |
| **2/72** | A murder — *"God brings out what you conceal"* |
| **2/140** | **Testimony** — and one that has "come from Him" at that |
| **2/146** | A known truth — *"a group of them conceals it knowingly"* |
| **2/159** | The proofs sent down — and its making good is *bayān* |
| **2/283** | Testimony — *"do not conceal the testimony"* |

Six occurrences use the same verb, and the last comes back as a **rule of law** in the legal section of the sūra. What is recounted through the section as a moral fault turns in the end into a regulation.

**عِندَهُۥ مِنَ ٱللَّهِ** — "that he has, come from God." The clause matters: what is concealed is not the person's own opinion but **a trust in his hands**. Testimony is not the property of the one who holds it; the one who holds it is only a carrier.

**I record this as my own reading:** concealment is therefore handled here not as a silence but as a **disposal of property**. A person who keeps silent looks as though he is doing nothing; in fact he is withholding something that is not his.

**وَمَا ٱللَّهُ بِغَٰفِلٍ عَمَّا تَعْمَلُونَ** — "God is not heedless of what you do." *Ghafla* — being unaware of something, the wandering of attention. The sentence is built with a negation: instead of "He knows" it says "He is not heedless". The assumption on which concealment rests — that it is not seen — is targeted directly.

### 141: the repetition

The section closes with the exact repetition of the sentence at verse 134: *"That was a community that has passed away…"*

This repetition and its function were handled under the heading **`## 2/134 and 2/141`**; I do not repeat the finding there. Here I only record its place: the sentence again comes **after a list of descent** — at verse 140 Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and his descendants had been listed — and it again cuts the list.

**And one must look at what has been put between the two repetitions:** between 135 and 140, the claim to own that list has been disputed. The sentence, that is, does the same work both times: the list is enumerated, the claiming begins, the sentence comes down.

---

# 2/142-152 — The change of qibla

This is the second great turning point of the sūra. After the emigration to Medina the prayer was performed for a time facing Jerusalem, and then the qibla was turned to Mecca.

## 2/142 — "The foolish will say"

*"Some foolish people will say, 'What has turned them from the qibla they used to face?' Say: **To God belong the east and the west.**"*

Two things are striking.

**First: the objection is announced in advance.** The verse is built in the future tense — "they will say". Before the change is made, the objection that will be made is recorded. This shows that the objection was no surprise.

**Second: the answer is a repetition of verse 115.** *"To God belong the east and the west"* — the same sentence was there. The sūra had made a preparation within itself: twenty-seven verses before announcing the change of qibla, it had said that direction is not holy in itself.

The answer to the objection is thereby already given: nothing is lost by the direction changing, because there was no holiness to lose.

## 2/143 — The middle community

*"Thus We have made you a **middle community**, that you may be witnesses to people, and that the Messenger may be a witness to you."*

**وَسَط** — root *w-s-ṭ*. The word carries two senses at once, and in Arabic the two are inseparable:

- **Middle.** What stands between two extremes, balanced, not running to excess.
- **Most valuable, choice.** In Arabic *"wasaṭ al-qawm"* means not the middle of a people but **the best** of them. The most valuable stone of a necklace is at its centre; the most honoured branch of a tribe is the "middle" branch.

The two senses are not together by accident. In the logic of the Arabic language **the centre is at the same time where the value lies** — the extremes are a being flung out, the middle is soundness. In Turkish the borrowed word *vasat* has drifted the other way and come to mean "ordinary, average"; and the usual English renderings show the same drift — "middle" slides into "middling", "average" into "mediocre". Anyone who reads the verse through such a word understands the opposite of what it says.

This expression joins with the measure we set up at the last verse of al-Fātiḥa: there the road was described as being far from two extremes — those who do not act on their knowledge, and those who stray with effort. The middle community is the name of the community standing on that road.

**شُهَدَآءَ عَلَى ٱلنَّاسِ** — "witnesses to people". The task is defined not as superiority but as **testimony**. A witness is one who reports what he has seen; he does not himself pass judgement, he provides the basis for judgement.

And how the testimony is to be borne is hidden in the word itself: a witness must be **visible**. A community's testimony is borne not by what it says but by the state in which it is seen.

## Why did the qibla change?

The verse gives the answer itself:

> **وَمَا جَعَلْنَا ٱلْقِبْلَةَ ٱلَّتِى كُنتَ عَلَيْهَآ إِلَّا لِنَعْلَمَ مَن يَتَّبِعُ ٱلرَّسُولَ مِمَّن يَنقَلِبُ عَلَىٰ عَقِبَيْهِ**
> *"We made the qibla you used to face only so that We might distinguish the one who follows the Messenger from the one who turns back on his heels."*

The change is itself a **test**. And what it tests is very specific: is a person's attachment to his **habit** or to the **command**?

This is a fine distinction. A person who keeps up a practice for a long time becomes attached to it — and that attachment feels like piety. But if what is attached to is the practice itself, resistance arises when the practice changes. The change makes exactly this difference visible: when the command changes although the one who gives it is the same, a person learns whom he is following.

**يَنقَلِبُ عَلَىٰ عَقِبَيْهِ** — "to turn back on one's heels". A concrete picture: someone walking, in order to turn, first pivots on his heel. The expression conveys a change of direction by a bodily movement — and since the question of the qibla is itself a question of direction, the comparison comes from inside the subject.

## 2/144 — "We see your face turning about towards the sky"

*"We see your face turning about towards the sky. We shall surely turn you to a qibla that will please you."*

One of the most human details in the sūra. A waiting is described — the request is not put into words, but is read from the movement of a face.

And in granting the request the verse names as its ground **the person's own contentment**: *"a qibla that will please you"*. In a text where commands are generally given without a ground, this clause is striking.

---

## 2/145-147 — "No proof avails"

*"Even if you brought every kind of proof to those who were given the Book, they would not follow your qibla; nor are you a follower of their qibla; nor do they follow one another's qibla. If you were to follow their desires after the knowledge that has come to you, you would then be among the wrongdoers. Those to whom We have given the Book recognise it as they recognise their own sons. But a group of them conceals the truth knowingly. The truth is from your Lord; so do not be of those who doubt."*

### Three negative sentences

Verse 145 sets three separate negations one after another, and together they build a diagnosis:

| | Sentence | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *mā tabiʿū qiblatak* | They will not follow your qibla |
| **2** | *wa mā anta bi-tābiʿin qiblatahum* | Nor will you follow theirs |
| **3** | *wa mā baʿḍuhum bi-tābiʿin qiblata baʿḍ* | **Nor do they follow one another's** |

The first two sentences are expected: they describe a mutual separation. **The third ends the whole dispute.**

For the objection rested on this assumption: there is one right qibla, and departing from it is straying. But those objecting do not turn the same way **among themselves** either. There is, that is, no measure agreed upon; each group has its own direction.

**I record this as an observation about the logic:** the sincerity of a demand can be measured by looking at whether the one making it applies that demand within his own house. The third sentence does exactly this.

**بِكُلِّ ءَايَةٍ** — "with every kind of proof." The sentence is built with *la-in*, that is, it is a **hypothetical condition**: the most extreme possibility is supposed. And the outcome does not change. This is an application of the finding at verse 6 of the sūra: *"it is the same for them whether you warn them or do not warn them."* There too the issue was not the insufficiency of the proof.

### أَهْوَآءَهُم — and the one the threat addresses

**أَهْوَاء** — the plural of *hawā*. The root **ه-و-ي** was treated at verse 87 of the sūra: **to fall, to go downward**; *hāwiya* (an abyss) is from the same root. Arabic has given desire the name "what pulls a person downward".

And that the word comes in the **plural** joins with the finding in the third sentence: not one *hawā* but *ahwāʾ*. This is also why each group has its own direction — desire does not unite, because every desire has a direction of its own. By contrast the **ٱلْعِلْم** that comes at the end of the sentence is singular.

**وَلَئِنِ ٱتَّبَعْتَ أَهْوَآءَهُم… إِنَّكَ إِذًا لَّمِنَ ٱلظَّٰلِمِينَ** — the one the warning addresses is **the Prophet himself**.

This is one of the most striking features of the Qurʾān's style, and it is seen several times in the sūra. The hardest clauses are placed not on the other side but on **one's own**. Indeed in the same sūra *"do you forget yourselves while you go on reciting the Book?"* (2/44) was also said to its own addressees.

And the ground of the clause is stated: *min baʿdi mā jāʾaka mina'l-ʿilm* — **"after the knowledge that has come to you."** The sūra's most repeated measure once again: the issue is not ignorance but what is done with what is known.

### يَعْرِفُونَهُۥ كَمَا يَعْرِفُونَ أَبْنَآءَهُمْ

This comparison at verse 146 occurs once more in the Qurʾān in the same wording (al-Anʿām 6/20).

**The field the comparison chooses is striking.** A person's recognising his own child is not a learned piece of knowledge: no thinking is needed to pick him out in a crowd, there is no margin of error, it requires no proof. Recognition is chosen here as **the most everyday form of certainty**.

The verse does not say "they knew"; it says **"they recognised"**. That is the difference between knowledge and recognition: knowledge is learned and can be forgotten; recognition is what happens on looking.

**The referent of the pronoun is a matter of disagreement and must not be concealed:**

| View | Whom or what *yaʿrifūna**hu*** points to | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **The Prophet** | They recognise him from the descriptions made known to them | The one addressed in the verse is the Prophet; the same context at al-Anʿām 6/20 |
| **The truth / the matter of the qibla** | They recognise the rightness of the change of qibla | The context is the qibla; the next sentence says *"they conceal the truth"* |

**I do not impose a preference.** The place both readings arrive at is the same: there is something known, and it is being concealed.

**وَإِنَّ فَرِيقًا مِّنْهُمْ لَيَكْتُمُونَ ٱلْحَقَّ وَهُمْ يَعْلَمُونَ** — and once more the distinction is preserved: **a group.** The scrupulousness seen at verses 99, 105 and 121 of the sūra continues here; the ruling is not closed over the whole community.

The chain of the verb *katm* in the sūra was given in a table at verse 140; this is the fourth link of that chain.

### ٱلْحَقُّ مِن رَّبِّكَ فَلَا تَكُونَنَّ مِنَ ٱلْمُمْتَرِينَ

**ٱلْحَقُّ مِن رَّبِّكَ** — the sentence is nominal, and the predicate is attached directly to the subject: "the truth is from your Lord." No verb, no tense — a state is stated.

**مُمْتَرِين** — root **م-ر-ي**. The lexicographers record the concrete sense of the root as the **repeated stroking of an animal's udder** to draw milk (*marā'l-nāqata*); *mirāʾ* and *imtirāʾ* come from here: to keep picking at a matter, to wrangle, to fall into doubt. **I transmit this connection as the lexicographers make it.**

The pattern is Form VIII (*iftiʿāl*), and in Arabic this form generally adds an element of **effort and intention** to the verb. *Imtirāʾ*, that is, is not a hesitation that comes of itself; it is doubt gone after, picked at.

And this forms a ring with the second verse of the sūra: there it was said of the book *lā rayba fīh* — "there is no doubt in it". A hundred and forty-five verses later the same idea comes, this time as a clause turned towards the addressee: if there is no doubt in the text, doubt must not be produced in the reader.

**That the warning is again directed at the Prophet** is a repetition of the pattern at verse 145. In this stretch of the sūra the measure is continually applied to one's own side — after the attitude of the other side is recounted, it is recorded that the same risk exists for oneself.

---

## 2/148 — "Race one another to good things"

*"Everyone has a direction to which he turns. So **race one another to good things.**"*

This sentence is the close of the dispute about the qibla, and it carries the dispute somewhere entirely different.

**فَٱسْتَبِقُوا۟ ٱلْخَيْرَٰتِ** — root *s-b-q*: **to get ahead in a race.** It is the word used for horse-racing; *sābiq* (the one in front) and *musābaqa* are from the same root.

We had seen two races earlier in this commentary; one was criticised, the other commanded:

| | The subject of the race | The ruling |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Takāthur 102/1** — *takāthur* | To outdo in quantity | Criticised: "it has distracted you" |
| **al-Muṭaffifīn 83/26** — *tanāfus* | To compete over the drink of the garden | Commanded: "let the competitors compete" |
| **al-Baqara 2/148** — *istibāq* | To get ahead in good things | Commanded |

The Qurʾān does not forbid racing; it changes **the subject of the race**. The desire to get ahead in a person is treated not as a fault to be torn out but as an energy whose direction is to be fixed.

And the difference between the two kinds of race lies in **whether the subject is divisible**. Property, position, number — these are limited; one person's winning means another's losing, and so the race produces conflict. But good is not limited; one person's doing much good does not reduce another's share. The same act, according to its subject, produces either conflict or improvement.

That this sentence is placed at the end of the discussion of the qibla is meaningful too. The dispute had been about "who turns in the right direction"; the closing sentence turns the dispute from direction to **deed**.

---

## 2/149-151 — The same command repeated three times

*"From wherever you set out, turn your face towards the Sacred Mosque. This is the truth from your Lord. God is not heedless of what you do.
