From wherever you set out, turn your face towards the Sacred Mosque. And wherever you are, turn your faces towards it — so that people may have no argument against you. Except those of them who do wrong; do not fear them, fear Me. And that I may complete My favour upon you, and that you may be guided. Just as We have sent among you a messenger from yourselves, who recites Our verses to you, purifies you, teaches you the Book and wisdom, and teaches you what you did not know."*

### The command is given three times

The command about the qibla is repeated in **three separate verses** in this sūra. Repetition is not rare in the Qurʾān; but what is repeated here is almost exactly the same wording, and **the ground changes each time.**

| Verse | The wording of the command | The ground | The kind of ground |
|---|---|---|---|
| **144** | *fa-walli wajhaka shaṭra'l-masjidi'l-ḥarām* | *qiblatan **tarḍāhā*** — "a qibla that will please you" | **Personal** — the expectation of the one addressed |
| **149** | *wa min ḥaythu kharajta fa-walli wajhaka shaṭra'l-masjidi'l-ḥarām* | *wa innahū **la'l-ḥaqqu min rabbik*** — "it is the truth from your Lord" | **Truth** — the rightness of the ruling itself |
| **150** | The same sentence + *wa ḥaythu mā kuntum fa-wallū wujūhakum shaṭrah* | *li-allā yakūna li'n-nāsi ʿalaykum **ḥujja*** and *li-**utimma niʿmatī*** | **Social and historical** — the closing of the dispute, the completion of the favour |

The three grounds do not cancel one another; they **lie one on top of another.** And their order is meaningful: it moves from the inside outward.

- At 144 the ground is the expectation of **one person**.
- At 149 the ground is **the ruling itself**; nothing belonging to a person is named.
- At 150 the ground is **the community's relation with those opposite it**.

**I record this as my own reading:** the reason for the repetition is not emphasis but **the widening of the ground**. Why a command is given is not told with a single answer; the same command is justified at three separate levels. And this is also a measure of how unsettling the change of qibla was — the sūra does not leave this ruling on its own.

**شَطْر** — root **ش-ط-ر**: the **half** of a thing, one of its sides, a quarter. The word states not "the exact point" but **direction**. This matters for the workability of the ruling: what is asked is not the calculation of the Kaʿba's exact point from everywhere on earth but a turning **towards that side**.

**وَمِنْ حَيْثُ خَرَجْتَ** — "from wherever you set out." *Ḥaythu* is an adverb of place and generalises. Verse 149 addresses a singular hearer (*kharajta*), the second sentence of 150 a plural one (*kuntum*). The command, that is, opens first to the person and then to the community — here too there is a widening from the inside outward.

### لِئَلَّا يَكُونَ لِلنَّاسِ عَلَيْكُمْ حُجَّةٌ

This ground given at verse 150 is one of the most practical sentences in the sūra.

**حُجَّة** — root **ح-ج-ج**: a proof, the support advanced in a dispute. The word had occurred in verbal form at verse 139 (*a-tuḥājjūnanā*).

What is said is this: if the ruling is not complied with, the other side will be left with **a usable objection**. One function of the ruling, that is, is to remove the ground that feeds the dispute.

**This is something rarely seen in the ground given for a religious practice:** the rule is justified not only by its own inherent value but by **the closing of the dispute it would produce**.

**إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ظَلَمُوا۟ مِنْهُمْ** — "except those of them who do wrong." The exception is realistic: not every objection can be closed. For the obstinate, proof is not decisive in any case — this had already been recorded at verse 145 with *"even if you brought every kind of proof"*.

**فَلَا تَخْشَوْهُمْ وَٱخْشَوْنِى** — "do not fear them, fear Me." The same verb is used in two sentences with its direction changed. The root **خ-ش-ي** in Arabic conveys a shrinking that rests on knowledge; the word is not a terror but **a wariness born of knowing the greatness of the one before you**.

The function of the sentence is not to lay down a prohibition but to **shift a place**: fear is not removed, its address is changed.

### وَلِأُتِمَّ نِعْمَتِى عَلَيْكُمْ

**تَمَام** — a thing's having nothing left wanting. The verb is in Form IV: *itmām* — to complete.

The word's use here forms a link with the Abraham section of the sūra. At verses 127-129 Abraham and Ishmael had prayed while raising the foundations of the Kaʿba; the last of the prayers was *"send them a messenger from among themselves"*. Now the same direction is made the qibla, and this is called **the completion of the favour**.

The change of qibla, then, is not merely a preference of direction; it is presented as the closing of a line the sūra has already recounted.

### كَمَآ أَرْسَلْنَا فِيكُمْ رَسُولًا مِّنكُمْ

Verse 151 begins with **كَمَا**, and this particle attaches the sentence to the previous one: the completion of the favour is **likened** to the sending of the messenger, or counted its continuation. The two favours, that is, are on the same line.

And the messenger's work is enumerated in four verbs: *yatlū* (he recites), *yuzakkī* (he purifies), *yuʿallimu'l-kitāba wa'l-ḥikma* (he teaches the Book and wisdom), *yuʿallimukum mā lam takūnū taʿlamūn* (he teaches you what you did not know).

This foursome and its comparison with the other three occurrences in the Qurʾān were treated in detail in the discussion of **`062-cuma.md`** (62/2); I do not repeat it. The summary of what was recorded there:

- **ت-ل-و** — *tilāwa* is not to read but **to go after**.
- **ز-ك-و** — *tazkiya* is not only to cleanse; in the root, **cleanness and growth** are present together.
- **ك-ت-ب** and **ح-ك-م** — one is "what has been bound", the other "what binds".

Here I add only two notes belonging to this sūra.

**First: a difference of order.** The same foursome had occurred at verse 129 of the sūra as Abraham's prayer, and there the order is different:

| | Order |
|---|---|
| **2/129** — Abraham's **prayer** | *yatlū* → *yuʿallimu* → **yuzakkī** |
| **2/151** — the **sending** as it happened | *yatlū* → **yuzakkī** → *yuʿallimu* |

This comparison was given in `062-cuma.md` in a table; I do not repeat the finding there, and I build no definite ruling upon this difference.

**Second: the fourth verb.** At verse 151 there is a sentence not found in the other occurrences: **وَيُعَلِّمُكُم مَّا لَمْ تَكُونُوا۟ تَعْلَمُونَ** — *"and he teaches you what you did not know."*

The function of the sentence is worth recording. The preceding verb already said "he teaches"; this second teaching is distinguished **by the source of what is taught**: the Book and wisdom are taught, but beyond this something is taught as well that they **could not have reached on their own**.

This sentence looks the same way as verses 31-33 of the sūra: there the names were taught to Adam, and the angels' knowledge was bounded by the clause *"we know nothing but what You have taught us"*. Here the same boundary is set for the human being, and it is passed in the same way: **by being taught.**

**فِيكُمْ رَسُولًا مِّنكُمْ** — "a messenger among you, from yourselves." Two clauses one upon the other: he is both among them and of them. The detailed analysis of this expression was likewise made in `062-cuma.md`; the finding there holds here too.

---

## 2/152 — "Remember Me and I shall remember you"

*"So remember Me and I shall remember you. Give thanks to Me and do not cover over what I have given."*

A short verse setting up two reciprocities.

**فَٱذْكُرُونِىٓ أَذْكُرْكُمْ** — "remember Me and I shall remember you". This is the same as the structure of the covenant at verse 40 of the sūra: *"fulfil My covenant and I shall fulfil yours."* A two-sided bond, again set up in the servant's favour — because the servant's remembering is limited and its return is not.

**وَٱشْكُرُوا۟ لِى وَلَا تَكْفُرُونِ** — and here the contrast we set up at the sixth verse passes openly into the text: **thanks** and **unbelief** are set opposite one another.

There I said that the root sense of unbelief is "to cover", and that its opposite is most often not faith but thanks. This verse confirms that finding: there is no word for faith in the sentence; unbelief is set directly opposite thanks.

The definition that follows is this: **unbelief is not to see what has been given; thanks is to see it and acknowledge it.** The difference between the two is not an article of belief but a **direction of looking**.

---

# 2/153-177 — Affliction, love and the definition of goodness

## 2/153 — The same command coming a second time

*"You who believe! Seek help through patience and prayer."*

This sentence occurs in the sūra for the **second time**; the first was at verse 45. But there is a difference, and the difference is in the one addressed:

| | Addressee |
|---|---|
| **2/45** | The Children of Israel — inside the address *"Children of Israel"* |
| **2/153** | *"You who believe"* |

The same counsel, that is, is first transmitted as given to the old community, and then given directly to the new one. An instance of what the sūra has been doing from the beginning: the history recounted turns into a warning belonging to the reader.

And the point at which it is placed matters. The previous section recounted the change of qibla, that is, the testing of a community. Immediately afterwards the verses of hardship will come. The command about patience and prayer stands right in the middle — not before the test and not after it, but **in between.**

---

## 2/154 — "Do not say 'dead'"

*"Do not say of those killed in God's way, 'they are dead'. Rather, they are alive; but you do not perceive it."*

### The prohibition is laid on the word, not on the belief

**وَلَا تَقُولُوا۟** — "do not say."

This is the first striking side of the sentence. The verse does not say "do not believe this" or "do not think this"; it forbids **saying** it.

**I record this as my own reading,** because it fits the sūra's stance on language: at verse 104 too a word was forbidden (*rāʿinā*) and another word proposed in its place. There too intention was not examined; the expression was changed.

The logic here may be this: how a community names its loss determines how it carries that loss. To say "he died" states an ending; and a word that states an ending shapes the community's stance as it is repeated. The verse intervenes in the naming.

**لِمَن يُقْتَلُ** — the verb is in the **imperfect**: "the one who is killed" — not one killed in the past but one being killed and one who will be killed. The ruling is laid not on a past event but on **an ongoing condition**.

And the verb is **passive** (*yuqtalu*): the killing side is not named. What the sentence is concerned with is not the agent but the condition of the one killed.

### أَمْوَٰتٌۢ — بَلْ أَحْيَآءٌ

Both words are plural, both indefinite, and both are **nouns**; no verb is used.

The particle **بَلْ**, as we saw at verse 135, is a particle that corrects the preceding expression — "no, not that; this". The sentence, that is, does not make an addition; it makes a **correction**.

And the form of the correction is absolute: not "they are not to be counted dead" or "they are not like the dead", but directly **"they are alive."** No intermediate expression is chosen.

The same ruling occurs once more in the Qurʾān, in a more detailed form: *"Do not suppose those killed in God's way to be dead; rather they are alive, provided for with their Lord"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/169).

**The difference between the two verses is worth recording:**

| | The end of the sentence |
|---|---|
| **al-Baqara 2/154** | *wa lākin lā tashʿurūn* — "but you do not perceive it" |
| **Āl ʿImrān 3/169** | *ʿinda rabbihim yurzaqūn* — "they are provided for with their Lord" |

One states **the limit of knowledge**, the other gives **a part of the knowledge**. The verse in al-Baqara breaks off without saying anything about the character of that life.

**In this commentary I keep to that limit:** I do not say what the text does not say about what kind of life this is, where and in what form it continues. Various explanations on this are found in the classical commentaries; I do not transmit them as certain knowledge.

### وَلَٰكِن لَّا تَشْعُرُونَ

The root **ش-ع-ر** has been treated in several sections of this commentary (al-Ḥāqqa, al-Ḥujurāt, al-Ṭūr, al-Najm); I do not repeat it. Its summary: the root is **to perceive something fine**, and the lexicographers relate it to *shaʿr* (hair) — to notice what is as fine as a hair. *Shuʿūr* and *shāʿir* are from the same root.

The placing of this word here determines the sentence as a whole. The verse does not say "you do not know" (*lā taʿlamūn*); it says **"you do not perceive"**. What is at issue, that is, is not the lack of a piece of knowledge that could be learned; it is something **outside the range of perception**.

And this is not set down as a fault. The particle **وَلَٰكِن** (but) at the head of the clause is a qualifier: it does not remove the ruling, it adds a clause to it. "They are alive — but this is not something your senses can reach."

**I record this as my own reading:** in changing the meaning of a loss, the verse does not deny that the loss is **felt**. For the one left behind, what appears is a death, and it will go on appearing so. What changes is whether what appears is the last word.

### Where the verse stands

Where this verse is placed cannot be separated from its content.

- **Verse 153:** *"Seek help through patience and prayer."*
- **Verse 154:** the heaviest loss — life — is taken up.
- **Verse 155:** the list of the testing will be enumerated: fear, hunger, loss of property, of **lives** and of fruits.

What will be counted as one item at 155, "loss of lives", has thus been taken up **separately** one verse earlier. Before the list arrives, a clause is entered about the heaviest item in the list.

**And the order is built in reverse:** normally the loss is enumerated first and the consolation comes after. Here the consolation comes **before** the loss is enumerated. At verse 153 of the sūra it was recorded that the command about patience stands neither before the testing nor after it but exactly **in between**; the same logic of placement is at work here.

And the sentence that will be asked for at verse 156 — *innā li'llāhi wa innā ilayhi rājiʿūn* — must be read together with this verse: there it is said that what is lost was never the person's own, here that what is supposed lost has not been lost. The two do the same work from two different places.

---

## 2/155-157 — Five losses and two sentences

*"We shall surely test you with something of fear and hunger, and with loss of property, lives and fruits. And give good news to the patient."*

### The list of the testing

Five things are listed: **fear, hunger, loss of property, loss of life, loss of fruits.**

The list is not abstract; it is the order of things a community can concretely undergo. And there is a detail that must be recorded: **"something of"** (*bi-shayʾin min*). The verse sets a limit at the outset on the amount of the testing.

The root **بَلَاء** — as we saw at verse 49, *b-l-w*: to test by wearing down. The Qurʾān said elsewhere that this is not one-directional: *"We test you with evil and with good"* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/35). The list here enumerates the bad ones; but as is seen in Sūrat al-Fajr, abundance is named with the same verb.

### مُصِيبَة — affliction

Root *ṣ-w-b*: **to hit the mark, to find the target.** This root has occurred at two earlier places in this commentary:

- **al-Baqara 2/125** — *mathāba*: the place returned to
- At **the nineteenth verse of this sūra** — *ṣayyib*: the downpour that finds its target

From the same root come *iṣāba* and *ṣawāb* (right). A *muṣība*, then, in the word's own logic, is **"what hits"** — not what falls at random but what finds its target. The word says from the outset that there is a direction inside the event.

### إِنَّا لِلَّهِ وَإِنَّآ إِلَيْهِ رَٰجِعُونَ

The sentence to be said at the moment of affliction contains two distinct propositions, and the two do distinct work:

**"We belong to God."** — This is a declaration of **ownership**. What is lost — property, life, fruits — was never the person's own. The loss is not the passing away of something possessed; it is the taking back of a trust. This is in the same line as the clause *"of what We have provided them"* at the third verse of the sūra.

**"And to Him we are returning."** — This is a declaration of **direction**. The loss is not the end of the story; the story itself is going somewhere else.

Together the two sentences do not deny the pain. The verse does not say "do not grieve"; grieving is indeed not a forbidden thing. What is said is this: if it is known what has been lost and where one is going, the loss ceases to be **meaningless**. The pain is not lifted; it is saved from being without ground.

**وَبَشِّرِ ٱلصَّٰبِرِينَ** — "and give good news to the patient". We saw at al-Baqara 2/25 that the root of the word for good news is *bashara* (skin), that is, that good news is seen in the face. Here good news is given to a condition that as yet has no visible outcome.

---

## 2/158 — The lifting of a scruple

*"Al-Ṣafā and al-Marwa are among God's signs. Whoever makes the pilgrimage to the House or performs the ʿumra, there is **no fault** in his going round the two."*

The expression is strange: one does not expect "there is no fault" to be said of an act of worship. A command or an encouragement would be expected.

The reason transmitted is this: because in the age of ignorance there were idols on these two hills, the Muslims had been reluctant to go back and forth between them. The scruple, that is, **arose out of piety** — not wanting to stand in a place tainted by association of partners with God.

The verse's answer does not condemn the scruple; it **lifts** it. And in doing so it sets a measure: that a place or a practice has been misused in the past does not permanently defile it. What was defiled was the thing done in that place; not the place.

This is among the rare verses showing that excessive scrupulousness can also be a deviation. At verse 143 of the sūra it had said "a middle community" — to stand clear of two extremes. If one of the extremes is laxity, the other is needless rigidity.

---

## 2/159-160 — The making good of concealment

*"Those who conceal the clear proofs and the guidance We have sent down, after We have made them clear to people in the Book — those God curses… Except those who repent, put things right and **make clear**."*

An addition comes to the subject of "concealing knowledge" treated at verses 75-79: **what makes it good?**

Three conditions are listed, and the third is striking:

1. **Repentance** — turning back (inward)
2. **Putting right** — correcting (conduct)
3. **Bayān** — **making clear** (turned to the public)

The third is a condition laid down specifically for this kind of sin. The making good of concealment is not to give up concealing; it is **to say it openly**. For although concealment looks like an inaction, its result is an action: a false conviction has formed on the other side, and that conviction is not corrected by silence.

This principle is general: someone who has spread a piece of false information removes his responsibility not by keeping silent but by correcting it with the same reach.

---

## 2/161-162 — The moment the door closes

*"Those who cover over the truth and die as unbelievers — upon them is the curse of God, of the angels and of all people. They abide in it; their punishment is not lightened, nor are they granted respite."*

### The term of the exception

These two verses are the continuation of the previous two and must not be read without them.

At verse 159 a curse was recorded for those who conceal, and at verse 160 an **exception** was brought: *"Except those who repent, put things right and make clear."* The door had been left open.

Verse 161 says **when** that door closes: **وَمَاتُوا۟ وَهُمْ كُفَّارٌ** — "and they died as unbelievers."

The word order is built accordingly. The sentence uses two verbs: *kafarū* (they covered over) and *mātū* (they died). Without the second the ruling is not settled. And a **circumstantial clause** is added to the second verb — *wa hum kuffār*, "while they were unbelievers" — that is, the state at the moment of death is recorded.

**This is the line the sūra follows in establishing a ruling:** what is taken as the basis is not a person's state at some moment but his **final state**. At verse 160 the road of return had been opened; here it is said that that road has a term.

### لَعْنَة

The root **ل-ع-ن**: **to drive off, to put at a distance.** The lexicographers explain the word with *ṭard* and *ibʿād* — to drive someone from one's side, to cast him far off. In Turkish the borrowed *lânet*, and in English the word "curse", both add a suggestion of imprecation; whereas the central idea in Arabic is **driving away**.

This difference matters. A curse states not an insult but a **position**: being outside the reach of mercy.

**وَٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةِ وَٱلنَّاسِ أَجْمَعِينَ** — "of the angels and of **all** people."

The threefold sequence is striking, and the last is surprising: **all** people. Yet the persons described have partisans, fellow townsmen and partners in interest in this world.

In the classical commentaries this expression is generally explained in two ways: either what is meant is that on the day of resurrection everyone will unite in the same judgement, or the expression "all people" is a widespread **general expression** in Arabic. **I do not impose a preference between the two.**

What can be recorded is this: the sentence does not take into account a person's supporters in this world. The four negations enumerated at verse 48 of the sūra — no soul can pay for another, no intercession is accepted, no ransom is taken, they are not helped — work here once more, in different words.

### خَٰلِدِينَ فِيهَا

The root **خ-ل-د**: to remain a long time, to be lasting. The lexicographers record that the root carries the idea of "remaining unchanged" — in Arabic it is used of what does not grow old, whose state does not deteriorate.

**A note on the word order:** the pronoun *fīhā* is feminine, and the nearest feminine word is **curse**. The sentence therefore says literally "they abide within the curse". Some exegetes attach the pronoun to the **fire** or the **punishment** understood from the context. Both readings are transmitted; **I state no preference.** But there is something worth recording in the first reading: what is abided in comes to be named not as a place but as a **state**.

### لَا يُخَفَّفُ عَنْهُمُ ٱلْعَذَابُ وَلَا هُمْ يُنظَرُونَ

Two negations close off two different expectations:

**First, lightening.** *Takhfīf* — the reducing of a burden. What is closed off is not the removal of the punishment but **the lowering of its degree**.

**Second, respite.** **لَا هُمْ يُنظَرُونَ** — root **ن-ظ-ر**: to look; in Form IV (*anẓara*) it means **to grant a term, to wait**. The lexicographers make the connection thus: to give someone a respite is to "look" to him, that is, to watch over his affair for a time.

**And this word occurs once more in the same sūra, in exactly the opposite direction:**

> **فَنَظِرَةٌ إِلَىٰ مَيْسَرَةٍ** — *"if he is in difficulty, then **a respite** until ease"* (2/280)

The same root, the same sūra, two opposite rulings. For the debtor a respite is commanded **in this world**; here it is said that respite **ends at some point**.

**I record this as my own reading:** read together, the two verses yield a balance. Respite is what a person owes to a person; and it is valuable precisely because it is not endless. On the one hand the sūra commands postponement, on the other it recalls that postponement has an end.

And this measure is consistent with verse 160 itself: there the door of return was opened; here it is said that that door has a time. The two do not conflict — one states the possibility, the other the term of the possibility.

---

## 2/163-164 — Eight signs

*"Your God is one God… In the creation of the heavens and the earth, in the alternation of night and day, in the ships that sail the sea carrying what profits people, in the water God sends down from the sky… there are signs for a people who use their reason."*

The claim of tawḥīd is set out and immediately afterwards the **proof** is enumerated. The common feature of the eight things listed is striking: all of them are **ordinary**.

The sky, day and night, the ship, rain, plants, animals, the wind, the cloud. None of them is extraordinary; they are things seen every day. The verse does not offer a miracle as proof — it offers the familiar.

The logic of this is: habit stops seeing. A person who sees a thing every day no longer notices it; the extraordinary, on the other hand, draws attention but is not repeated. The Qurʾān insistently points not to the second but to the first — because the real issue is not seeing something new but **being able to look at what is seen**.

**لِّقَوْمٍ يَعْقِلُونَ** — "for a people who use their reason". At verse 44 we saw that the root *ʿaql* means "to bind". The use here completes that sense: the signs stand before everyone; they become signs for the one who can **bind** them to one another.

---

## 2/165 — Association of partners defined by love

*"Among the people are some who take equals besides God, loving them as God is loved. But those who believe are **stronger** in love for God."*

The verse describes association of partners not as an error of belief but as a **matter of love**. The criterion is not "whose existence do you believe in" but "what do you love, and how much".

We saw the word **أَنْدَاد** at verse 22: **an equal, a match.** *Shirk* is not to refuse God; it is to set something on the same line with Him. And here the measure of that line is given: **love.**

The comparison is built accordingly. The verse does not say of the believers "they believe more correctly"; it says **"their love is stronger"**. The difference between the two sides, that is, is not in knowledge but in the degree of attachment.

The practical consequence of this is heavy: the way to learn what a person worships is not to ask what he believes but to look at **what he cannot bear to lose**. If losing something weighs heavier than losing God's good pleasure, the line has been drawn.

---

## 2/166-169 — The ties that snap and the footsteps of Satan

*"When those who were followed disown those who followed them; when they see the punishment and the ties between them are cut… Those who followed say: 'If only we had a return, that we might disown them as they have disowned us.' Thus does God show them their deeds as regrets for them. And they will not come out of the fire.*

*O people! Eat of what is lawful and good on the earth; and do not follow the footsteps of Satan. He is a plain enemy to you. He commands you only evil and indecency, and that you say about God what you do not know."*

### The link with 165: the undoing of love

At verse 165 association of partners was described not as an error of belief but as a **matter of love**: *"loving them as God is loved."*

Verse 166 shows the **end** of that love. And the manner of the showing is striking: it is not said that the love was mistaken; it is shown that it was **unreturned**.

There are two sides in the scene, and the act is not mutual but one-directional: **those who were followed** disown those who followed them. The attachment turns out to have been one-sided.

**تَبَرَّأَ** — root **ب-ر-أ**: to **strip oneself of, to separate from, to be cleared.** From the same root come *barāʾa* (acquittal) and *Bāriʾ* (the one who brings into being from nothing — who separates from matter and gives form). The root has occurred in several sections of this commentary; the pattern here is **Form V** and states an **effort**: to separate oneself, to cut the relation deliberately.

The passive–active arrangement of the verbs also builds the scene: *alladhīna **uttubiʿū*** (those who were followed — passive) and *alladhīna **ittabaʿū*** (those who followed — active). One side, that is, is in a passive position: it was followed, it was not the one who wanted to follow. This also explains the disowning that comes after.

### وَتَقَطَّعَتْ بِهِمُ ٱلْأَسْبَابُ

**أَسْبَاب** — the plural of the root **س-ب-ب**. And the concrete sense of the root separates the word from its present-day use: a *sabab* is first of all a **rope**.

More particularly: the **cable** used for going down into a well or climbing up somewhere. The Qurʾān confirms this sense itself: *"Then let him stretch a **rope** to the sky, then let him cut it"* (al-Ḥajj 22/15) — here the word can be nothing but a rope thrown to a ceiling.

The abstract sense — the reason for a thing — is derived from this concrete one; Turkish has borrowed the word in exactly that abstract sense (*sebep*). A cause is the **rope** that binds to a result. Arabic, that is, has pictured causality as a bond — as English does when it speaks of a causal *tie* or of the *thread* of an argument.

Now return to the sentence in the verse: **taqaṭṭaʿat bihimu'l-asbāb** — "the ties, the ropes, were cut with them."

**تَقَطَّعَتْ** — root **ق-ط-ع**: to cut. The pattern is again **Form V** and here states **multiplicity and repetition**: not breaking at one point but breaking **in pieces, at every point**.

The picture is complete: at the edge of a cliff, all the cables they were holding snapping at once. And from whom were the ropes cut? From both sides: the rope binding the followers to those they followed, and the rope that would save those followed from the reckoning.

**I record this as my own reading:** the two senses of the word work together here. What is cut is both **the connection** (the relationship) and **the reason**. Both the person one held on to and the ground on which one could say "this was why I acted so" go at the same time.

### لَوْ أَنَّ لَنَا كَرَّةً

**كَرَّة** — root **ك-ر-ر**. This root was treated in the discussions of al-Mulk (67/4) and al-Nāziʿāt (79/13); I do not repeat it. Its summary: the root's proper place is the battlefield — in the tactic of *karr wa farr* (charge and withdraw), **to turn to the attack again** after withdrawing. From this the sense "a time, an occasion" arose.

What is wanted, then, is not simply "to go back"; in the word's own logic it is **the chance to repeat a charge**. To try once more.

The particle **لَوْ** is used here for **wishing the impossible**. In Arabic *law* states an unrealised condition; when it comes with a wish it also carries within it the wish's being without return.

**And the content of the wish is worth recording.** What is wanted is not to believe, to put things right, to make amends: *"that we might disown them."* What is wanted, that is, is **to be able to do the same thing to the other side.**

**I record this as an observation:** the wish shows that the attitude has not changed. The regret is felt not for the thing done but **for the other side's having acted first**.

### أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ حَسَرَٰتٍ عَلَيْهِمْ

**حَسْرَة** — root **ح-س-ر**. This root was treated in the discussions of al-Mulk (67/4) and al-Ḥāqqa; I do not repeat it. Its summary: the idea beneath the root is **opening, stripping, the taking off of what is on top** — the collapse of an exhausted animal and the lifting of a covering are told with the same root. *Ḥasra* is therefore not longing but **regret with its covering removed**: the truth appearing naked.

The word order of the verse uses this sense exactly: **يُرِيهِمُ ٱللَّهُ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ حَسَرَٰتٍ** — "God shows them **their deeds** as regrets."

What is shown is not a punishment but **their own works**. The torment, that is, is not brought as something separate; the covering over the deed is lifted and the deed is seen **as it is**. No transformation between deed and regret is recounted; it is said that the two are the same thing.

And the word is **plural** (*ḥasarāt*): not a single regret but one for every deed.

### كُلُوا۟ مِمَّا فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ حَلَٰلًا طَيِّبًا

At verse 168 the address changes: **يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ** — "O people."

This is a widening that stands out in this stretch of the sūra. After the matter of association of partners and attachment has been discussed, the first ruling about food is given to **all people**. And four verses later the same command will be repeated, this time to believers alone:

| Verse | Address | Command |
|---|---|---|
| **2/168** | *yā ayyuha'l-nās* | *kulū mimmā fi'l-arḍi **ḥalālan ṭayyiban*** |
| **2/172** | *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* | *kulū min **ṭayyibāti** mā razaqnākum* |

In the second the clause **ḥalāl** has dropped and only **ṭayyib** remains. This difference will be taken up at verse 172.

**حَلَال** — root **ح-ل-ل**: **to untie, to open a knot;** from the same root come *ḥall* (a solution) and *ḥalāl* (the unloading of a burden in order to halt). *Ḥalāl*, in the word's own logic, is **that whose knot has been untied**. Its opposite is *ḥarām*: what has been tied, made inviolable.

**طَيِّب** — root **ط-ي-ب**: pleasant, clean, good. The word covers both what is pleasant to the senses and what is clean in its own substance.

The two adjectives together set two distinct measures: one looks to the **ruling** (is it permitted?), the other to the **quality** (is it good?). That a thing is not forbidden does not mean that it is good.

**And the command itself is worth recording:** the sentence begins not with a prohibition but with a **permission**. What is on the earth is left free; the boundary comes afterwards. This is in the same line as verse 29 of the sūra: *"He created for you everything on the earth."*

### خُطُوَٰتِ ٱلشَّيْطَٰن

The root **خ-ط-و**: **to take a step.** *Khuṭwa* — the distance of one step; the space between two feet.

This expression occurs **twice** in the sūra: here and at verse 208. The finding recorded at 208 holds here too, and its proper place is here: **the word is not "leap" but "step".** A road is covered not by a single decision but by small steps following one another.

**That the word comes in the plural reinforces this:** *khuṭuwāt* — steps. Not a single step is named; a **series** is named. And each step looks reasonable if the one before it has been taken.

**I record this as my own reading:** the placing of the prohibition on the steps belongs to the same family as *"do not approach the tree"* at verse 35 of the sūra and *"do not approach the bounds"* at verse 187. In all three the prohibition begins not with the act itself but **with the distance that leads to it**.

And a detail: the verb is *lā tattabiʿū* — "do not follow", that is, **do not become a follower.** The same root had been used two verses earlier (166-167) for those who followed and those who were followed. Here the sūra turns the same word into a warning: do not enter the relation of following whose end you have just seen.

### إِنَّمَا يَأْمُرُكُم بِٱلسُّوٓءِ وَٱلْفَحْشَآءِ

Verse 169 enumerates in three items where the "steps" come out, and the order grows heavier.

**ٱلسُّوٓء** — root **س-و-أ**: to be bad, to become ugly. It is a general word; it covers evil of every kind.

**ٱلْفَحْشَآء** — root **ف-ح-ش**. Treated in the discussion of al-Najm: *fuḥsh* — **an ugliness that passes the bound, excess**; a *fāḥisha* is a deed whose ugliness is open. The second item is thus a heavier degree of the first: evil in its state of having passed the measure.

**وَأَن تَقُولُوا۟ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ** — *"and that you say about God what you do not know."*

The third item differs **in kind** from the first two. The first two have to do with conduct; the third with **speech.** And its being placed last in the list is in Arabic generally the mark of the heaviest.

This item is the name of an act the sūra has described at three earlier places:

| Verse | The act |
|---|---|
| **2/78** | Speaking about the Book without knowing, on wishful fancy |
| **2/79** | Writing by hand and saying *"this is from God"* |
| **2/140** | *"Do you know better, or God?"* |
| **2/169** | Saying about God what one **does not know** |

All four recount the same thing: presenting a word whose source is oneself as though its source were God.

**And its being counted among the commands of Satan fixes its position.** The verse places this act not as an error of knowledge but as the furthest item of evil. The rule of method laid down at the very beginning of this commentary — not to ascribe to a source what one is not sure of — finds its ground here.

---

## 2/170-171 — The forefathers and the shepherd's voice

*"When they are told 'follow what God has sent down', they say, 'No, we follow what we found our forefathers upon'. **Even if their forefathers understood nothing and were not on the right way?**"*

The objection advances no proof; it advances a **continuity**. Not "it is true" but "this is how we came".

The verse's answer is likewise not a counter-claim but a **question**: and what if they did not know either? The question shows that a tradition is not self-verifying. That a practice is old is no proof that it is right — because what is wrong can also grow old.

Care is needed here: the verse does not reject tradition, it rejects **tradition's being counted beyond question**. Indeed the same Qurʾān praises attachment to the inheritance of Abraham and commands following the chain of prophets. The difference is this: the reason for following that chain is not that it is old but that it is **right**. The measure stays the same; what changes is where the measure is applied.

**The comparison:** *"The case of those who cover over is like the case of one who calls out to animals that hear nothing but a shout and a cry."*

The picture is this: a shepherd calls to the flock. The sound reaches the animal — the ear is sound, the sound has been heard. But what reaches it is only **the sound**; not the meaning inside it.

This comes out at the same place as the tableau "deaf, dumb, blind" at verse 18 of the sūra: the organs work, the function is missing. And the detail here is sharper — the trouble is not not hearing but **not understanding what is heard**.

---

## 2/172-173 — What is forbidden to eat

*"You who believe! Eat of the good things We have provided you, and give thanks to God — if it is Him alone you serve. He has forbidden you only carrion, blood, the flesh of swine and what has been dedicated to other than God. But whoever is compelled — without desiring it and without exceeding the limit — there is no sin upon him. God is forgiving, merciful."*

### The command coming a second time, narrowed

At verse 168 the same command had been given to all people: *"eat of what is **lawful and good** on the earth."* Here the command is repeated to the believers and **one adjective drops:**

| | Address | Clause |
|---|---|---|
| **2/168** | *yā ayyuha'l-nās* | *ḥalālan **ṭayyiban*** — both lawful and good |
| **2/172** | *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* | *min **ṭayyibāti** mā razaqnākum* — of the good things |

**I record this as my own reading:** the dropping of the clause "lawful" is not a loosening but an **assumption**. For those who believe, the distinction of lawful and forbidden is a subject already discussed; what needs recalling is that within the permitted field too there is a choice. The second command, that is, goes a step further: not everything that is not forbidden is equal.

**مَا رَزَقْنَٰكُمْ** — and the source of the provision is recorded at once. This expression had occurred in the same form at the third verse of the sūra: *"they spend of what We have provided them."* In both places ownership is attached not to the eater or the giver but **to the one who gives the provision**.

**وَٱشْكُرُوا۟ لِلَّهِ** — the second half of the command. At verse 152 we recorded that *thanks* and *unbelief* are set opposite one another, and that the root sense of unbelief is "to cover". Here the same measure is applied to eating: eating is set down as an occasion for what is given to be seen.

**إِن كُنتُمْ إِيَّاهُ تَعْبُدُونَ** — "if it is Him alone you serve."

**A point of word order:** in the sentence the object (*iyyāhu*) is brought **before** the verb. In Arabic bringing the object forward conveys **restriction** — the same pattern as *iyyāka naʿbudu* in al-Fātiḥa. The sentence therefore means not "if you serve Him" but **"if it is Him alone you serve"**.

And the placing of the conditional clause here prepares the next verse: the fourth of the things forbidden has to do precisely with the breaking of that restriction.

### إِنَّمَا حَرَّمَ — four items

The particle **إِنَّمَا** in Arabic conveys **qaṣr** (restriction): "only, no more than". The sentence, that is, does not merely give a list; it says that the list is **closed**.

Its function within the context is plain. The previous section of the sūra (2/170-171) had criticised practices taken over from forefathers; in the Arabia of the age of ignorance there were many settled prohibitions about which animal might or might not be eaten. This sentence sets against all those prohibitions a **closed list of four items**.

| Forbidden | The word | Clause |
|---|---|---|
| **Carrion** | *al-mayta* | An animal that dies without being slaughtered |
| **Blood** | *al-dam* | At al-Anʿām 6/145 it occurs with the clause *masfūḥan* — "poured out" |
| **The flesh of swine** | *laḥma'l-khinzīr* | The flesh itself is named |
| **What is slaughtered in the name of other than God** | *mā uhilla bihī li-ghayri'llāh* | Below |

The same four items are enumerated at several other places in the Qurʾān (al-Anʿām 6/145; al-Naḥl 16/115), and at al-Māʾida 5/3 the list is repeated in more detail. **In this commentary I do not enter into their legal scope, their exceptions or the views of the schools of law;** the subject belongs to the field of law, and what is dwelt on here is the language of the verse.

### مَآ أُهِلَّ بِهِۦ لِغَيْرِ ٱللَّهِ

The fourth item **differs in kind** from the others, and this difference must be recorded.

The first three look to the **substance**: carrion, blood, the flesh of swine — the subject of the prohibition is the object itself. In the fourth, nothing at all is said about the object. The animal may be healthy, may have been slaughtered properly, its flesh may be clean. The ground of the prohibition is **the word said while it is slaughtered.**

**أُهِلَّ** — root **ه-ل-ل**: **to raise the voice, to cry out.** And the best-known derivative of the root will occur a few verses later in the same sūra: **هِلَال** (*hilāl*) — the new moon. The connection, as the lexicographers record it, is this: when the new moon was seen people announced it **by shouting**; the moon took this name because it is the thing shouted for.

The sūra uses this root in two separate places:

- **2/173** — *uhilla bihī* — the voice raised at the slaughter
- **2/189** — *al-ahilla* — the new moons

**The measure of the prohibition, then, is the name announced.** Slaughtering is an act; but a name is uttered at the head of the act, and that name determines to whom the act is done. This is the direct continuation of the clause *"if it is Him alone you serve"* at the end of verse 172.

**I record this as my own reading:** that three of the four items are built on hygiene and on the object while the last is built on **intention and declaration** prevents the list from being reduced to a single measure. The regulation of food cannot be explained wholly by health; there is also a question of **belonging** in it.

### فَمَنِ ٱضْطُرَّ — the licence of necessity

**ٱضْطُرَّ** — root **ض-ر-ر**: harm. The pattern is Form VIII (*iftiʿāl*) and the verb is **passive**: *uḍṭurra* — "he was compelled."

Its being passive matters. The person does not **put** himself into the situation of necessity; the necessity comes to him. This limits from the outset for whom the licence is.

And then two clauses follow: **غَيْرَ بَاغٍ وَلَا عَادٍ.** What these two words mean is a classical disagreement and must not be concealed:

| Reading | *bāgh* | *ʿād* |
|---|---|---|
| **First** | One who eats out of desire, because he craves it | One who goes beyond the amount of need |
| **Second** | One on an unlawful journey / in a state of rebellion | One who passes the bound, the measure |
| **Third** | One who eats when there is no necessity | One who goes on eating after the necessity has passed |

**I do not impose a preference.** The common ground of the three readings is this: the licence is **bounded by the scope of the necessity** — it opens neither to desire nor to duration.

That the root **ب-غ-ي** carries the senses "to want, to seek" and "to pass the bound" together has occurred earlier in this commentary; the two senses are seen to work together here as well.

**فَلَآ إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ** — "there is no sin upon him." The form of the sentence is worth recording: the ruling is not lifted, **responsibility** is lifted. The prohibition stands where it is; the burden is taken off the person.

This is a structure repeated in the legal sections of the sūra: at verse 158 *"there is no fault"*, at verses 184-185 the licence for the sick and the traveller, at verse 239 the changing of the form of the prayer in a state of fear, at verse 280 the postponement in hardship. Beside every ruling a door is left.

**And the closing attributes are chosen accordingly:** *ghafūr* (much-forgiving) and *raḥīm* (merciful). That the sentence following a prohibition ends with the attributes of pardon and not of punishment says what the licence is: not an open door but an **application of mercy.**

---

## 2/174-176 — Those who fill their bellies with fire

*"Those who conceal anything of the Book God has sent down and sell it for a small price — they fill their bellies with nothing but fire. God will not speak to them on the day of resurrection, nor will He purify them; and for them is a painful punishment. They are the ones who have bought straying at the price of guidance and punishment at the price of forgiveness. How enduring they are of the fire! That is because God has sent the Book down with the truth. And those who differ about the Book are in a far-off breach."*

### The placing is itself a statement

These three verses come immediately after the rulings about food (172-173) and **use the same verb: eating.**

In the previous verse what may be eaten had been regulated — carrion, blood, the flesh of swine, what is slaughtered in the name of other than God. Now a fifth thing is added, but not to the list: **مَا يَأْكُلُونَ فِى بُطُونِهِمْ إِلَّا ٱلنَّارَ** — "they fill their bellies with nothing but fire."

**I record this as an observation about the word order:** in changing the subject the sūra does not change the word. After what is forbidden to eat comes something **not forbidden to eat but corrupt in the way it is earned**. The discussion of food turns into a discussion of gain.

And the same transition will be made once more in the sūra: at verse 188 it will say *"do not **eat** one another's property wrongfully"*. The Qurʾān uses the verb "to eat" insistently for unlawful gain — at verse 275 *"those who **eat** ribā"* is the same verb.

**فِى بُطُونِهِمْ** — "into their bellies." In the grammar of the sentence it is a superfluous detail; everyone knows that what is eaten goes to the belly. It is placed there for emphasis — just like *"with their hands"* at verse 79. The comparison is not left in the abstract; a body and a stomach are pictured.

### يَكْتُمُونَ — the last link of the chain

The sūra's chain of **katm** (concealment) was given in a table at verse 140. This verse is the heaviest sentence of that chain, and it adds a second verb beside concealment: **وَيَشْتَرُونَ بِهِۦ ثَمَنًا قَلِيلًا** — "they sell it for a small price."

Concealment here, that is, does not stand alone; it is named together with a **gain**. This is the continuation of the threefold sequence at verse 79 (writing — ascribing — selling).

**ثَمَنًا قَلِيلًا** — "a small price." The adjective states not the smallness of the sum but **the ratio of the value**: whatever is received in return, it is small beside what is given. The same expression had occurred at verse 41.

### وَلَا يُكَلِّمُهُمُ ٱللَّهُ… وَلَا يُزَكِّيهِمْ

Two negations are listed, and both had occurred earlier in this sūra in their **positive** form:

| Verse | Its positive form |
|---|---|
| **2/186** | *"I **answer** the call of the caller when he calls Me"* — being addressed without an intermediary |
| **2/151** | *"…We sent a messenger who **purifies** you"* — *yuzakkīkum* |
| **2/174** | He will not speak, and will not **purify** |

What is enumerated as punishment, that is, is the withdrawal of what is enumerated elsewhere in the sūra as favour. **I record this as my own reading:** the description of the torment is made by the taking back of what has been given.

That the root **ز-ك-و** carries the senses "to purify" and "to make grow" together was treated in detail in the discussion of al-Jumuʿa (62/2); I do not repeat it.

### ٱشْتَرَوُا۟ ٱلضَّلَٰلَةَ بِٱلْهُدَىٰ

Verse 175 sets up once more the comparison the sūra uses most often — **trade**. Its occurrences in the sūra are these: 16 (those who buy straying with guidance), 86 (those who exchange the hereafter for this world), 90 (those who sell themselves), 102 (those who sell their share in the hereafter), 108 (those who exchange faith for unbelief), 175 (here).

And here **two exchanges at once** are enumerated: straying at the price of guidance, punishment at the price of forgiveness. Both the road and the outcome, that is, have been exchanged.

**The word order is built in reverse, and this is Arabic's own logic:** in the verb *ishtarā* the particle *bāʾ* marks **the price** (what is paid). "They bought straying with guidance" — the straying is what is taken, the guidance what is paid. What is left in hand is worse than what is given.

### فَمَآ أَصْبَرَهُمْ عَلَى ٱلنَّارِ

This sentence is one of the most disputed short expressions in the Qurʾān.

The pattern in Arabic is the **verb of wonder** (*mā afʿalahū* — "how … they are!"). But *what* the sentence states is disputed:

| Reading | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Wonder / irony** | "How enduring they are of the fire!" — because they undertake what they do as though they were people who could endure the fire |
| **A question** | "What makes them so bold in the face of the fire?" — *mā* is taken as an interrogative particle |
| **Imprecation / statement** | "How enduring of the fire they have been made!" — pointing to the continuance of the punishment |

**I do not impose a preference.** What is common to all three is this: the sentence expresses an **astonishment**, and what is wondered at is that this road should be chosen with the outcome known.

**The choosing of the root ص-ب-ر is striking in its own right.** At verse 153 the sūra had commanded patience, and at 155-157 had given good news to the patient. The same word is used here **in reverse**: there is a power of endurance, but it is spent in the wrong place. The word itself is not praise; the praise comes from what is endured for.

### شِقَاقٍۭ بَعِيدٍ

Verse 176 gives the ground of the three verses: **ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّ ٱللَّهَ نَزَّلَ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ بِٱلْحَقِّ** — "that is because God has sent the Book down with the truth."

The ruling, that is, is attached not to the character of those who conceal but **to the character of what they conceal**. The weight comes not from the act but from its subject.

**شِقَاق** — root **ش-ق-ق**: to split, to divide in two. This word was treated at verse 137; the finding there holds here too: the pattern *shiqāq* conveys reciprocity and its picture is concrete — **each standing on one side of the split**.

The difference is in the adjective added here: **بَعِيد** — far. The split, that is, has widened; the two sides have opened so far apart that they cannot see one another.

**And the subject of the division is worth recording:** *ikhtalafū **fi'l-kitāb*** — the division is **about the Book**. The parties, that is, are disputing not about different books but about **the same book**. The sūra had recorded this at verse 113 as well: both sides were reading the book and annulling one another.

This will be attached to a principle at verse 213 of the sūra: it will be said there that disagreement arose **after** the book came, and by reason of *baghy* (passing the bound). The source of the disagreement, that is, is not a lack of knowledge.

---

## 2/177 — What is goodness?

*"Goodness (birr) is not that you turn your faces to the east and the west. Goodness is that of one who believes in God, the last day, the angels, the Book and the prophets; who gives of his wealth, though he loves it, to relatives, orphans, the poor, the wayfarer, those who ask, and for the freeing of slaves; who keeps up the prayer and gives the alms; who fulfils his word when he gives it; who is patient in hardship, in sickness and at the moment of battle. Those are the ones who are true; those are the ones who guard themselves."*

This verse is one of the most comprehensive sentences of definition in the sūra — perhaps in the Qurʾān. At verse 44 we saw that the root of *birr* is **breadth** (from the same root *barr*: dry land, a wide expanse) and referred to this verse. Now the definition itself.

### The place of the opening

**لَّيْسَ ٱلْبِرَّ أَن تُوَلُّوا۟ وُجُوهَكُمْ قِبَلَ ٱلْمَشْرِقِ وَٱلْمَغْرِبِ**

Where the sentence is said matters: **immediately after the dispute about the qibla.** For thirty verses the sūra had discussed the question of direction, changed the qibla, answered objections. And now it says: goodness is not the direction you turn your face to.

This is not a step back. The qibla is still a command. What is said is that the qibla is **not goodness itself**. To carry out an obligation and to be good are not the same thing; the first may be a condition of the second but does not take its place.

**And the pattern itself must be recorded:** *laysa'l-birra* — "goodness is not…". This pattern occurs **twice** in the sūra, and in both a practice's being counted the whole of goodness is refused:

| Verse | What is refused | What is put in its place |
|---|---|---|
| **2/177** | *"that you turn your faces to the east and the west"* | A long definition — faith, spending, prayer, alms, keeping one's word, patience |
| **2/189** | *"that you enter houses from their backs"* | *"Goodness is to guard oneself (taqwā)."* |

The difference between the two is in the kind of thing refused: at 177 it is a **commanded** practice (the qibla), at 189 an **uncommanded** custom (entering houses from behind while in a state of ritual consecration). The pattern, that is, works in two directions at once — neither the carrying out of what is commanded nor the keeping up of an inherited custom is counted goodness itself.

And the shared answer of the two verses is **taqwā**: at 189 openly (*al-birra mani'ttaqā*), at 177 in the closing sentence (*wa ulāʾika humu'l-muttaqūn*).

And this finding is the exact counterpart of the attitude criticised throughout the sūra: to carry out a practice and count it the whole of piety.

### The structure of the definition

What is listed gathers into four groups, and their order is meaningful:

| Group | Content | Its field |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Faith** | God, the hereafter, the angels, the Book, the prophets | The inner world |
| **2. Property** | Relatives, orphans, the poor, the traveller, the one who asks, the freeing of slaves | Wealth |
| **3. Worship** | Prayer, alms | Body and order |
| **4. Character** | Keeping one's word, patience in three situations | Continuity |

**What stands out: property comes before worship.** And the group about property is the longest — six separate recipients are listed. Prayer and alms are passed over in a single line.

This ordering is the same as the arrangement the Qurʾān set up at verse 83: there too, after tawḥīd, came parents, kin, orphans and the poor; prayer and alms were last of all.

### عَلَىٰ حُبِّهِۦ — "though he loves it"

The finest clause of the definition is here. The giving of property is asked for, but on one condition: **while he loves it.**

The measure, that is, is not in the amount given but in **what is felt in giving**. To give what is left over, to distribute what is surplus to need, to hand over what is easy to part with anyway — these do not enter the definition. What enters the definition is the one who gives what he wants to keep.

The same clause is present in Sūrat al-Insān as well: *"They give food, though they love it, to the poor, the orphan and the captive"* (al-Insān 76/8). The two verses set the same measure.

### The six recipients

In the list, **ibnu'l-sabīl** (the wayfarer), **sāʾil** (the one who asks) and **fi'l-riqāb** (the freeing of slaves) are especially striking. The first three (relatives, orphans, the poor) are the near circle; the last three are **strangers** — the traveller is not known, the one who asks is of uncertain identity, the slave is outside society altogether.

The list, that is, widens from the near to the far and passes outside belonging. This looks the same way as the clause *"speak good words to people"* at verse 83 of the sūra: the measure does not change according to who the other person is.

### Three patiences

**فِى ٱلْبَأْسَآءِ وَٱلضَّرَّآءِ وَحِينَ ٱلْبَأْسِ** — three distinct situations are listed:

- **Baʾsāʾ** — poverty, hardship in livelihood
- **Ḍarrāʾ** — bodily hardship, illness
- **Ḥīna'l-baʾs** — the moment of battle

The three are three separate fronts on which a person's power of endurance is tested: property, body, life. And the last differs from the others — the one is continuous, the other momentary. Poverty and illness call for long breath; the moment of battle measures the stance in a single instant.

### The closing

**أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ ٱلَّذِينَ صَدَقُوا۟ وَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُتَّقُونَ**

Two qualities are given. The first is **ṣidq** — truthfulness; but here in a particular sense: one whose words and deeds agree. The attitude criticised throughout the sūra was exactly the lack of this — at verse 44 commanding another and forgetting oneself, at verse 84 keeping part of the book, at verse 94 saying one believes in the hereafter and clinging to life.

The second is **taqwā** — and this turns back to the second verse of the sūra. It had been said that the Book is "a guidance for those who guard themselves"; who those were had been enumerated in five attributes at the third verse. Now, a hundred and seventy-five verses later, the same word comes back with a far more detailed definition.

The sūra has come round on itself: it opened with taqwā, continued by recounting what taqwā is, and closed this section by defining taqwā again.

---

# 2/178-195 — Rulings: retaliation, fasting, the limit

Here the sūra enters its legal section. Up to this point there was description, history and measure; now regulation comes. And the language of the regulations is striking — most of them begin with the verb **كُتِبَ** (it was written). At the second verse we saw the senses of the root *kitāb*, "to bring together" and "to write an obligation"; here the second sense is at work.

---

## 2/178-179 — Retaliation

*"You who believe! Retaliation for those killed has been written for you… Whoever is pardoned anything by his brother, let what is fitting be followed, and let payment be made to him in a good way."*

### قِصَاص — retaliation

Root *q-ṣ-ṣ*: **to follow a track, to go after.** From the same root come *qiṣṣa* (a story told — the tracing of an event's trail) and *qaṣaṣ*. The Qurʾān uses this root in what Moses's mother said to his sister as well: *"Follow him"* (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/11).

*Qiṣāṣ*, in the word's own logic, is therefore **the following of a track**: whatever the act did, the return goes along its trail, neither short of it nor beyond it. The word carries the **equality** of the penalty in its own root.

### The historical function of the regulation

To understand this ruling one has to know what went before it. In the Arabia of the age of ignorance the blood feud was disproportionate: when someone from a strong tribe was killed, not only the killer but many people from the weak tribe could be killed. The measure was not what the dead man had done but **whose man he was.**

The ruling of retaliation cuts this chain: the return is attached **to the doer** and fixed **one for one**. The regulation, that is, does not introduce the penalty — the penalty was already there and was without limit. What the regulation introduces is **a limit**.

### "If he is pardoned by his brother"

The finest place in the verse is here. In setting out the possibility of pardon, the word **"his brother"** is used of the guardian of the one killed.

Within the sentence, that is, a bond of brotherhood is established between the killer and the side of the killed — at precisely the moment when there is blood between them. The choice of word does not merely offer pardon as a possibility; it also builds **the ground that makes pardon possible.** A person finds it hard to forgive someone he does not count a brother.

And what follows the pardon is regulated too: *"let what is fitting be followed and let payment be made in a good way"* — the creditor is not to press, the debtor is not to delay. So that the pardon itself does not turn into a new enmity.

### وَلَكُمْ فِى ٱلْقِصَاصِ حَيَوٰةٌ

*"In retaliation there is life for you, O possessors of understanding."*

On the face of it a contradictory sentence: life is said to lie in a sentence of death. But it is true in two respects:

**First, deterrence.** An act whose requital is known to be certain is committed less often.

**Second, and more importantly, the cutting of the chain.** The result of disproportionate vengeance is counter-vengeance; every return produces a new debt, and the blood feud runs for generations. Retaliation stops this chain by fixing the proportion. The lives saved are not those of the person on whom the ruling is carried out but of **those who would die if it were not.**

### أُو۟لِى ٱلْأَلْبَٰبِ — a third word for the mind

Earlier in this commentary we saw two other words Arabic uses for the mind:

| Word | Its root | What it measures |
|---|---|---|
| **ʿaql** (2/44) | To bind — *ʿiqāl*, the rope that ties a camel | The power to hold |
| **ḥijr** (al-Fajr 89/5) | Stone, obstacle, boundary — *ḥajr*, *maḥjūr* | The power to restrain |
| **lubb** (2/179) | The **core, the inside** of a thing — the kernel of the almond, of the walnut | Passing the shell and reaching the core |

All three measure not a capacity but **a function**. And the third says something different from the others: the possessor of *lubb* is one who does not stay at the shell. Why possessors of understanding are addressed in this verse is plain — the sentence "in retaliation there is life" is meaningful only for someone who looks below the surface.

---

## 2/180-182 — The bequest

*"When death comes to one of you, if he leaves property behind, it is written for you to make a bequest in a fitting way for parents and near relatives; this is a duty upon those who guard themselves. Whoever alters it after he has heard it — the sin is only on those who alter it. God is hearing, knowing. But whoever fears that the testator has slipped into a mistake or a sin, and puts things right between them, there is no sin upon him. God is forgiving, merciful."*

### كُتِبَ عَلَيْكُمْ

As was recorded at the opening of the section, the regulations in this stretch come with the verb **كُتِبَ** (it was written). The verb does this work in five places in this sūra:

| Verse | What is written |
|---|---|
| **2/178** | Retaliation |
| **2/180** | **The bequest** |
| **2/183** | Fasting |
| **2/216** | Fighting |
| **2/246** | (The same pattern, inside a story) |

What the five have in common is that all of them are **heavy** obligations. The verb's being used in the passive (the writer is not named) also puts the source of the ruling outside dispute.

**إِذَا حَضَرَ أَحَدَكُمُ ٱلْمَوْتُ** — "when death comes to one of you." The same expression had occurred at verse 133 of the sūra: there death had come to Jacob and his sons had been asked **what they would serve**.

**I record this as my own reading:** the same scene is set up twice and two different subjects of bequest are given — one service, the other property. In attaching the bequest of property to a ruling, the sūra has already shown in a scene what the real subject of inheritance is.

### إِن تَرَكَ خَيْرًا — property called "good"

The word in the conditional clause is striking: the word used for the property left behind is not **مَال** but **خَيْر**.

This naming recurs in the Qurʾān and was treated in the discussion of al-ʿĀdiyāt (100/8). The summary of the finding there: **a person names his property not "property" but "good"** — he sees what he has not as a neutral thing but as the good thing.

The same use is present at verse 215 of this sūra as well: *"Whatever good you spend…"* The sūra, that is, names property with the same word in two separate places: once as it is given, once as it is left.

**And the condition itself sets a limit:** the ruling holds for one who leaves property behind. For one with nothing to leave, no obligation arises.

### بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ

The root **ع-ر-ف**: to know, to recognise. *Maʿrūf*, in the word's own logic, means **what is known, recognised** — that is, what the society knows as good and does not find strange.

The word returns continually in the legal sections of this sūra as a **word of measure**: 178 (payment after retaliation), 228-233 (the regulations on divorce and nursing), 235, 240-241. Each time, instead of a figure, a **quality** is set down.

**This choice is worth recording.** The amount of the bequest is not stated; to whom it is to be made is enumerated, but how much it will be is left to *maʿrūf*. Instead of laying down a fixed proportion, taking as the measure "what is known and accepted" in each situation leaves the ruling open to time and circumstance.

The same root had occurred at verse 146 in the form *yaʿrifūnahu* (they recognise). The two uses show the centre of the root: **what is recognised needs no further proof.**

### The verses of bequest and of inheritance — a disagreement

The relation of this verse to the verses of inheritance that came down later (al-Nisāʾ 4/11-12) is a classical dispute and **must not be concealed:**

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **It has been abrogated** | The verses of inheritance fixed the shares of the heirs; this verse commanded a bequest to heirs, and so its ruling has lapsed |
| **It has been partly narrowed** | The ruling is in force for near relatives who are **not heirs**; the regulation for those who inherit has passed to the verses of inheritance |
| **It is in force** | The verse commands a bequest and does not interfere with inheritance; the two are separate institutions |

**In this commentary I impose no preference and issue no legal ruling;** the limit of the bequest, the permissibility of a bequest to an heir, and its proportion belong to the field of law, and there are differences among the schools.

I only recall the method I adopted at verse 106: **if two verses can be reconciled, reconciling them is preferred to claiming abrogation.** By this measure the second view contains less claim than the first.

### فَمَنۢ بَدَّلَهُۥ بَعْدَمَا سَمِعَهُۥ

**بَدَّلَ** — root **ب-د-ل**: to exchange one thing for another.

And this verb had occurred **twice** earlier in this sūra, in both cases in the heaviest of contexts:

| Verse | What was altered |
|---|---|
| **2/59** | *fa-baddala'lladhīna ẓalamū qawlan ghayra'lladhī qīla lahum* — the **word** given was altered |
| **2/108** | *wa man yatabaddali'l-kufra bi'l-īmān* — **faith** was exchanged for unbelief |
| **2/181** | **The bequest** was altered |

The sūra names the altering of a dead man's last word with **the same verb** as the altering of the word of revelation. And the condition is the same: *baʿda mā samiʿah* — "after he has heard it." Exactly the clause *min baʿdi mā ʿaqalūh* ("after they had understood it") at verse 75.

**فَإِنَّمَآ إِثْمُهُۥ عَلَى ٱلَّذِينَ يُبَدِّلُونَهُۥ** — "the sin is only on those who alter it."

The function of this sentence is not a threat but a **protection**. The testator's responsibility is complete at the moment he makes his bequest; what happens afterwards is not written to his account. The particle *innamā* (restriction) settles this.

And the principle of verse 48 of the sūra turns here into a rule of law: **the reckoning cannot be transferred** — neither for nor against.

**إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ** — the choice of attributes fits the context. A bequest is a word **heard**; the one who alters it has heard it too. The attribute *samīʿ* records that the word has not been lost.

### فَمَنْ خَافَ مِن مُّوصٍ جَنَفًا أَوْ إِثْمًا

Verse 182 opens a **licence** in the prohibition of 181 — and this is an exact instance of the sūra's way of establishing a ruling: the ruling is laid down, it is protected, and then its exception is given.

**جَنَف** — root **ج-ن-ف**: **to lean to one side, to slip.** The lexicographers explain the word as an unintended inclination: swerving to the wrong side while wanting the right.

**إِثْم** — what is deliberate. The two words thus form a pair: **error and intent.**

| Word | What it conveys |
|---|---|
| *janaf* | Slipping to one side unknowingly, unwillingly |
| *ithm* | Wrong done knowingly |

The verse names both; the authority to correct therefore arises without regard to the testator's intention. If the outcome is unjust, what caused it does not change matters.

**A note about the language and a limit:** *janaf* (**ج-ن-ف**) and the word *ḥanīf* (**ح-ن-ف**) at verse 135 are close in their field of meaning — both carry the idea of "leaning to one side". But **their roots are different** and the lexicographers treat them separately; I make **no claim** that there is a derivational bond between them. All that can be recorded is this: Arabic tells both the praised and the blamed swerving with the picture of "leaning" — the difference is in the direction of the lean.

### فَأَصْلَحَ بَيْنَهُمْ فَلَآ إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ

**إِصْلَاح** — root **ص-ل-ح**: to remove the defect of a thing and bring it into working order.

And what the verse does is striking: in the previous verse **altering** was counted a sin; here **altering** is permitted. Two different rulings are given for the same act, and what separates them is **the purpose**.

**I record this as my own reading:** this gives a measure for how fidelity to a text is to be understood. Faithfulness to the wording is not the preserving of the wording **for its own sake**; what is preserved is the justice the wording carries. If the wording of a bequest produces injustice, clinging to the wording is not counted fidelity.

And this measure is the exact reverse of the attitude criticised from the beginning of the sūra: at verse 65 the wording of the sabbath prohibition **was preserved and its purpose cancelled.** Here the opposite is done — the wording is altered and the purpose preserved. **The same act, two opposite directions.**

**فَلَآ إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ** — the pattern of the licence of necessity at verse 173 is used here too: the prohibition stands, responsibility is lifted from the person. The closing attributes are the same: *ghafūr* and *raḥīm*.

---

# 2/183-187 — Fasting

## 2/183 — "As it was written for those before you"

**صِيَام** — root *ṣ-w-m*: **to hold oneself back, to restrain.** In Arabic this word is used of the wind's dropping, of a horse being cut off from its feed, of the sun standing at its zenith.

The scope of the word is not confined to food and drink, and the Qurʾān shows this itself: in the word put in Mary's (Maryam) mouth, *ṣawm* is a fast of **not speaking** — *"I have vowed a fast to the Merciful; today I shall not speak to any human being"* (Maryam 19/26).

*Ṣawm*, then, is in essence **to hold oneself back from something**; abstaining from food and drink is one application of it.

**كَمَا كُتِبَ عَلَى ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِكُمْ** — "as it was written for those before you". The claim of continuity at the fourth verse of the sūra is applied here to a ruling: the practice is not new, its form has been renewed.

**لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَّقُونَ** — the ground: to guard oneself. We saw at the second verse that the root of *taqwā* is "to take cover". Fasting is an exercise in setting up a cover against one's own wants.

## 2/184 — Counted days and the ransom

*"For counted days. Whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days. And for those who can bear it there is a ransom: the feeding of a poor person. Whoever does more good of his own accord, it is better for him. And that you fast is better for you — if you but knew."*

### أَيَّامًا مَّعْدُودَٰتٍ

*"Counted days."* This expression is placed at the very head of the ruling and states a **limit**: the obligation is not open-ended but counted.

**And this exact expression had occurred once before in the sūra** — and moreover in exactly the opposite work:

| Verse | Who says it | For what |
|---|---|---|
| **2/80** | Those addressed | *"The fire will not touch us except for **a counted number of days**"* — to make the punishment smaller |
| **2/184** | The sentence of ruling | *"**For counted days**"* — to make the obligation lighter |

**I record this as my own reading:** the same expression is in one place the word of a groundless wish and in the other the clause of a ruling. At verse 80 that claim was answered with *"Have you taken a covenant from God?"* Here a covenant is given — but not for the shortness of the punishment, for the limit of the obligation.

The same expression will be used a third time in the sūra, for the days of the pilgrimage (2/203).

### Two licences, two different forms

The verse lists two situations and brings **different** solutions for them:

| Situation | Solution | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| **Sick or travelling** | *fa-ʿiddatun min ayyāmin ukhar* | The obligation is **postponed** — the number is kept |
| **Those who can bear it** | *fidyatun ṭaʿāmu miskīn* | The obligation is **transformed** — another work takes its place |

In the first solution the fast is not dropped, **its time changes.** This is a measure seen elsewhere in the sūra as well: at verse 239 the prayer is not lifted in a state of fear, its **form** changes. In hardship the obligation does not fall; its form is lightened.

In the second solution what takes its place is **of another kind**: an obligation belonging to the body is replaced by one belonging to property. And what is set in its place is not arbitrary: **the feeding of a poor person.** The one who cannot cut himself off from food and drink feeds another.

**I record this as my own reading:** that the substitute should be chosen in this way also shows what fasting looks to. The ransom is fixed not as money but as **food**; and the recipient is one who is hungry. A fast that cannot be kept is made good through another's hunger.

### وَعَلَى ٱلَّذِينَ يُطِيقُونَهُۥ — a disputed expression

This part of the verse is one of the known debates in the history of exegesis and **must not be concealed.**

**يُطِيقُونَهُ** — root **ط-و-ق**. The lexicographers relate the root to *ṭawq*: **the ring put round the neck, a collar.** From this comes *ṭāqa*: the load that can be put on a person's neck, that he can carry. The word states not "power" but **carrying capacity**.

The difficulty is this: the expression means literally "those who **have the power** for it". Yet what is expected of a person who has the power is not to pay a ransom but to fast. The views:

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **The ruling has been abrogated** | At the beginning there was a choice between fasting and ransom; when verse 185 came down, fasting was settled. The view most widely transmitted in the classical commentaries |
| **"Those who can barely manage it"** | The word states one at the last limit of his power — such as the aged, or the sick with no hope of recovery. On this view the ruling is in force |
| **A variant reading** | It is also transmitted that the word is read *yuṭawwaqūnahu* (those who are hard put to manage it); this reading supports the second view |

**I impose no preference and issue no legal ruling.** I record only this much: all three views arrive at the same result — the ransom **is not an easy road for one who is in a state to fast.** Indeed the verse's own closing says as much.

### فَمَن تَطَوَّعَ خَيْرًا

**تَطَوَّعَ** — root **ط-و-ع**. This root was treated in the discussions of Nūḥ and al-Qalam; the summary of the finding there: beneath the root lies **willing compliance**; its opposite is *karh* (unwillingly), and in Arabic what is done under compulsion is not called *ṭāʿa*.

The pattern is **Form V** and here reinforces the voluntariness: *taṭawwuʿ* — a work done of itself, over and above, without being asked for. In English the same field is covered by "supererogatory" and "voluntary offering".

Immediately after the ruling is laid down, then, a road is opened for going **above** the ruling. This is a structure repeated in the legal sections of the sūra: at verse 237 *faḍl*, at verse 280 *taṣadduq*, here *taṭawwuʿ*. Each time a minimum limit is stated and immediately afterwards what lies above it is shown.

The word **خَيْرًا** occurs three times here: *taṭawwaʿa **khayran** fa-huwa **khayrun** lahū… wa an taṣūmū **khayrun** lakum.* The repetition itself builds an emphasis, and in the last sentence the matter is settled.

### وَأَن تَصُومُوا۟ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ إِن كُنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ

After enumerating all the licences, the verse states a **preference**: to fast is better.

**And this shows how the licence is to be read.** An ease is given, but it is not said that the ease is the norm. Two sentences stand together: if you cannot, there is a road; if you can, it is better that you do.

**إِن كُنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ** — "if you but knew." The conditional clause ties the ground of the ruling to knowledge. This pattern is familiar in the sūra; but here it is turned round: throughout the sūra *"and that while you know"* (2/22, 42, 75, 188) had been repeated and knowledge had been used as a clause of **accusation**. Here the same word comes as an **invitation**.

And the next verse (185) will balance this preference with the principle of ease: *"God wants ease for you, He does not want hardship for you."* The two verses must be read together — one states the aim, the other how the aim is set.

---

## 2/185 — The clause of ease

*"God wants ease for you, He does not want hardship for you."*

That a ruling should be given together with its ground is the characteristic of this section. And the clause is not merely stated, it is applied: the licence for the sick and the traveller is inside the same verse.

**In `094-insirah.md`** the relation of the words *ʿusr* and *yusr* was treated — there it was said that hardship and ease are "together".
