Here something else is said: **the obligation itself is built upon ease.** The two do not conflict; one is about the structure of life, the other about the design of the ruling.

## 2/186 — An answer without an intermediary

*"When My servants ask you about Me — I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me."*

This verse is **unique** among the question-and-answer verses of the Qurʾān, and the reason lies in an absence:

When a question is put in the Qurʾān the pattern is almost always the same: *"They ask you about… **Say:** …"* There are instances of this a few verses apart in the same sūra — the new moons are asked about, and *"say"* comes (2/189); what is to be spent is asked about, and *"say"* comes (2/215).

In this verse **there is no "say".**

The question was put to the Prophet, but the answer was not given to the Prophet; it was given directly to the one who asked. The intermediary is removed at precisely the place where nearness is the subject. What the sentence says is shown by the way the sentence is built.

**The point at which it is placed is meaningful too.** The verse stands right in the middle of the rulings on fasting — before and after it, licence, duration, limit. In the middle of the regulations the language of ruling is left for a moment and something else is said: the relationship that is the reason for all these regulations is recalled.

**فَإِنِّى قَرِيبٌ** — "I am near". And the clause at verse 115 holds here too: this nearness is not spatial. Indeed the sentence speaks not of distance but of **answering**.

## 2/187 — "They are a garment for you"

**هُنَّ لِبَاسٌ لَّكُمْ وَأَنتُمْ لِبَاسٌ لَّهُنَّ**

The best-known comparison the Qurʾān uses for spouses. And the word **libās** had occurred earlier in this commentary: at verse 42, *talbīs* — dressing falsehood in the garment of truth. The same root, here in the positive direction.

Look at what a garment does; the whole force of the comparison is there:

- **It covers** — it does not show the fault outside
- **It protects** — from heat, from cold, from what is outside
- **It is nearest** — the first layer touching the body, with nothing in between
- **It adorns** — it completes the appearance
- **And it takes the shape of the wearer** — it is not a ready-made mould

And the comparison is **reciprocal**: the same sentence is said twice, with its direction reversed. Each side is both the wearer and the worn. No superiority or precedence is set up in the grammar of the sentence.

**تِلْكَ حُدُودُ ٱللَّهِ فَلَا تَقْرَبُوهَا** — "these are God's bounds, so do not **approach** them". The pattern we saw at verse 35: the prohibition begins not with the act itself but with the distance leading to it. A person cannot stand firmly right at the edge of a boundary.

---

## 2/188 — Property eaten knowingly

*"Do not eat one another's property wrongfully. And do not **let it down** to the judges, so as to eat a portion of people's property in sin, while you know."*

**وَتُدْلُوا۟ بِهَآ إِلَى ٱلْحُكَّامِ** — the root *d-l-w*: **to lower a bucket into a well.** The verb pictures property being let down to the judge like a bucket — from above downward, secretly, in order to draw something up.

What is forbidden is doing wrong **by using** the law. And its mechanism is striking: here the law is not broken, **the law is made an instrument.** The process is run, a decision is taken, everything looks in order — and the outcome is unjust.

This is the counterpart in the field of law of the pattern followed throughout the sūra: going round the prohibition (2/65), altering the word (2/59), dressing falsehood in the garment of truth (2/42). In all of them the form is preserved and the function emptied out.

**وَأَنتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ** — "and that while you know". The sūra's most repeated clause once again.

---

## 2/189 — "Entering houses from their backs"

*"…Goodness is not that you enter houses from their backs. Goodness is to guard oneself. So enter houses by their doors."*

The pattern **لَيْسَ ٱلْبِرُّ** comes for the second time after verse 177. There it was said that goodness is not the turning of a direction; here the same is said of another practice.

According to what is transmitted, not entering houses by the door while in a state of ritual consecration, and opening a hole at the back instead, was a practice left from the age of ignorance and was counted piety. The verse lifts it and gives its ground: goodness is not **making things hard**.

The sentence that follows has become a principle: **وَأْتُوا۟ ٱلْبُيُوتَ مِنْ أَبْوَٰبِهَا** — "enter houses by their doors". Its wording corrects a practice, but its idiomatic sense in Arabic is wider: to enter a matter by its proper way, from its right place.

---

## 2/190-195 — The limit of fighting

*"Fight in God's way those who fight you, but **do not transgress.** God does not love the transgressors."*

These verses are among the first regulations permitting fighting in the Qurʾān, and in reading them one must attend to two things at once: what they make permissible and **what they limit them with.**

### Two clauses in the first sentence

**ٱلَّذِينَ يُقَٰتِلُونَكُمْ** — "those who fight you". The verb is in the reciprocal pattern and the addressee is specified: those who fight. The sentence does not cover those who do not fight.

**وَلَا تَعْتَدُوٓا۟** — "do not transgress". The root *ʿ-d-w*: to pass the limit, to march upon. From the same root come *ʿadūw* (enemy) and *ʿudwān* (aggression).

That the limit should be set immediately beside the act permitted is the characteristic of this section. And the limit is not left abstract; it takes concrete form in the following verses: *"If they cease, then you cease"* (2/192-193), *"Aggression is only against wrongdoers"* (2/193).

### وَٱلْفِتْنَةُ أَشَدُّ مِنَ ٱلْقَتْلِ

*"Fitna is heavier than killing."*

We saw the root **فتنة** in Sūrat al-Burūj: **to test gold's purity by melting it in fire.** From this arise both the sense "trial" and the sense "torment, oppression".

In this context the word's counterpart is people's being held under pressure because of their beliefs, being tortured, being driven from their homes — indeed in the same section this is made concrete with *"from where they drove you out"*.

The sentence sets up a comparison: systematic oppression is a heavier harm than individual killings — because death finishes one person, whereas oppression holds a community under continual torment. This is not a sentence that makes light of killing; it is a sentence that weighs the ground of fighting **against the loss of life itself**.

### 2/195 — Two extremes

*"Spend in God's way, and **do not cast yourselves into destruction with your own hands.** Do good; God loves those who do good."*

In the middle of the verses about fighting, two different warnings side by side:

- **Spend** — do not hold back, do not spare what you have
- **Do not cast yourselves into destruction** — do not act without measure

It is transmitted that in the classical commentaries this verse has been understood both as "do not be miserly and leave yourselves unprepared" and as "do not take needless risk". The two readings do not exclude one another; both preserve the same measure — the avoidance of excess. The expression *wasaṭ* at verse 143 of the sūra is at work here too.

And the section ends, in the middle of the discussion of fighting, with an unexpected word: **وَأَحْسِنُوٓا۟** — "do good, do it well". *Iḥsān*, as we saw at verse 112, is both to do good and to do a work as it should be done. That it is placed at the end of the hardest regulation is no coincidence.

---

## 2/196 — The pilgrimage: a licence set beside the ruling

*"Complete the pilgrimage and the ʿumra for God. If you are **prevented**, then send whatever offering is within your means…"*

**وَأَتِمُّوا۟** — *itmām*: to finish without shortfall a work that has been begun. The command is not "do" but **"complete"**. The subject, that is, is that a thing begun should not be left half done.

**فَإِنْ أُحْصِرْتُمْ** — root **ح-ص-ر**: to surround, to hem in and cut off the way. *Ḥaṣr* (restriction) and *muḥāṣara* (siege) are from the same root. The verb comes in the **passive**: who does the preventing is not said — illness, an enemy, a road closed; all fall within the scope.

The characteristic of the section is at work here too: **as the ruling is laid down, its exception is given inside the same verse.** What was done for fasting at verse 185 is done here for the pilgrimage.

And the ransom is given with three options: **fasting, alms or an offering.** The choice is left to the person — he does whichever is within his means. That the ruling is not locked to a single form is the practical counterpart of the clause *"God wants ease"* at verse 185.

**لِمَن لَّمْ يَكُنْ أَهْلُهُۥ حَاضِرِى ٱلْمَسْجِدِ ٱلْحَرَامِ** — to whom the licence belongs is limited by geography: to those who come from afar. One who lives nearby can come at any time in any case. **The ease is given where the hardship is.**

---

## 2/197 — Three prohibitions and one provision

*"The pilgrimage is in known months. Whoever undertakes the pilgrimage in them: no **coarse speech, no transgression, no wrangling** in the pilgrimage."*

Three words, three distinct fields:

| Word | Root | What it cuts off |
|---|---|---|
| **رَفَث** | ر-ف-ث | Speech and conduct of a sexual kind — looking **to the body** |
| **فُسُوق** | ف-س-ق | Going outside the bound — looking **to the ruling** |
| **جِدَال** | ج-د-ل | Contention, a quarrel of words — looking **to other people** |

The concrete sense of the root **ف-س-ق**, as the lexicographers record it, is **a date's coming out of its skin**: overflowing one's own vessel. The word had occurred earlier in this commentary (2/26).

The root **ج-د-ل** means **to twist a rope tight**; *jadīl* — a firmly plaited rope. It is used in wrestling for throwing one's opponent to the ground. *Jidāl*, then, is each side trying to twist the other. **The prohibition of wrangling is not the prohibition of speaking but of speaking with the intention of winning** — this is recorded as a reading; the root of the word is open in this direction.

**وَتَزَوَّدُوا۟ فَإِنَّ خَيْرَ ٱلزَّادِ ٱلتَّقْوَىٰ** — "take provision; the best provision is taqwā."

The command is first **concrete**: take your food with you when you set out. According to what is transmitted, some would set out without provisions and say "we are trusting in God", and then become a burden on people all along the road. The verse does away with this.

And the second half of the sentence carries the same word **to another plane**: the real provision is taqwā. **The concrete command is not cancelled — a layer is added on top of it.** A working often seen in the sūra: the ruling stays in this world, the ground is attached upward.

---

## 2/198 — Trade is not a sin

*"There is no sin upon you in seeking a **bounty** from your Lord."*

The word **فَضْل** here means provision and gain. Whether trading during the pilgrimage season spoiled the act of worship was disputed; the verse plainly does away with the question.

The pattern **لَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ** is a pattern of licence repeated in the sūra, and it always does the same work: **to remove an extra prohibition people have laid down of themselves.** What was done for entering houses from behind at verse 189 is done here too.

**فَإِذَآ أَفَضْتُم مِّنْ عَرَفَٰتٍ** — *ifāḍa*: water overflowing and pouring from a place. The movement of the crowd is told as **flowing water**; the word will continue into the next verse.

---

## 2/199 — "Press on from where the people press on"

*"Then **press on from where the people press on.**"*

According to what is transmitted, a group counted themselves privileged and would not stand where the crowd stood, but waited in a separate place. **The verse removes this distinction.**

And the manner of the removal is striking: no prohibition is laid down, **no identity is touched**; it is only said "press on from where the people press on". And the word used is **ٱلنَّاس** — that is, not lineage, not tribe, not rank: **people.**

It fits the general line of the sūra: at verse 62 the return was attached to an attribute rather than to a community's name, at verse 177 goodness was defined as conduct rather than direction, and here a claim of privilege in worship is removed.

---

## 2/200-202 — Two prayers, two persons

*"…Among the people are some who say, 'Our Lord, give to us in this world'; and they have no **share** in the hereafter. And among them are some who say, 'Our Lord, give us good in this world and good in the hereafter, and guard us from the punishment of the fire.'"*

Two prayers are set side by side, and the difference is in **a single addition**:

| Prayer | What is asked | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| The first | This world alone | *Mā lahū fi'l-ākhirati min **khalāq*** — no share in the hereafter |
| The second | This world **and** the hereafter | Counted among those accepted |

**The point to be noticed:** the prayer that is refused is not refused because it asks for this world. **This world is asked for in the second prayer too.** What is refused is asking for this world **alone**.

**خَلَاق** — root **خ-ل-ق**: to measure and shape a thing. From this come both the sense "to create" and the sense "portion, share". The word here means **an allotted share**.

The command at the head of the section prepares this pair as well: **فَٱذْكُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ كَذِكْرِكُمْ ءَابَآءَكُمْ أَوْ أَشَدَّ ذِكْرًا** — "remember God as you remember your forefathers, or with a stronger remembrance." Gathering after the pilgrimage to praise one's forefathers was a widespread custom; the verse **does not abandon the act of remembering, it changes its direction.** A working repeated in the sūra: the form stays, the content is set in its place.

---

## 2/203 — No sin on the one who hastens, none on the one who stays

*"Remember God in counted days. Whoever **hastens on in two days**, no sin upon him; whoever **stays behind**, no sin upon him either — for one who guards himself."*

The same sentence twice, for two opposite choices: **فَلَآ إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ … فَلَآ إِثْمَ عَلَيْهِ.**

Both options end with an equal ruling. The verse holds neither side superior; it leaves the choice to the person. **And the condition is at the end: لِمَنِ ٱتَّقَىٰ** — what is decisive is not how many days one stays but in what state one stays.

This is the measure seen throughout the section on the pilgrimage: duration, place and form are regulated; **but no extra burden is laid, and where a field of choice is left, both options are counted legitimate.**

---

## 2/204-206 — The man whose speech is pleasing

*"Among the people is one whose speech about the life of this world pleases you; and he calls God to witness what is in his heart. Yet he is **the fiercest of adversaries.**"*

**أَلَدُّ ٱلْخِصَام** — root **ل-د-د**: *ladīd*, the two sides of the neck and of the jaw. The lexicographers describe the *aladd* as "an adversary who, seized from one side, slips to the other, who cannot be held". **The word itself carries a picture of not being able to be held.**

The verse draws a portrait, and the portrait has three elements:

1. **His speech is fine** — *yuʿjibuka qawluhu*
2. **He swears** — he calls God to witness what is in his heart
3. **When he turns away** — *wa idhā tawallā* — his work changes

**وَإِذَا تَوَلَّىٰ سَعَىٰ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ لِيُفْسِدَ فِيهَا وَيُهْلِكَ ٱلْحَرْثَ وَٱلنَّسْلَ** — "he destroys the tilth and the stock."

**حَرْث** (tilth) and **نَسْل** (stock) are set side by side. One grows from the ground, the other comes from the human being; **both are things that reach into the future.** Corruption, that is, is described as the cutting off not of the present but of **continuity**. The root **ح-ر-ث** — to plough, to sow — was treated at 56/63 (al-Wāqiʿa) as the human being's portion; here the destruction of that portion is recounted.

**وَإِذَا قِيلَ لَهُ ٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ أَخَذَتْهُ ٱلْعِزَّةُ بِٱلْإِثْمِ** — "when he is told 'fear God', **his pride drives him into sin.**"

The subject of the sentence is *al-ʿizza*: **pride takes him, carries him off.** The person, that is, does not make a decision; he is himself seized. What is recounted is not the warning's **being ineffective but its having the reverse effect** — the warning increases the resistance.

---

## 2/207 — And the opposite portrait

*"And among the people is one who **sells himself** seeking God's good pleasure."*

**يَشْرِى نَفْسَهُ** — the root *shirāʾ* is used in Arabic both for selling and for buying (*aḍdād*: words of contrary sense). The widespread rendering here is **to sell**: the person gives himself.

Two verses, two portraits, opening with the same pattern: **وَمِنَ ٱلنَّاسِ مَن…** Both begin with "among the people is one who".

| | 204-206 | 207 |
|---|---|---|
| His speech | Pleasing | Not stated |
| His oath | Present | Absent |
| When he turns away | He spoils | — |
| The measure | **What is seen** | **What is given** |

The sūra is describing a type here, not denouncing a person. **An attribute is set down; whoever carries that attribute is included in it** — a line preserved throughout the sūra.

---

## 2/208 — "Enter into peace, all of you"

*"You who believe! Enter into **silm**, all of you, and do not follow the footsteps of Satan."*

**سِلْم** — root **س-ل-م**: to be sound, wholeness, submission, peace. The word is open both to the reading "peace" and to the reading "submission", and both are transmitted in the classical commentaries; **I impose no preference.**

**كَآفَّةً** — "wholly, all of you together". The root of the word is connected with **ك-ف-ف** (*kaff*, the palm of the hand, gathering in and holding the edge of a thing): **leaving no piece out at the edge.**

Two readings follow from this, and both are transmitted: **the whole of those entering** (let everyone enter) or **the whole of what is entered** (let it be entered wholly).

**خُطُوَٰتِ ٱلشَّيْطَٰن** — "the footsteps of Satan". This expression comes for the second time in the sūra (it had occurred at verse 168), and what was recorded there holds here too: **the word is not "leap" but "step".** A road is covered not by a single decision but by small steps following one another.

---

## 2/209-212 — Slipping, mockery and point of view

*"If you **slip** after the clear proofs have come to you, know that God is mighty, wise."*

**زَلَلْتُم** — root **ز-ل-ل**: the slipping of the foot. The choice of word is striking: not turning, not denying, not refusing — **slipping.** The subject, that is, is not a decision but a failure to hold on.

And the same expression as at verse 213 occurs here too: *min baʿdi mā jāʾatkumu'l-bayyināt* — **after the clear proofs have come.** The same clause four verses apart: the trouble is not in the knowledge.

*"Ask the Children of Israel: how many clear **signs** We gave them…"*

**سَلْ بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ** — the long history recounted through the first third of the sūra is summed up here in a single sentence and used **as proof**: the abundance of proofs given does not of itself bring change.

*"The life of this world has been **made to seem fair** to those who cover over, and they mock those who believe. But those who guard themselves will be above them on the day of resurrection."*

**زُيِّنَ** — a passive verb: **the one who adorns is not named.** This pattern had been used earlier in the sūra; the passive construction carries the situation of a person not noticing how his own way of seeing has been built for him.

And **سُخْرِيَّة** (mockery) comes here as a consequence: when the life of this world becomes the measure, whatever does not fit the measure looks ridiculous. **The verse recounts mockery not as a problem of character but as the natural consequence of a point of view** — this is recorded as a reading, and its ground is that the two clauses are joined with *wāw*.

**وَٱللَّهُ يَرْزُقُ مَن يَشَآءُ بِغَيْرِ حِسَابٍ** — this sentence at the end of the section says that the thing mocked (poverty, weakness) cannot be a measure.

---

## 2/213 — Why there is disagreement

*"People were **one community**; God sent prophets as bringers of good news and as warners, and with them He sent down the Book with the truth, to judge between people in what they differed about. **And those to whom the Book was given** differed about it, after the clear proofs had come to them, out of envy between them."*

The verse sets up a picture of history, and its order is striking:

1. **Unity** — *kāna'l-nāsu ummatan wāḥida*
2. **Prophets and a book** — to settle the disagreement
3. **Disagreement** — and this time **among those who received the book**

**The cause of the division, that is, is not given as ignorance.** The text says it plainly: *min baʿdi mā jāʾathumu'l-bayyināt* — **after** the clear proofs had come.

**بَغْيًۢا بَيْنَهُمْ** — root **ب-غ-ي**: to pass the bound, to begrudge. The cause is given not as a lack of knowledge but as **a state held against one another**.

This is in the same line as the narrative of the Children of Israel in the earlier sections of the sūra: there too a problem of conduct in spite of knowledge was treated (2/75, 2/89, 2/146). **The sūra takes disagreement out of being a matter of knowledge and makes it a matter of character.**

---

## 2/214 — "Did you suppose you would enter the garden?"

*"Or did you suppose you would enter the garden without there coming to you the like of what came to those who passed away before you? **Poverty and hardship** touched them and they were **shaken**, until the messenger and those who believed with him said, 'When will God's help come?' Surely God's help is near."*

The verse descends in three steps:

| Word | Root | What |
|---|---|---|
| **ٱلْبَأْسَآء** | ب-أ-س | Poverty, hardship coming from outside |
| **ٱلضَّرَّآء** | ض-ر-ر | Harm touching the body and the life |
| **زُلْزِلُوا۟** | ز-ل-ز-ل | **They were shaken** — the same word as the shaking of the earth |

And the fourth step is a word spoken: **مَتَىٰ نَصْرُ ٱللَّهِ** — "When will God's help come?"

**Who says this is stated plainly in the verse:** *al-rasūlu wa'lladhīna āmanū maʿahū* — the messenger and those who believed with him.

This is a detail worth recording: **the question itself is not condemned.** The verse transmits this word not as an instance of weakness but as a measure of the point the hardship had reached. **And the answer is not a rebuke but a statement:** *alā inna naṣra'llāhi qarīb.*

The choice of the word **زُلْزِلُوا۟** is meaningful too: the root is the name of the 99th sūra (*al-Zilzāl*), and there the shaking of the earth is recounted. **Here what is shaken is not the earth but people.**

---

## 2/215 — The question is "what", the answer "to whom"

*"They ask you what they should spend. **Say:** whatever good you spend, let it be for parents, near relatives, orphans, the poor and the wayfarer."*

The question is **what**: *mādhā yunfiqūn* — "what shall we spend?"
The answer is **to whom**: parents, near relatives, orphans, the poor, the wayfarer.

**The answer does not give what the question asked for.** No amount or kind is stated; instead an address is enumerated.

I record this as an observation about the word order: **the question is framed as "what will leave my hand", the answer is given as "into whose hand it will pass".** The verse takes spending out of the giver's reckoning and attaches it to the need of the one who receives.

And the order is worth recording too: **it begins with the nearest** — parents, then near relatives, then the orphan, the poor, the wayfarer. **The circle widens.** The same ordering was present at verse 177; the two verses set up the same arrangement.

**وَمَا تَفْعَلُوا۟ مِنْ خَيْرٍ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ بِهِۦ عَلِيمٌ** — the closing does not reopen the question of amount: it is said that what is done is known.

---

## 2/216 — "Though you dislike it"

*"Fighting has been written for you, though you dislike it. It may be that you dislike a thing and it is good for you; and it may be that you love a thing and it is bad for you. **God knows, and you do not know.**"*

The most striking side of the verse is that in laying down the ruling it **does not deny the dislike:** *wa huwa kurhun lakum* — "and it is heavy upon you".

**The ruling is laid down without discounting the hearer's feeling.** There are other instances of this in the sūra: at verse 183 a ground was given when fasting was written, and at 185 the clause of ease was entered.

**عَسَىٰٓ** — "it may be that". This particle states no certainty. **The verse, that is, does not say "everything you dislike is good for you";** it says that the bond between the two is **not necessary**. It is built in both directions: what you love may also be bad.

**وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تَعْلَمُونَ** — the sentence is not a claim about knowledge but **a statement of a limit.** What a decision rests on is not what a person sees at that moment.

---

## 2/217-218 — The measure of the measure

*"They ask you about fighting in the sacred month. **Say:** fighting in it is a great thing. But barring from God's way, covering over Him, barring from the Sacred Mosque and driving its people out of it are greater with God. **Fitna is greater than killing.**"*

The verse sets up a **balance** and shows both its pans plainly:

| Pan | Content |
|---|---|
| What is asked about | Fighting in the sacred month — *kabīr* (great) |
| What is set against it | Barring from the way, covering over, barring from the mosque, driving out the people — *akbar* (greater) |

**Note: the first pan is not made lighter.** The verse does not say "it is unimportant", it says *kabīr*. **Then it sets down the second pan.**

This is a method seen earlier in the sūra (it was treated in the section on 2/190-195): **the ruling is not given without a comparison being made.**

**ٱلْفِتْنَةُ أَكْبَرُ مِنَ ٱلْقَتْلِ** — at verse 191 it said *ashaddu* (heavier), here *akbar* (greater). **The same sentence twice, with two different adjectives.** The root of the word (**ف-ت-ن** — to test gold in fire) was treated in the section on verse 191.

---

## 2/219 — Wine, gambling and "what is left over"

*"They ask you about wine and gambling. **Say:** in both there is great sin and some benefits for people; **and their sin is greater than their benefit.**"*

The construction of the answer is worth recording and is a frequent instance in the Qurʾān's language of ruling:

1. **The harm is stated** — *ithmun kabīr*
2. **The benefit is also stated** — *manāfiʿu li'l-nās*
3. **Then it is weighed** — *wa ithmuhumā akbaru min nafʿihimā*

**The benefit is not denied.** What gambling brings in, and the commercial and social place of wine in the society of that day, are not disregarded. **The ruling is laid down not by refusing the benefit but by showing the balance.**

**A note on a variant reading:** instead of *ithmun kabīr* (great sin), the reading *ithmun kathīr* (much sin) is also transmitted. **The difference between the two readings is between magnitude and multitude, and I record it because it changes the meaning.**

**وَيَسْـَٔلُونَكَ مَاذَا يُنفِقُونَ قُلِ ٱلْعَفْوَ** — "they ask you what they should spend; say: **al-ʿafw.**"

The root **ع-ف-و**: to wipe out, to pardon; and **what is left over, what is surplus to need.** The lexicographers give the second sense here: **what remains over from one's own need.**

At verse 215 the same question was asked and the answer was given as an **address**. Here the same question comes again and this time a **measure** is given: not all one has, but what is left over. **The two answers complete one another: to whom, and how much.**

---

## 2/220 — The property of orphans: a matter with two edges

*"They ask you about orphans. **Say:** to set their affairs right is good. And if you live together with them, **they are your brothers.**"*

According to what is transmitted, after the severe warnings about the property of orphans some people had separated the orphans' food entirely from their own, which both caused difficulty and troubled the children. **The verse sets a measure between two extremes.**

**فَإِخْوَٰنُكُمْ** — "they are your brothers". The ruling here is given not by a rule of procedure but by **a description of a relationship**.

**وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ ٱلْمُفْسِدَ مِنَ ٱلْمُصْلِحِ** — and a limit is set: living together is permitted, but it is recalled that the difference between the one who spoils and the one who sets right is known. **The permission is not left unsupervised.**

---

## 2/221 — The measure is not membership but direction

*"…A believing bondwoman is better than a mushrik woman, though she please you… **They call to the fire; God calls to the garden and to forgiveness by His permission.**"*

The ground of the verse is given plainly at the end, and the ground is about **the direction of the call**: *ulāʾika yadʿūna ila'l-nār.*

**The point to be recorded:** the verse issues no wholesale ruling about a lineage, a people or a community; **it speaks by way of an attribute.** At verse 62 of the sūra it was treated that what one receives is tied not to the name of a community but to an attribute; the measure here comes from the same place.

And in making the comparison, **social standing is expressly set aside:** *wa law aʿjabatkum* — "though she please you". Given the distance between slave and free in the society of that day, what the sentence does becomes visible: **the measure is taken from status and attached to direction.**

---

## 2/222 — The choice of the word

*"They ask you about menstruation. **Say: it is an adhā.**"*

**أَذًى** — root **أ-ذ-ي**: hurt, discomfort, something that causes trouble.

**The choice of the word is worth recording.** The verse does not use here a word stating impurity, filth, or a state to be kept away from; **it uses a word stating discomfort.**

It is widely transmitted that in the classical commentaries this word is explained in respect of the trouble it gives the woman.

**As historical background this is transmitted:** in that period, in some communities there were practices such as putting a menstruating woman out of the house and not eating with her. **The verse's limiting of the boundary to a single act has been understood as doing away with that wide exclusion.**

**I do not enter into the legal rulings;** the details the schools derive from this verse differ, and what is conveyed here is the linguistic side of the word.

**إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُحِبُّ ٱلتَّوَّٰبِينَ وَيُحِبُّ ٱلْمُتَطَهِّرِينَ** — the verse closes the discussion of purity with a sentence about love. The pattern **مُتَطَهِّرِين** (Form V) means **one who purifies himself**; that is, the act belongs to the person.

---

## 2/223 — "Your tilth"

**نِسَآؤُكُمْ حَرْثٌ لَّكُمْ**

The root **ح-ر-ث**: to plough the soil and cast seed. The root has occurred at two other places in this commentary — the corrupter's destroying of the *ḥarth* at 2/205, and the distinction at al-Wāqiʿa 56/63 between the human being's act of *ḥarth* and God's act of *zarʿ*.

**What the comparison looks to can be read from the root itself.** *Ḥarth*, in the lexicographers' description, is **the work of sowing**, and sowing has a single direction: **the future.** A field that is sown is sown for a crop.

**For this reason it is widely said in the classical commentaries that the comparison is a description looking to children and offspring** — indeed the continuation of the sentence supports this: *wa qaddimū li-anfusikum* — "send on ahead for yourselves."

**And there is a further point that must be recorded:** *ḥarth* in Arabic is what is valued, what labour is spent on, what is protected. **A tilth is its owner's source of livelihood; it is not what is neglected but what is watched over.** To pull the comparison in a demeaning direction runs against the word's use in Arabic.

**وَٱتَّقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَٱعْلَمُوٓا۟ أَنَّكُم مُّلَٰقُوهُ** — this clause, coming immediately after the sentence, does not leave the permission unbounded. **A form repeated in the sūra: a note of responsibility beside a permission.**

And in the preceding verse (222) the boundary had already been set. **The two verses are read together: one sets the boundary, the other leaves the field outside the boundary free.**

---

## 2/224-227 — The oath: a word used as an excuse

*"Do not make God a **shield** for your oaths, so as to be kept from doing good, from guarding yourselves and from setting things right between people…"*

**عُرْضَة** — root **ع-ر-ض**: what lies crosswise, what is set in front. The lexicographers give here the senses "an obstacle placed in between" and "an excuse constantly put forward". **The oath, that is, turns into a pretext put forward for not doing what ought to be done.**

The situation the verse points to is concrete: in anger a person says "I swear I will never speak to him again"; then, when reconciliation is called for, he offers his oath as a ground. **The verse does away with this use of the oath.**

**لَّا يُؤَاخِذُكُمُ ٱللَّهُ بِٱللَّغْوِ فِىٓ أَيْمَٰنِكُمْ** — "you are not held answerable for the **laghw** in your oaths."

The root **ل-غ-و** was analysed in the section on al-Wāqiʿa 56/25: **a word not counted, that goes for nothing.** Here the word is used as a legal term: **an oath that comes out of the mouth without being intended.**

And the measure is given at once: *bimā kasabat qulūbukum* — **what the hearts have earned.** The measure, that is, is not the wording but the intention.

**ٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْلُونَ مِن نِّسَآئِهِمْ تَرَبُّصُ أَرْبَعَةِ أَشْهُرٍ** — *īlāʾ*: to swear not to approach one's wife. In the age of ignorance this was used to leave a woman in an indefinite uncertainty: neither married nor divorced.

**What the verse does is to set a term: four months.** After that there are two roads, and both are named in the verse: **to return** (*fāʾū*) or **to divorce** (*ʿazamu'l-ṭalāq*).

**The uncertainty, that is, is removed.** This is the working seen again and again in this section of the sūra: a practice is not forbidden, its **being without a term** is forbidden.

---

## 2/228 — The waiting period and a right in two directions

*"Divorced women wait by themselves for **three qurūʾ**."*

**قُرُوء** — the singular is *qarʾ*/*qurʾ*. **The word is among those counted in Arabic as of contrary sense (*aḍdād*), and this is the source of the best-known disagreement over the verse:**

| Reading | What *qurʾ* is | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Menstruation** (the period of the menses) | The waiting period is three menstrual periods |
| 2 | **Ṭuhr** (the period of purity between two menses) | The waiting period is three periods of purity |

**Both views have been defended by the great schools of law, and their grounds have to do with language:** the lexicographers record that the root (**ق-ر-أ**) carries the sense "to gather, to bring together", and that this can fit both the gathering of blood in the womb and its flowing.

**I adopt no preference and issue no legal ruling.** The disagreement is real and must not be concealed; **both views agree in the purpose of the waiting period:** that lineage should not be confused, and that the parties should have time for a return.

**وَبُعُولَتُهُنَّ أَحَقُّ بِرَدِّهِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ إِنْ أَرَادُوٓا۟ إِصْلَٰحًا** — the right of return is tied to a condition: **the intention of setting right.** The return, that is, cannot be used to keep matters hanging. The same clause will be repeated in harder language at verse 231.

**وَلَهُنَّ مِثْلُ ٱلَّذِى عَلَيْهِنَّ بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ** — "women have rights, according to what is fitting, like what is due from them."

The construction of the sentence is symmetrical: **lahunna** (in their favour) / **ʿalayhinna** (against them). And between them stands the word **مِثْل** (the like, the equivalent).

**وَلِلرِّجَالِ عَلَيْهِنَّ دَرَجَةٌ** — "and men have a degree over them."

**What this sentence states has been debated in the classical commentaries, and the chief explanations are these:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| That the authority to divorce and to take back lies with the man | The context: the verse is about this |
| The obligation of maintenance | A link is made with the obligation of maintenance at al-Nisāʾ 4/34 |
| The weight of responsibility within the family | An undertaking on the side of obligation |

**I transmit these explanations as they are transmitted; I impose no preference and draw no legal conclusion.**

**The verse's own word order must be recorded:** the sentence comes **after** it has been stated that the rights are mutual, and the context is the process of divorce. **It is not a sentence built as a general declaration of superiority** — I record this on the basis of the order of the text.

---

## 2/229-230 — Divorce tied to a number

*"**Divorce is twice.** After that, either keeping in a fitting way or releasing in a good way."*

**In the age of ignorance divorce could be without number:** the man divorced, took her back before the waiting period was out, then divorced again. **This was a way of keeping a woman indefinitely in suspense.**

**The verse ties this to a number: two.** And after that it leaves two options — both of them qualified:

| Option | Its qualifier |
|---|---|
| **إِمْسَاكٌۢ** (to keep) | *bi-maʿrūf* — in the fitting, recognised way |
| **تَسْرِيحٌۢ** (to release) | *bi-iḥsān* — in a good way |

**That separation too has a manner is said by the qualifier of the word.** The notion of *iḥsān* was treated at verse 112: to do a work as it should be done and well. **That this qualifier should be given to divorce is worth recording.**

**وَلَا يَحِلُّ لَكُمْ أَن تَأْخُذُوا۟ مِمَّآ ءَاتَيْتُمُوهُنَّ شَيْـًٔا** — taking back what has been given is forbidden. **The exception is kept narrow:** where the parties fear they cannot keep God's bounds, the woman's separating by giving a compensation.

**تِلْكَ حُدُودُ ٱللَّهِ فَلَا تَعْتَدُوهَا** — "these are God's bounds, do not overstep them." The word **حُدُود** is repeated many times in this section; at verse 187 it said *fa-lā taqrabūhā* (do not approach them), here *fa-lā taʿtadūhā* (do not overstep them). **Two patterns in two places: one distance, one boundary.**

---

## 2/231 — Keeping her in order to harm

*"When you divorce women and they reach the end of their waiting period, either keep them in a fitting way or release them in a fitting way. **Do not keep them in order to harm them, to transgress against them.**"*

**ضِرَارًا لِّتَعْتَدُوا۟** — root **ض-ر-ر**, and the pattern is Form III (*mufāʿala*): it conveys **reciprocity or intent.** That is, "so that she may be harmed, knowingly."

**The verse brings intention into the ruling.** The act is the same — taking back. But if the intention is different the ruling changes: what is permitted at verse 228 with *in arādū iṣlāḥan* (if they want to set things right) is forbidden here when done *ḍirāran* (in order to harm).

**I record this as an observation:** in the same section the same act occurs twice, and what separates them is intention alone.

**وَلَا تَتَّخِذُوٓا۟ ءَايَٰتِ ٱللَّهِ هُزُوًا** — "do not take God's verses in mockery." That this sentence is placed here is striking: **to use a ruling in due form and turn it against its purpose is called *mockery* in the verse.**

This is the continuation of the pattern followed in the sūra: at 2/188 doing wrong by making the law an instrument, at 2/65 going round the prohibition, at 2/42 dressing falsehood in the garment of truth. **In all of them the form is preserved and the function emptied out.**

---

## 2/232 — Do not obstruct

*"When you divorce women and they have completed their waiting period, **do not prevent** them from marrying their former husbands, if they agree between themselves in a fitting way."*

**فَلَا تَعْضُلُوهُنَّ** — root **ع-ض-ل**. The concrete sense the lexicographers record is striking: *ʿaḍalati'l-nāqa* — **the she-camel's giving birth being blocked, the young not being able to come out.** From this comes the sense "to squeeze, to block the way, to leave without a solution".

**The choice of the word shows the character of the behaviour forbidden:** the obstruction is set up not by a ruling but **by the blocking of the way out**.

Who is addressed is not named in the verse; **a plural masculine address is used**, and it is transmitted in the classical commentaries that these are the woman's guardians or her former husband.

**ذَٰلِكُمْ أَزْكَىٰ لَكُمْ وَأَطْهَرُ** — the ground is again given: **purer and more cleansing.** That the ground should be given together with the ruling is the characteristic of this section.

---

## 2/233 — Nursing: a prohibition of harm on both sides

*"Mothers nurse their children **two full years** — for one who wishes to complete the nursing."*

**حَوْلَيْنِ كَامِلَيْنِ** — "two full years". And immediately afterwards the clause: *li-man arāda an yutimma'l-raḍāʿa* — **for one who wishes to complete it.** The term, that is, is an upper limit, not a necessity.

In distributing the obligations the verse **sets a limit separately for both sides:**

**لَا تُضَآرَّ وَٰلِدَةٌۢ بِوَلَدِهَا وَلَا مَوْلُودٌ لَّهُۥ بِوَلَدِهِۦ**

"Let no mother be harmed on account of her child, nor a father on account of his child."

**The sentence is symmetrical, and its symmetry must be recorded:** the same verb, the same pattern, separately for both sides. **The child is taken out of being an instrument one party may use against the other.**

**فَإِنْ أَرَادَا فِصَالًا عَن تَرَاضٍ مِّنْهُمَا وَتَشَاوُرٍ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْهِمَا** — two conditions are set for the decision to wean: **mutual consent** (*tarāḍin*) and **consultation** (*tashāwur*). **Both words are from Forms III and VI, that is, from patterns conveying reciprocity.**

**Two people who are divorced are asked to decide together about the child.** Separation does not do away with the joint decision.

---

## 2/234-235 — The waiting period and "veiled speech"

*"Those of you who die and leave wives behind — they wait by themselves four months and ten days."*

Here too the term is definite. **And when the term is out, the ruling is plainly lifted:** *fa-lā junāḥa ʿalaykum fīmā faʿalna fī anfusihinna bi'l-maʿrūf* — there is no fault upon you in what they do about themselves after the term.

**وَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ فِيمَا عَرَّضْتُم بِهِۦ مِنْ خِطْبَةِ ٱلنِّسَآءِ** — *taʿrīḍ*: **veiled, indirect speech.** Root **ع-ر-ض** — the same root as *ʿurḍa* at verse 224: to come crosswise, to be indirect.

While forbidding an **open offer of marriage** during the waiting period, the verse leaves **veiled speech** free: *wa lākin lā tuwāʿidūhunna sirran illā an taqūlū qawlan maʿrūfā.*

**This distinction is fine and realistic:** a person is not asked to suppress his intention altogether; **the intention's turning into a binding word** is what is prevented.

**وَٱعْلَمُوٓا۟ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ يَعْلَمُ مَا فِىٓ أَنفُسِكُمْ فَٱحْذَرُوهُ** — and it is recalled that what passes within is known. **And immediately after:** *waʿlamū anna'llāha ghafūrun ḥalīm* — that He is forgiving and **ḥalīm** (not hasty).

**Two sentences one after another: a warning and a widening.** This is the general balance of the section.

---

## 2/236-237 — Divorce before the marriage

*"There is no responsibility upon you if you divorce women before touching them or before fixing a dower for them. **But provide for them** — the one of ample means according to his measure, the one of straitened means according to his."*

**مَتِّعُوهُنَّ** — *matāʿ*: what is enjoyed. Even where no obligation has arisen in law, **a return is asked for.**

And the measure is tied to **the person's circumstances**: *ʿala'l-mūsiʿi qadaruhu wa ʿala'l-muqtiri qadaruhu.* No fixed amount is laid down.

**وَأَن تَعْفُوٓا۟ أَقْرَبُ لِلتَّقْوَىٰ** — "that you forgo is nearer to taqwā."

After laying down the ruling that half the dower is to be given, verse 237 **opens a way for both sides to forgo**, and enters this clause: **وَلَا تَنسَوُا۟ ٱلْفَضْلَ بَيْنَكُمْ** — "do not forget the **faḍl** between you."

**فَضْل**: what is over and above, a bounty. **It is recalled, that is, that there is a field beyond the share the law assigns.**

**This is the line repeated throughout the section on divorce:** the rulings are laid down in detail, **but beside every ruling a door of widening is left** — *iḥsān* (229), *maʿrūf* (231, 232), *tarāḍī* and *tashāwur* (233), *ʿafw* and *faḍl* (237).

---

## 2/238-239 — The prayer set in the middle of the law

*"Keep up the prayers, and the **middle prayer**; and stand before God in reverence."*

**The placing of these two verses has been discussed more than their content — and rightly.** The verse stands right in the middle of the rulings about divorce: before it dower and divorce, after it bequest and the waiting period again.

**There was one other instance of this in the sūra:** verse 186 was placed in the middle of the rulings about fasting, and the same thing was recorded there. **The same working is repeated here: in the middle of the regulation the language of ruling is left for a moment.**

And the place these verses are put is the most strained section of the sūra: separation, division of property, mutual claims of right. **Right there it says "keep up the prayers".** I record this as my own reading: **it is recalled that the ground on which the rulings are to be applied stands before the rulings.**

**ٱلصَّلَوٰةِ ٱلْوُسْطَىٰ** — "the middle prayer". **Which prayer it is is disputed and must not be concealed:**

| View | Ground |
|---|---|
| **The afternoon prayer** | The view most widely transmitted |
| **The dawn prayer** | Its being in the middle between two prayers |
| **The noon prayer** | Its coming at the middle of the day |
| **It has been left unspecified** | That its name was not given so that all the prayers might be kept |

**I impose no preference.** I record only this much: the root of the word (**و-س-ط**) was treated at verse 143 of the sūra in the construct *ummatan wasaṭan*, and it was recorded there that **the word "middle" states both position and value**. **The same double sense holds here.**

**فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ فَرِجَالًا أَوْ رُكْبَانًا** — "if you are in fear, then on foot or mounted."

**The licence is again beside the ruling.** And the form of the licence is worth recording: the prayer is **not lifted**, its form is changed. **The measure repeated in the sūra: in hardship the obligation does not fall, its form is lightened.**

---

## 2/240-242 — Bequest, *matāʿ* and the closing of the section

*"Those of you who die and leave wives behind should make a bequest for their wives: provision for a year without their being turned out of the house. But if they leave of themselves, there is no responsibility upon you for what they do about themselves in a fitting way. God is mighty, wise. And for divorced women there is a provision in a fitting way; a duty upon those who guard themselves. Thus does God make His verses clear to you, that you may use your reason."*

### The place of the verses

These three verses stand immediately after the prayer verses at 238-239 and immediately before the story at 243. The section on family law, that is, has been interrupted once by the prayer verses and **continues from where it was cut.**

And the subject it continues with is the subject at the head of the section: at verses 234-235 the waiting period of a woman whose husband has died was regulated; here the **financial and residential** side of the same situation is regulated.

### مَتَٰعًا إِلَى ٱلْحَوْلِ غَيْرَ إِخْرَاجٍ

**مَتَاع** — root **م-ت-ع**: what is enjoyed, provision. The word was treated at verse 236; there a return was asked for even where no obligation had arisen in law.

**ٱلْحَوْل** — root **ح-و-ل**: **to turn, to pass from state to state.** From the same root come *taḥawwul* (transformation), *ḥāl* and *iḥāla*. Arabic has given the year this name because a year is the span that **comes round to its beginning**. The word itself draws a circle.

**غَيْرَ إِخْرَاجٍ** — "without their being turned out." The crucial place of the regulation is here.

What is given is not merely a maintenance; it is at the same time **the right to stay in the house**. And the expression is built in the negative: a right is not granted, an act is forbidden — *turning out.*

**I record this as my own reading:** the regulation targets the most concrete risk at the moment when the woman is most fragile. The first threat a woman whose husband has died faces is the heirs' turning her out of the house. Before money, the verse secures **shelter**.

And immediately afterwards a clause comes: **فَإِنْ خَرَجْنَ فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْكُمْ** — *"if they leave of themselves, there is no responsibility upon you."* The right, that is, is in the woman's favour and **she is not obliged to use it.** The right to stay is not turned into an obligation to stay.

**فِى مَا فَعَلْنَ فِىٓ أَنفُسِهِنَّ مِن مَّعْرُوفٍ** — "in what they do about themselves **in a fitting way**." The decision is left to the woman, and the only clause is *maʿrūf*. The same word had done the same work at verse 235.

### Its relation to 234 — a disagreement

The relation of this verse to verse 234, which states the waiting period of four months and ten days, is a classical dispute and **must not be concealed:**

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **It has been abrogated** | The period of a year was the earlier practice; verse 234 brought it down to four months and ten days. The view widely transmitted in the classical commentaries |
| **Their subjects differ** | 234 regulates the **waiting period** (*ʿidda*), 240 the **right of residence and maintenance**; the two are separate institutions and do not conflict |
| **It is bound up with the verses of inheritance** | When the rulings on inheritance came, the provision secured by bequest gave way to a fixed share |

**I impose no preference and issue no legal ruling.** I confine myself to recalling the method I adopted at verse 106: **if two verses can be reconciled, reconciling them is preferred to claiming abrogation.** By this measure the second view contains less claim; but the wide currency of the first view in the classical commentaries must be recorded too.

A detail of arrangement can also be noted: verse 240 stands **after** 234. That a ruling said to have been abrogated should be arranged after the ruling said to abrogate it is a well-known instance of the order of the muṣḥaf not being the same as the order of revelation.

### وَلِلْمُطَلَّقَٰتِ مَتَٰعٌۢ بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ

Verse 241 **generalises** the ruling of *matāʿ*. The three occurrences of this word in the sūra draw a course:

| Verse | For whom | Clause |
|---|---|---|
| **2/236** | Those divorced before being touched | *"The one of ample means according to his measure, the one of straitened means according to his"* — *ḥaqqan ʿala'l-**muḥsinīn*** |
| **2/240** | Those whose husbands have died | Up to a year, without being turned out of the house |
| **2/241** | **Divorced women** (in general) | *bi'l-maʿrūf* — *ḥaqqan ʿala'l-**muttaqīn*** |

The third link opens so as to take in the first two: no condition is now counted, no situation distinguished. A provision is laid down **for all divorced women**.

### حَقًّا عَلَى ٱلْمُتَّقِينَ

This closing expression occurs several times in the legal section of the sūra, and each time it is placed at the end of **a financial obligation**:

- **2/180** — the bequest: *ḥaqqan ʿala'l-muttaqīn*
- **2/236** — provision in divorce: *ḥaqqan ʿala'l-muḥsinīn*
- **2/241** — provision for divorced women: *ḥaqqan ʿala'l-muttaqīn*

**The structure itself is worth recording.** The obligation is tied not to a penalty or to a means of enforcement but to the **moral attribute** of the one addressed: "a duty upon those who guard themselves", "a duty upon those who do good".

**I record this as my own reading:** this is not a weakening of the ruling but a fixing of the place where it is applied. An obligation whose amount is left to *maʿrūf* cannot in any case be measured exactly by a court; knowing this, the verse commits the oversight to the person's own attribute. The word **حَقّ** reinforces this: what is at issue is not a recommendation but a **right** — it has a claimant.

And note: the word *muttaqī* is the word that says at the second verse of the sūra to whom the Book shows the way. The sūra uses its own opening word as the measure of **a financial obligation**.

### كَذَٰلِكَ يُبَيِّنُ ٱللَّهُ لَكُمْ ءَايَٰتِهِۦ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَعْقِلُونَ

Verse 242 closes the long section on family law that has run since verse 221.

**يُبَيِّنُ** — root **ب-ي-ن**: to distinguish, to make plain. Form II states that the work is done gradually and in detail. That is exactly what was done throughout the section: situations were separated one by one and each was given its own ruling.

**لَعَلَّكُمْ تَعْقِلُونَ** — "that you may use your reason." That the root *ʿaql* means "to bind" was recorded at verse 44.

And this closing is familiar in the sūra: **the story of the cow had ended with the same sentence** — *"…and He shows you His signs; that perhaps you may use your reason"* (2/73). In both places, after details set one after another, the reader is asked **to bind them to one another**.

**I record this as my own reading:** that the legal sections close with a call to reason shows that the rulings are presented not as a list to be memorised but as a whole in which the measure between them is to be seen. And the measure repeated in this section is plain and is the same in every one of the separate rulings: **the prohibition of causing harm, and the leaving of a door of widening beside every ruling.**

---

## 2/243 — "Those who went out in fear of death"

*"Have you not seen those who went out from their homes **in fear of death**, though they were thousands? God said to them, 'die', then He brought them to life."*

The story is given without detail: who they were, where it happened, when — none of it is said. **Various reports are transmitted in the classical commentaries; I transmit none of them as certain knowledge.**

**Its function in the arrangement is plain:** the next verse will call to fighting (*wa qātilū fī sabīli'llāh*). **This verse is placed as an introduction showing that fleeing from death does not prevent death.**

**حَذَرَ ٱلْمَوْتِ** — "in fear of death". And their number is given: *wa hum ulūf* — **thousands.** Multitude gives no protection. **This clause will be repeated in reverse a few verses later: a small company may overcome a large one (2/249).**

---

## 2/244 — "Fight in God's way"

*"Fight in God's way, and know that God is hearing, knowing."*

A command of a single sentence. But where it is placed says more than the sentence.

### The arrangement of three verses

| Verse | What it recounts |
|---|---|
| **243** | Thousands going out from their homes in fear of death — and dying all the same |
| **244** | *"Fight in God's way"* |
| **245** | *"Who is it that will lend God a good loan…"* |

This ordering is no coincidence. Verse 243 had shown that **fleeing is of no use**: they were thousands, they fled what they feared, and death found them all the same. The command comes immediately after.

**I record this as an observation about the word order:** the command to fight is grounded not on the praise of courage but on **the fruitlessness of flight**. Since fleeing from death does not prevent death, the question that remains is not whether one dies but where one stands.

And verse 245, coming immediately after, completes the second half: **life** and **property.** The Qurʾān often names this pair side by side; here they stand divided between two separate verses. The sūra sets out one after the other the two things it asks to be given, and calls the second a "loan".

### وَقَٰتِلُوا۟ — the pattern itself

**قَٰتِلُوا۟** — root **ق-ت-ل**, and the pattern is **Form III**.

This pattern in Arabic conveys **reciprocity**: *qātala* means "he fought with another". The one-sided act of killing is stated with **قَتْل** (*qatl*); in the sūra this word was used for the act of killing itself (2/61, 2/72, 2/85, 2/91).

This distinction was treated at verse 190 of the sūra, and what was recorded there holds here too: *"Fight those who **fight** you, but **do not transgress**"* — the verb is in the reciprocal pattern, its addressee is specified, and the limit is set immediately beside the permission.

**This verse cannot be read with those limits removed.** The command at verse 244 looks short and unqualified; but the clauses laid down for the same root fifty verses earlier in the same sūra are in force: against those who fight, without transgressing, ceasing when the other side ceases (2/192-193).

In the method of reading rulings in the Qurʾān this is a known principle: **the unqualified is carried to the qualified** — a ruling given without a clause in one place is understood together with the clause laid down in another. The sūra itself has put these two verses inside the same text.

**فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ** — "in God's way." The clause is itself a limit: the act is tied to a purpose. And the same expression is not used in this sūra for fighting alone — at verse 195 it is used for **spending**, at verse 262 again for spending, at verse 273 for the poor who cannot work. *Sabīlu'llāh*, that is, is not the name of fighting; it is the name of what several acts have in common.

### وَٱعْلَمُوٓا۟ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ

The two attributes chosen at the close are striking.

After a command to fight, the attributes one would expect are those conveying power and might — *ʿazīz*, *qawī*, *qadīr*. Indeed these are used in the sūra as well (the close of 2/240 is *ʿazīzun ḥakīm*).

What comes here is **hearing** and **knowing**.

**I record this as my own reading:** both attributes look not to the act but **to intention and to speech**. Fighting is an act that looks the same from outside but can be utterly different in its cause; two men standing on the same field are not counted to be doing the same thing. The attributes *samīʿ* and *ʿalīm* record that exactly this distinction is made.

And this fits the measure already laid down in the section on fighting in the sūra: verse 195 had placed the sentence *"do good"* at the end of the hardest regulation. Here too a clause of **inward oversight** is placed at the end of the command.

The pair of attributes occurs several more times in the sūra, and each time **a word spoken** or **a hidden intention** is at issue: 181 (the altering of a bequest), 224 (oaths), 227 (the decision to divorce). It does the same work here.

---

## 2/245 — "A good loan"

*"Who is it that will lend God **a good loan**, that He may multiply it for him many times over?"*

**قَرْض** — root **ق-ر-ض**: **to cut, to cut off and set apart.** *Miqrāḍ* — scissors. The lexicographers connect a loan with this root thus: **the lender cuts a piece from his property and sets it apart.**

**The choice of the word is worth recording.** The verse does not say "give" or "spend"; it says **"lend"**. A loan is what is given in order to be taken back. **Spending, that is, is described not as something lost but as something entered in the column of what is owed to one.**

**وَٱللَّهُ يَقْبِضُ وَيَبْصُۜطُ** — "God straitens and enlarges." **That this sentence is placed here is an answer to the anxiety about diminution felt in giving.**

---

## 2/246-248 — The people who asked for a king

*"Have you not seen the chiefs of the Children of Israel after Moses? They said to their prophet, 'Send us a **king** and we will fight in God's way.'"*

The story proceeds in three stages, and at each stage **the distance between word and deed** is measured:

| Stage | What was said | What happened |
|---|---|---|
| 246 | "We will fight, give us a king" | *Tawallaw illā qalīlan minhum* — **they turned away, except a few of them** |
| 247 | "How can he be king over us, we are worthier" | The measure is objected to |
| 249 | "Today we have no strength" | The army is sifted |

**أَنَّىٰ يَكُونُ لَهُ ٱلْمُلْكُ عَلَيْنَا وَنَحْنُ أَحَقُّ بِٱلْمُلْكِ مِنْهُ وَلَمْ يُؤْتَ سَعَةً مِّنَ ٱلْمَالِ** — the ground of the objection is stated plainly: **"he has not been given ampleness of wealth."**

And the answer changes the measure: **وَزَادَهُۥ بَسْطَةً فِى ٱلْعِلْمِ وَٱلْجِسْمِ** — "He has given him ampleness **in knowledge and in body**."

**The order is worth recording: knowledge first, then body.** The objection had spoken of wealth; in the answer wealth is not named at all. **The measure is changed, the dispute is not continued.**

**وَٱللَّهُ يُؤْتِى مُلْكَهُۥ مَن يَشَآءُ** — and the ground of the choice is taken out of the measure of those objecting.

---

## 2/249 — The trial at the river

*"When Ṭālūt set out with the army he said: 'God will test you with a **river**. Whoever drinks from it is not of me; whoever does not taste it is of me — except one who takes **a handful with his hand**.'"*

The construction of the test is striking and has three degrees:

| Behaviour | Result |
|---|---|
| To drink one's fill | Sifted out |
| Not to drink at all | Passing |
| **To take a handful** | **Passing** — a licence |

**The licence is set inside the prohibition.** The prohibition is not absolute; measured use is permitted. **A pattern seen many times in the sūra: a widening set beside a ruling.**

**فَشَرِبُوا۟ مِنْهُ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا مِّنْهُمْ** — "they drank of it, except a few." **The same expression as at verse 246: *illā qalīlan minhum*.** The same pattern, twice in the same story. **Many give their word, few stand.**

**قَالُوا۟ لَا طَاقَةَ لَنَا ٱلْيَوْمَ بِجَالُوتَ وَجُنُودِهِۦ** — even those who cross the river enter a second sifting. **The army is reduced twice.**

**كَم مِّن فِئَةٍ قَلِيلَةٍ غَلَبَتْ فِئَةً كَثِيرَةًۢ بِإِذْنِ ٱللَّهِ** — "**how many a small company has overcome a large company.**"

This sentence is the counterpart of the expression *wa hum ulūf* (though they were thousands) at verse 243: **there multitude did not protect, here fewness is no obstacle.** The two verses complete one another.

---

## 2/250-252 — David and "the pushing back of one by another"

*"When they came out against Jālūt (Goliath) and his army they said: 'Our Lord! Pour patience upon us, make our feet firm, and help us against the people who cover over.'"*

**أَفْرِغْ عَلَيْنَا صَبْرًا** — *ifrāgh*: to **empty out completely** what is in a vessel. Patience is asked for not drop by drop but **poured out**. The concrete picture in the word carries the prayer.

**وَقَتَلَ دَاوُۥدُ جَالُوتَ وَءَاتَىٰهُ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُلْكَ وَٱلْحِكْمَةَ** — the story is given not with the details of the battle but with its outcome. **And David (Dāwūd) is named here for the first time in this sūra: not as the foremost name of the army but as one from among the company left after the siftings.**

**وَلَوْلَا دَفْعُ ٱللَّهِ ٱلنَّاسَ بَعْضَهُم بِبَعْضٍ لَّفَسَدَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ**

*"Had God not pushed back one part of people by another, the earth would have been corrupted."*

**دَفْع** — root **د-ف-ع**: to push, to drive back, to cut off the way of something.

**The sentence is laid down as the ruling of the whole story,** and what must be recorded is this: **the balance is set up not by the dominance of a single power but by forces limiting one another.**

**I give this as my own reading, and its ground is the sentence itself:** the verse does not say "God destroys the corrupters"; it says **"He pushes back one part of people by another."** The instrument, that is, is again the human being, and the order stands by mutual limitation.

**The same sentence occurs once more at al-Ḥajj 22/40, in the context of the protection of places of worship.**

---

## 2/253-254 — Preferment and disagreement

*"Those messengers: We preferred some of them above others… **Had God willed, those who came after them would not have fought one another after the clear proofs had come to them. But they differed.**"*

The verse is the continuation of verse 213 and says the same thing: **disagreement arises not from a lack of proof but after the proof.** In both verses the same clause occurs — *min baʿdi mā jāʾathumu'l-bayyināt.*

**فَضَّلْنَا بَعْضَهُمْ عَلَىٰ بَعْضٍ** — a difference of degree among the prophets is spoken of, but the difference is **not enumerated**: who is superior and on what ground is not detailed. At verse 285 of the sūra it will be said *lā nufarriqu bayna aḥadin min rusulih* (we make no distinction between any of His messengers). **The two verses are read together: a difference of degree is stated, but that difference is not made a ground for taking sides.**

*"…spend of what We have provided you before a day comes in which there is **no trading, no friendship and no intercession**."*

Three words enumerate three mechanisms that work in this world: **بَيْع** (to pay a price and receive), **خُلَّة** (getting things done through friendship), **شَفَاعَة** (putting someone in between). **All three are lifted for that day.**

**And the command to spend is placed before this trio:** what is to be done today with what is at hand takes the place of what cannot be done on that day.

---

## 2/255 — The Verse of the Throne

**ٱللَّهُ لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا هُوَ ٱلْحَىُّ ٱلْقَيُّومُ**

The verse is the sūra's most concentrated text of transcendence, and from beginning to end it consists of **declarative sentences**: there is not one command, not one prohibition in it. **Nothing is asked for; something is stated.**

**The arrangement of the sentences follows an order:**

| Order | Sentence | What it states |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Lā ilāha illā hū* | **Oneness** |
| 2 | *al-Ḥayyu'l-Qayyūm* | **The character of the existence** |
| 3 | *Lā taʾkhudhuhū sinatun wa lā nawm* | **The denial of deficiency** |
| 4 | *Lahū mā fi's-samāwāti wa mā fi'l-arḍ* | **Dominion** |
| 5 | *Man dha'lladhī yashfaʿu…* | **The limit of mediation** |
| 6 | *Yaʿlamu mā bayna aydīhim wa mā khalfahum* | **Knowledge** |
| 7 | *Wa lā yuḥīṭūna bi-shayʾin min ʿilmih* | **The limit of knowledge — for the other side** |
| 8 | *Wasiʿa kursiyyuhu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* | **Extent** |
| 9 | *Wa lā yaʾūduhu ḥifẓuhumā* | **Not being wearied** |
| 10 | *Wa huwa'l-ʿaliyyu'l-ʿaẓīm* | **The close** |

**The structure widens from the inside outward:** from the Essence to the attribute, from the attribute to dominion, from dominion to extent. **And it closes at the end with two names.**

### ٱلْحَىُّ ٱلْقَيُّوم

**ٱلْحَىّ** — root **ح-ي-ي**: life. In the **ṣifa mushabbaha** pattern: it conveys **continuance and fixity**.

**ٱلْقَيُّوم** — root **ق-و-م**: to stand, to stand over a matter and keep it going. **The pattern is on the measure *fayʿūl* and in Arabic conveys intensification.**

The lexicographers explain the word in two directions, and both are transmitted: **the one who stands by Himself** (not needing another) and **the one who keeps everything standing** (the existence of everything depending on Him).

**That the two names come side by side is worth recording:** one states **existence**, the other **the sustaining of existence**. *Qayyūm* is akin to *qiyām* (standing) and *iqāma* (holding a matter standing) from the same root — the construct *iqāmu'l-ṣalāt*, used in the sūra for the prayer, is from the same root as well.

### لَا تَأْخُذُهُۥ سِنَةٌ وَلَا نَوْمٌ

**The order is striking: first *sina* (drowsiness), then *nawm* (sleep).**

**سِنَة** — root **و-س-ن**: a heaviness settling on the eye, the state of dozing. It is the **beginning** of sleep, not yet sleep.

**It moves from the lighter to the heavier**, and in Arabic this sequence (*taraqqī*) strengthens the negation: **if even the lightest is absent, the heavier is absent altogether.** The same structure had been set up at al-Wāqiʿa 56/25 for *laghw* and *taʾthīm*.

**And the verb must be recorded: *lā taʾkhudhuhū* — "it does not take him, does not seize him".** Sleep is described as something that **takes** a person: a person does not decide on sleep, sleep takes him. **What is denied is precisely this capacity to be taken.**

### مَن ذَا ٱلَّذِى يَشْفَعُ عِندَهُۥٓ إِلَّا بِإِذْنِهِۦ

The sentence is built **in the form of a question**, and in Arabic this pattern (*istifhām inkārī*) states a negation: "who is there that can intercede?"

**But the sentence does not refuse intercession altogether: it leaves an exception** — *illā bi-idhnih*. **The door, that is, is not shut; it is tied to permission.**

This fits the line set up earlier in the sūra: at verse 48 it was stated that intercession will not be accepted, and the same emphasis was made at verse 254. **The detail here is that permission is kept separate.** It is a disputed subject; **I confine myself to transmitting the debate in the classical commentaries and establish no ruling.**

### وَلَا يُحِيطُونَ بِشَىْءٍ مِّنْ عِلْمِهِۦٓ إِلَّا بِمَا شَآءَ

**إحاطة** — root **ح-و-ط**: to go round a thing, to encompass it. *Ḥāʾiṭ* — a wall.

**The choice of the word fixes the meaning:** what is denied is not **knowing** but **encompassing**. A person may know; **he cannot go round a thing and grasp the whole of it.**

**And here too there is an exception:** *illā bimā shāʾ.* **The sentence does not close knowledge off entirely; it bounds it by what is given.**

### وَسِعَ كُرْسِيُّهُ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضَ

**كُرْسِىّ** — the lexical sense the lexicographers give: **a place of sitting, a throne, a seat;** and, in connection with the root, **something joined together and made firm.**

**There is disagreement in the classical commentaries about what the word amounts to in this verse:**

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Knowledge** — the *kursī* is a figure for the breadth of His knowledge |
| 2 | **Dominion and sovereignty** — the extent of His rule |
| 3 | **Its reality is not known** — the wording is accepted as it stands and its manner left to God |

**All three views are transmitted; I impose no preference.**

**But one clause is required, and it has been consistently preserved in this index:** the word cannot be understood in a way that ascribes place, body or sitting to God. **That would run against the verse's own integrity:** the same verse states that not even sleep can take hold of Him. **To build a bodily meaning after a sentence that denies sleep breaks the text's own logic.**

### وَلَا يَـُٔودُهُۥ حِفْظُهُمَا

The root **أ-و-د**: **to weigh down, to bend, to incline.** *Ādahu'l-ḥiml* — the load bent him.

**The word again comes by way of a bodily picture, and again it is denied.** Like sleep, weariness too points to a being's limit of carrying. **The verse lifts both.**

**At Qāf 50/38 the same idea occurs with another word:** *wa mā massanā min lughūb* (no weariness touched Us) — it was treated in `050-kaf.md`.

### وَهُوَ ٱلْعَلِىُّ ٱلْعَظِيمُ

**ٱلْعَلِىّ** — root **ع-ل-و**: height. **ٱلْعَظِيم** — root **ع-ظ-م**: greatness; the connection the lexicographers make with *ʿaẓm* (bone) was recorded in `069-hakka.md`.

**After a sequence of ten sentences of transcendence, the verse closes with two attributes**, and both attributes **state a comparison:** high and great. **The close, that is, reduces to two words the fact that nothing enumerated can encompass Him.**

---

## 2/256 — "There is no compulsion in religion"

**لَآ إِكْرَاهَ فِى ٱلدِّينِ قَد تَّبَيَّنَ ٱلرُّشْدُ مِنَ ٱلْغَىِّ**

**إِكْرَاه** — root **ك-ر-ه**: to dislike. **A verbal noun of Form IV: to make someone do what he dislikes, to compel.**

**The construction of the sentence must be recorded:** the *lā* here is the *lā* that **negates the genus** (*lā nāfiya li'l-jins*). Not "let there be a little compulsion": **the very category of compulsion is removed.**

**And the ground comes immediately afterwards:** *qad tabayyana'r-rushdu mina'l-ghayy* — "right guidance has been **distinguished** from error."

| Word | Root | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **رُشْد** | ر-ش-د | Finding the right way, maturity, hitting the mark |
| **غَىّ** | غ-و-ي | Going off the way in pursuit of desire |

**The logic of the ground is plain:** if the distinction is in the open, there is no need for compulsion. **Compulsion is needed only where the way to choice is blocked.**

**فَمَن يَكْفُرْ بِٱلطَّٰغُوتِ وَيُؤْمِنۢ بِٱللَّهِ فَقَدِ ٱسْتَمْسَكَ بِٱلْعُرْوَةِ ٱلْوُثْقَىٰ** — "he has taken hold of **the firmest handle**."

**عُرْوَة** — the part of a vessel or a garment by which it is held, a handle. **وُثْقَىٰ** — the feminine of *awthaq*: **the firmest, what does not break.**

**The comparison is concrete:** that what is held to does not break comes not from the strength of the one holding but **from the firmness of the handle**. **And the verb is *istamsaka* — Form X: to hold on firmly of one's own accord.** There is no one compelling in the sentence; **there is one holding on.**

**لَا ٱنفِصَامَ لَهَا** — "there is no breaking in it." The root **ف-ص-م**: a thing's **cracking, coming apart, without breaking off.** The lexicographers record the difference between *faṣm* and *qaṣm*: one is a complete breaking off, the other a crack without separation. **The verse denies even the finer of the two.**

---

## 2/257 — The darknesses are plural, the light singular

*"God is the friend of those who believe; He brings them out **of the darknesses into the light**. The friends of those who cover over are the ṭāghūt; they bring them out **of the light into the darknesses**."*

**ٱلظُّلُمَٰت** is plural, **ٱلنُّور** is singular. **And this is a consistent usage in the Qurʾān.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the number of the words:** darkness is the absence of light, and the forms of absence are many; light is one. **The way is lost in more than one manner, but the place arrived at is one.**

**And in the second half of the sentence the direction is reversed:** *yukhrijūnahum mina'l-nūri ila'l-ẓulumāt.* **The same verb (*ikhrāj* — to bring out) is used in both directions.** The second group too, that is, is **taken** somewhere; it is not left to itself.

---

## 2/258 — An argument in which the proof is not changed

*"Have you not seen the one who argued with Abraham about his Lord? … Abraham said, 'My Lord gives life and gives death.' He said, '**I too give life and give death**.' Abraham said, 'God brings the sun from the east; then bring it from the west.' And the one who covered over **was struck dumb.**"*

**The course of the argument is worth recording.**

The first proof: *rabbiya'lladhī yuḥyī wa yumīt.* The other side's answer meets the proof by **shifting the meaning**: to say "I do it too" by setting one man free and killing another. **The objection, that is, rests not on the proof but on the breadth of the word.**

**Abraham does not continue the argument, he changes the proof.** The new proof chooses what is closed to shifting: **the direction in which the sun rises.**

**I record this as an observation about method:** the first proof was not wrong; **it carried a place the other side could play in.** The second proof leaves no such place.

**فَبُهِتَ ٱلَّذِى كَفَرَ** — *buht*: the other party's being struck dumb, finding no word to answer with. **The argument ends in a silence;** it is not said that he was persuaded.

---

## 2/259 — The man who waited a hundred years

*"…He left him dead **a hundred years**, then raised him. He said, 'How long have you stayed?' He said, 'A day, or part of a day.' He said, 'No, you have stayed a hundred years. **Look at your food and your drink, they have not spoiled**; and look at your donkey…'"*

**The proof is built in three stages:**

| Stage | What is shown |
|---|---|
| The food | **Unspoiled** — as though no time had passed |
| The donkey | **Its bones remaining** — time has passed completely |
| The man himself | On waking he supposes a day has passed |

**The same hundred years has left three different traces in three different beings.** I record this as an observation: **the verse takes as its subject not time itself but the measurability of time's effect.**

**كَيْفَ نُنشِزُهَا ثُمَّ نَكْسُوهَا لَحْمًا** — *inshāz*: to raise and set in place, to lay one upon another. *Kiswa* — to clothe. **The picture of the bones being "clothed" with flesh is used.**

**A note on a variant reading:** it is also transmitted that the word is read *nunshiruhā* (We spread them out and give them life). **The difference between the two readings is between raising and giving life.**

**قَالَ أَعْلَمُ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ قَدِيرٌ** — the one who asked speaks after he has seen. **And what he says is the language not of a confession but of a piece of knowledge.**

---

## 2/260 — "So that my heart may be at rest"

*"Abraham said, 'My Lord, show me how You give life to the dead.' God said, '**Do you not believe?**' He said, 'Yes, I believe; but **so that my heart may be at rest**.'"*

**This verse must be read together with the two before it:** at 258 an unbeliever who argues, at 259 someone who doubts, at 260 **a prophet who says he believes.** All three on the same subject: the giving of life.

**أَوَلَمْ تُؤْمِن** — the question looks like a rebuke, but the acceptance of the answer does not show it to be one: **the request is granted.**

**لِّيَطْمَئِنَّ قَلْبِى** — root **ط-م-ن**: to grow calm, to settle, to come to rest in place. **The lexicographers record the picture of "a thing settling into a hollow and becoming fixed".**

**The distinction is worth recording and follows from the verse itself:** **faith and *iṭmiʾnān* are not the same thing.** One is acceptance, the other a settling within. **What Abraham asks for is not the first but the second** — and his request is not refused.

**I record this as my own reading:** the verse counts a request for reinforcement by seeing as legitimate. **Asking a question is not set down as a lack of faith.**

---

## 2/261-262 — Seven ears of grain

*"The case of those who spend their wealth in God's way is like **a grain that grows seven ears**; in every ear a hundred grains."*

The comparison is chosen from agriculture and a number is given: **one grain → seven ears → a hundred grains in each ear.**

**The function of the number is worth recording:** the verse could have contented itself with saying "He multiplies"; and indeed at the end of the sentence it says this too, with *wa'llāhu yuḍāʿifu li-man yashāʾ*. **But it gives the number first.** A concrete reckoning is set before an abstract promise.

**And the field the comparison chooses is the field in which a person does not see the result of his labour.** As was treated at al-Wāqiʿa 56/63-64: it is the human being who sows, not he who makes it grow. **Spending is set in the same place.**

**ثُمَّ لَا يُتْبِعُونَ مَآ أَنفَقُوا۟ مَنًّا وَلَآ أَذًى** — "then they do not follow what they have given with **reproach** and **hurt**."

**مَنّ** — root **م-ن-ن**: to cut; and to enumerate one's kindnesses. The lexicographers make the connection thus: **reproach cuts the bond of what has been given.**

The condition is placed not on the act of giving but on **what comes after**: *thumma lā yutbiʿūn* — "then they do not follow it with…". **Spending, that is, does not end at the moment of giving.**

---

## 2/263-264 — Where a word is better than alms

*"**A good word and forgiveness** are better than alms followed by hurt."*

The comparison is plain: an attitude that gives nothing but does not hurt is held above a help that is given but with hurt.
