**لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟ صَدَقَٰتِكُم بِٱلْمَنِّ وَٱلْأَذَىٰ** — "do not **void** your alms with reproach and hurt."

**إبطال** — to render a thing invalid, to make it of no effect. **The work done is not erased; it is counted void.**

And then the comparison comes: **كَٱلَّذِى يُنفِقُ مَالَهُۥ رِئَآءَ ٱلنَّاسِ** — like one who spends for show. His case is likened to **a slippery rock with soil on it**: a downpour falls, carries the soil away, and **the bare stone remains.**

**صَفْوَان** — smooth, slippery stone. **The detail of the comparison carries the work:** there is soil on the stone, so that from outside it looks fit for sowing. **What is missing does not show: depth.** The rain — that is, what normally brings blessing — here becomes the thing that lifts the covering.

---

## 2/265-266 — The counter-comparison: the garden on high ground

*"And the case of those who spend their wealth seeking God's good pleasure is like **a garden on high ground**: heavy rain falls on it and it yields its fruit twofold. And if heavy rain does not fall, a light drizzle suffices."*

**Compared with the previous comparison the difference lies in one thing alone: the soil.**

| | 264 | 265 |
|---|---|---|
| The ground | Thin soil on slippery stone | **رَبْوَة** — high, fertile ground |
| Rain | Carries the soil away | Doubles the yield |
| Little rain | — | **Still suffices** — *fa-ṭallun* |

**The same rain gives two different results on two grounds.** I record this as my own reading: **the verse takes as its subject not the outward conditions but the ground.**

**طَلّ** — a drizzle, a light rain. **The work of the detail is this: fertile ground does its work even with little.**

Verse 266 adds a third tableau with a question: **the garden of dates and vines of a man grown old, whose children are weak, burning in a whirlwind with fire in it.** That the loss should come **at the moment of greatest need** is the centre of the description.

---

## 2/267-268 — "Do not pick out the bad"

*"Spend of the good things you have earned and of what We have brought forth for you from the earth. **Do not set about giving away the bad things that you yourselves would not take except with your eyes shut.**"*

The measure is tied to the addressee's own reaction: *wa lastum bi-ākhidhīhi illā an tughmiḍū fīh* — **"and you would take it only with your eyes shut."**

**إغماض** — to shut the eye, to overlook. **The verse, that is, gives no list; it makes the person's own threshold of acceptance the measure.**

**ٱلشَّيْطَٰنُ يَعِدُكُمُ ٱلْفَقْرَ وَيَأْمُرُكُم بِٱلْفَحْشَآءِ وَٱللَّهُ يَعِدُكُم مَّغْفِرَةً مِّنْهُ وَفَضْلًا** — two promises are set against one another:

| Source | The promise |
|---|---|
| Satan | **Poverty** — if you give you will be diminished |
| God | **Forgiveness and bounty** |

**Note: the other side too is named with the verb "to promise" (*yaʿidukum*).** Fear, that is, is described as a promise — **a word about the future.** Both speak of the future; the verse sets the two side by side with the same verb and makes the choice visible.

---

## 2/269-270 — Wisdom

*"He gives **wisdom** to whom He wills. And whoever is given wisdom has been given much good. But only the possessors of understanding take it to heart."*

**حِكْمَة** — root **ح-ك-م**: to restrain, to make firm. **The concrete sense the lexicographers record is striking: *ḥakama* is the bit that turns an animal's head.** From this comes the sense "to hold a thing in place, to keep it from being flung about". *Ḥukm*, *ḥākim*, *muḥkam* are from the same root.

**The word's relation to this root also gives its definition:** wisdom is the **use of knowledge in its place**; what keeps one from being flung about.

**That the verse stands in this section is worth recording:** all the verses around it are about spending — to whom, how much, how, secretly or openly. **This verse standing in the middle names what all those details require:** to know what is to be done in which situation is a different thing from knowing the rule.

**وَمَآ أَنفَقْتُم مِّن نَّفَقَةٍ أَوْ نَذَرْتُم مِّن نَّذْرٍ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يَعْلَمُهُۥ** — that what is done is known; and the close with *wa mā li'ẓ-ẓālimīna min anṣār*.

---

## 2/271-274 — Secret or open

*"If you give alms openly, how good it is; **but if you hide it and give it to the poor, that is better for you.**"*

**Both options are counted legitimate** — one is called "how good", the other "better". **No prohibition is laid down; a degree is given.**

It is transmitted that in the classical commentaries open giving is explained in respect of its encouraging others, and secret giving in respect of preserving the dignity of the recipient.

**لِلْفُقَرَآءِ ٱلَّذِينَ أُحْصِرُوا۟ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ** — verse 273 describes those to whom it is to be given, and the description is striking:

**يَحْسَبُهُمُ ٱلْجَاهِلُ أَغْنِيَآءَ مِنَ ٱلتَّعَفُّفِ تَعْرِفُهُم بِسِيمَٰهُمْ لَا يَسْـَٔلُونَ ٱلنَّاسَ إِلْحَافًا**

*"One who does not know thinks them rich, **because they refrain from asking**. You will know them **by their marks**. They do not ask people **insistently**."*

**تَعَفُّف** — root **ع-ف-ف**: to hold back one's hand, to abstain. **إِلْحَاف** — root **ل-ح-ف**: to wrap oneself in a covering; from this, "to cling and not let go, to insist".

**The description defines the needy not by his asking but by his not asking.** I record this as an observation: **the address of help is shown to be not the one who raises his voice most but the one who does not raise it at all.** And this lays a burden on the giving side: **he is obliged to search.**

**تَعْرِفُهُم بِسِيمَٰهُمْ** — "you will know them by their marks". **To know them, that is, requires not an investigation but an attentiveness.**

---

## 2/275-276 — Ribā: where two transactions part

*"Those who eat ribā rise only **as one rises whom Satan has struck down**. That is because they say 'trade is just like ribā'. **But God has made trade lawful and ribā forbidden.**"*

**رِبَا** — root **ر-ب-و**: to increase, to rise, to swell. From the same root come *rabwa* (high ground — it occurred at verse 265) and *tarbiya* (nurture). **The word itself means "increase";** what is forbidden is not increase itself but **which transaction it arises from** — indeed the same verse counts the profit in trade lawful.

**The objection itself is reported in the verse:** *innama'l-bayʿu mithlu'r-ribā* — "trade is just like ribā." **The other side, that is, looks at the outcome of the two transactions and counts them the same: in both the money increases.**

**The answer looks not to the outcome but to the structure of the transaction.** It is transmitted that in the classical commentaries the distinction is set up at these points: in trade a good changes hands, there is risk, one of the parties may lose; in ribā the increase is **fixed from the outset according to time** and the risk stays on one side.

**I transmit this as the explanations made within the tradition and issue no legal ruling;** the scope of ribā and its application to contemporary transactions is a separate debate, and there are differences among the schools.

**يَمْحَقُ ٱللَّهُ ٱلرِّبَوٰا۟ وَيُرْبِى ٱلصَّدَقَٰتِ** — "God **wipes out** ribā and **makes the alms grow.**"

**The word order turns things round, and it does so with the words themselves:** the verb *yurbī* is from **the same root** as *ribā*. For the thing that bears the name "increase" the verb of diminution is used, and for giving without return the verb of increase. **The verse exchanges the names of the two transactions.**

**مَحْق** — the wiping out of a thing's blessing, its vanishing by degrees. **The lexicographers call the last phase of the moon *miḥāq* too: what diminishes while it shows.**

---

## 2/277 — The one positive verse in the middle of the ribā section

*"Those who believe and do righteous deeds, who keep up the prayer and give the alms — their reward is with their Lord. No fear shall be upon them, nor shall they grieve."*

### Its place in the arrangement

The most striking side of this verse is **where it is placed**.

| Verse | What it says |
|---|---|
| **275-276** | The condition of those who eat ribā; *"God wipes out ribā and makes the alms grow"* |
| **277** | **Reward for those who believe and do good; no fear and no grief** |
| **278-279** | *"Give up what remains of ribā… if you do not, know that you are at war"* |

Immediately **before** the sūra's harshest financial threat, that is, a verse of good news has been placed. In the middle of the ribā section, this is the one sentence that departs entirely from the section's tone.

**I record this as my own reading, and there are instances of it in the sūra:**

- **2/186** — right in the middle of the rulings on fasting, the verse of the answer without an intermediary.
- **2/238-239** — right in the middle of the rulings on divorce, the prayer verses.
- **2/277** — right in the middle of the rulings on ribā, this verse.

In all three the same working is present: in the middle of the regulation the language of ruling is left for a moment and the ground on which the regulation rests is recalled. The sūra does this not by chance but **as a method**.

And its function here is particularly clear. At verse 279 it will be said *"know that you are at war"* — the heaviest expression used in the Qurʾān for a financial transaction. Immediately before that sentence a verse is placed showing that **the door is still open.** The threat does not leave one without a way out.

### The four things enumerated

**ءَامَنُوا۟ — عَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ — أَقَامُوا۟ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ — ءَاتَوُا۟ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ**

The first two are the pair the Qurʾān uses most often: faith and righteous deed. The next two are two **specific** practices.

**And that the last of them is named here has a direct bearing on the context: the alms.**

Ribā and zakāt are the movement of property in two opposite directions:

| | Direction | Who gives, who takes |
|---|---|---|
| **Ribā** | From below upward | The one in straits pays, the one with means takes |
| **Zakāt** | From above downward | The one with means gives, the one in straits takes |

What is set against the transaction forbidden throughout the section is not merely "keeping clear of what is forbidden"; it is **an institution working in the opposite direction**.

**And there is something worth recording in the roots of the two words. I give it as my own reading:**

- **رِبَا** — root **ر-ب-و**: to increase, to swell. (Treated at verse 275.)
- **زَكَاة** — root **ز-ك-و**: to be purified **and to grow.** (Treated in the discussion of al-Jumuʿa 62/2; beneath the root there are two senses at once.)

Both words, that is, state **increase**. The difference lies in how the increase happens: one by taking from another, the other by giving to another. And verse 276 had already said this with the words themselves — *yurbi'ṣ-ṣadaqāt*, "He **makes the alms grow**", with a verb from the same root as *ribā*.

The sūra divides one and the same notion — increase — between two words and sets the two against one another.

### لَهُمْ أَجْرُهُمْ عِندَ رَبِّهِمْ وَلَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ

This closing is repeated several times in the sūra, word for word or nearly so:

| Verse | For whom |
|---|---|
| **2/62** | Everyone who meets the criterion — whatever the name of the group |
| **2/112** | *"Whoever submits himself to God while doing good"* |
| **2/262** | Those who do not follow what they give with reproach and hurt |
| **2/274** | Those who spend by night and by day, secretly and openly |
| **2/277** | **This verse** |

**The distribution of the repetition is worth recording:** three of the five occurrences are in the context of **spending**. The sūra repeats the same promise most often to the person who gives away property.

**وَلَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ** — two negations looking to two different times:

- **خَوْف** (fear) — about what has not yet come
- **حُزْن** (grief) — about what has happened and is over

Together the two close off both sources of a person's unease: what will happen ahead and what has been left behind.

**And this has a direct bearing on the subject of the ribā section. I record this as my own reading:** this is exactly the psychological ground on which interest rests — fear about the future and anxiety over what has left one's hand. The wish to guarantee the increase of one's property is the financial form of insecurity. In the middle of the section the verse names precisely these two feelings and gives what answers them.

And the next verse will continue from the same place: *"You who believe! Guard yourselves against God and give up what remains of ribā — **if you are believers**"* (2/278). The link between the two verses is plain: the ground of the command to give up is the security put in the place of what is given up.

---

## 2/278-281 — The drawing of the boundary

*"You who believe! … give up what remains of ribā. **If you do not, know that you are at war with God and His Messenger.**"*

**فَأْذَنُوا۟ بِحَرْبٍ** — the heaviest expression used in the Qurʾān for a financial transaction.

**And immediately afterwards the boundary is drawn:** *wa in tubtum fa-lakum ruʾūsu amwālikum* — "if you repent, **your capital is yours.**"

| Side | What it takes |
|---|---|
| The creditor | **The capital** — as much as he gave |
| The debtor | Pays no more than that |

**لَا تَظْلِمُونَ وَلَا تُظْلَمُونَ** — "**you neither do wrong nor are wronged.**" **The active and the passive of the same verb side by side.** The regulation protects both parties at once: the creditor's capital is not wiped out, the debtor's excess is lifted.

**وَإِن كَانَ ذُو عُسْرَةٍ فَنَظِرَةٌ إِلَىٰ مَيْسَرَةٍ** — "if he is in difficulty, then **a respite until ease** is required."

**The ruling postpones collection.** And it takes one step further: *wa an taṣaddaqū khayrun lakum* — **"and that you should forgo it is better for you."**

**The ordering is worth recording:** first the right (the capital is yours), then postponement (in hardship one waits), then remission (to cancel is better). **Three degrees, from hard to soft.** This is the structure repeated in the legal sections of the sūra.

**وَٱتَّقُوا۟ يَوْمًا تُرْجَعُونَ فِيهِ إِلَى ٱللَّهِ** — verse 281 closes the whole financial section with the recalling of a day.

---

## 2/282 — The longest verse in the Qurʾān

*"You who believe! When you contract a debt with one another for a fixed term, **write it down.**"*

**The verse is the longest in the Qurʾān, and its subject is striking in itself: the recording of a debt transaction.**

**The details regulated:**

| Subject | The ruling |
|---|---|
| Writing | A command — *fa'ktubūh* |
| The writer | Not one of the parties but **a scribe between them**, with justice |
| The one who dictates | **The debtor** — *wa'l-yumlili'lladhī ʿalayhi'l-ḥaqq* |
| Diminishing | Let the debtor diminish nothing of his debt |
| One of limited capacity | Let **his guardian** dictate with justice |
| Witnesses | Two men; if not available, one man and two women |
| Testimony | Not to be refused when called |
| Small or large | **Whatever the amount**, let there be no weariness of writing |
| Exception | **A transaction on the spot** — it may go unwritten |
| Harm | Let neither scribe nor witness be brought to harm |

**That the one who dictates should be the debtor is worth recording:** the text is drawn up by the one who will pay. **The record therefore does not become the creditor's one-sided declaration.**

**About the regulation of testimony:** there is a wide debate over this part of the verse. **The ground the verse itself gives is in the text:** *an taḍilla iḥdāhumā fa-tudhakkira iḥdāhuma'l-ukhrā* — that if one of them forgets, the other may remind her. **It is transmitted that in the classical commentaries this regulation is connected with the fact that in that period women were mostly not parties to commercial transactions.** I transmit these explanations; **I establish no ruling, and the debate about the scope of this verse belongs to the field of law and legal method.**

**وَلَا تَسْـَٔمُوٓا۟ أَن تَكْتُبُوهُ صَغِيرًا أَوْ كَبِيرًا** — *saʾam*: to weary, to find it irksome. **The verse does not count the smallness of the transaction a ground.**

**ذَٰلِكُمْ أَقْسَطُ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ وَأَقْوَمُ لِلشَّهَٰدَةِ وَأَدْنَىٰٓ أَلَّا تَرْتَابُوٓا۟** — three grounds are given at once: **more just, sounder for testimony, and nearer to your not falling into doubt.**

**I record this as my own reading:** the verse does not leave trust to morals, **it ties it to procedure.** And its ground is this: the record arises not from a suspicion of the parties' good faith but **from the protection of memory and of the relationship.** Indeed at verse 283 the situation resting on mutual trust is regulated too: *fa-in amina baʿḍukum baʿḍan fa'l-yuʾaddi'lladhi'ʾtumina amānatah.*

---

## 2/283 — Pledge and trust

*"If you are on a journey and cannot find someone to write, then a pledge taken in hand suffices. And if you trust one another, let the one who is trusted render his trust, and let him guard himself against God his Lord. Do not conceal the testimony. Whoever conceals it — his heart is sinful. God knows what you do."*

### Three degrees

Verse 282 had commanded the **writing** of a debt. This verse regulates the case in which writing is not possible — and in doing so it builds a structure of three degrees:

| Degree | Instrument | What it rests on |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Writing** (282) — scribe, witnesses, dictation | Procedure |
| **2** | **A pledge** (283) — a security taken in hand | Material guarantee |
| **3** | **A trust** (283) — *fa-in amina baʿḍukum baʿḍan* | Trust |

The ordering **descends** from guarantee to trust — and at each degree the oversight from outside grows less.

**I record this as my own reading:** the verse accepts that the law cannot cover every situation. Procedure is the firmest but does not work everywhere; security stands second but there is not always something to give. What remains is the parties' trusting one another — and the verse does not leave this case unregulated either.

### فَرِهَٰنٌ مَّقْبُوضَةٌ

**رِهَان** — root **ر-ه-ن**. This root was treated in the discussion of al-Muddaththir (74/38); I do not repeat it. The summary of the finding there: a *rahn* is **a security left against a debt** — held in the creditor's hand and given back when the debt is paid.

**مَّقْبُوضَة** — root **ق-ب-ض**: to close the hand, to grasp, to hold. *Qabḍ* is to take a thing into the hand and close it. A pledge, that is, must be **actually taken in hand**; not pointed at in words but held.

The clause is itself a measure of security: a pledge not delivered is, in case of dispute, a new subject of dispute.

**A note on a variant reading:** it is transmitted that the word is also read *ruhun*; both are plurals of *rahn* and the meaning does not change. **This difference has no bearing on the ruling.**

### فَإِنْ أَمِنَ بَعْضُكُم بَعْضًا

The root **أ-م-ن**: **to be safe, to be without fear.** This root was treated at the third verse of the sūra and at verse 125; there it was recorded that the name of the Kaʿba (*amn* — a place of safety) comes from the same root as the name of belief (*īmān*).

And in this verse **three derivatives** of the root come one after another:

> *fa-in **amina** baʿḍukum baʿḍan fa'l-yuʾaddi'lladhi'**ʾtumina amānata**h*
> "if you **trust** one another, let the one who is **trusted** render his **trust**"

Three words, three distinct works of the same root: **to trust** (the verb), **the one trusted** (the person), **the trust** (the object). The sentence is built around a single root.

**I record this as my own reading:** that the word for faith and the word for a trust come from the same root turns in this verse into a ruling. If there is no record, no witness, no pledge — the one thing left is the person's being a **believer**; and the verse does not leave this as an empty attribute, it ties it directly to a debt.

**فَلْيُؤَدِّ** — root **أ-د-ي**: to deliver a thing fully, without shortfall. *Adāʾ* is the giving not of the debt itself but of **the whole of it**. The verb is in the imperative pattern (the *lām* of command): not a recommendation but a ruling.

**وَلْيَتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ رَبَّهُۥ** — "and let him guard himself against God his Lord." The oversight of the command is stated here: this is the only overseer of a transaction of which no record has been kept. That the root of *taqwā* is "to take cover" was recorded at the second verse; here what the cover is set up against changes — the person is guarded against his own desire for gain.

And the clause **رَبَّهُۥ** is added as well: it could have said "let him guard himself against God" and passed on. The addition of *rabbihī* makes the relationship personal.

### وَلَا تَكْتُمُوا۟ ٱلشَّهَٰدَةَ

The sūra's chain of **katm** (concealment) is completed here — and where it is completed matters: as a **rule of law**.

At verse 140 this chain was given in a table: 42 (concealing the truth), 72 (concealing a murder), 140 (concealing testimony), 146 (concealing knowingly), 159 (concealing what was sent down). The act recounted through the sūra as **a moral fault** is, at the sūra's end, forbidden **in the imperative** inside a verse about trade.

**I record this as my own reading:** here the sūra shows its own method. The behaviour diagnosed in the long historical sections is turned into a regulation in the legal section. History comes to be read as the ground of the ruling.

And the measure recorded at verse 140 works here once more: testimony **is not the property** of the witness. The one who holds it is only a carrier; when he withholds it he is holding not his own property but another's right.

### فَإِنَّهُۥٓ ءَاثِمٌ قَلْبُهُۥ

The construction of the sentence is rare in the Qurʾān and striking: the sin is ascribed not to the person but to **his heart**. Literally: "his **heart** is sinful."

Why it is built so becomes clear from the character of the act. Concealment is not an act that can be seen from outside; no movement is made, no word is said. To the onlooker, one who keeps silent is doing nothing. The place where the offence is committed, that is, is **inside** — and the sentence names the offence in the place where it is committed.

**This is the last link of the sūra's chain of the heart:**

| Verse | What happens to the heart |
|---|---|
| **2/7** | It was sealed — *khatama* |
| **2/10** | There is disease in it — *fī qulūbihim maraḍ* |
| **2/74** | Harder than stone — *qāsiya* |
| **2/88** | Wrapped — *ghulf* |
| **2/93** | The calf was made to be drunk in — *ushribū* |
| **2/283** | **Sinful** — *āthimun qalbuh* |

In all six occurrences the heart is named as **the place where an act is done**. The sūra never describes behaviour from the outside alone.

**وَٱللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ عَلِيمٌ** — the close completes the logic of the sentence. After an unseen act has been recounted, it is recorded that the act is known. The same attribute had come in the form *baṣīr* at verse 110 of the sūra; there too the subject was a work whose return is not seen.

### What the two verses say together

282 and 283 set up a balance together.

Verse 282 had tied trust to **procedure**: write, take witnesses, do not distinguish small from large — *"this is more just, sounder for testimony, and nearer to your not falling into doubt."*

Verse 283 regulates the place where procedure is not possible, and there it turns back **to the person himself**.

**I record this as my own reading:** together the two build neither a pure proceduralism nor a pure moralism. Where a record is possible, trust in morals is not asked for; where a record is not possible, morals are not left hanging. The longest verse of the sūra and the short verse that follows it are two halves of the same question.

---

## 2/284-286 — The closing of the sūra

*"To God belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. **Whether you disclose what is within you or hide it**, God will bring you to account for it."*

**The weight of verse 284 is read together with the answer of the two verses that follow.** It is widely transmitted in the classical commentaries that this verse produced a heavy anxiety in those addressed, and that the verses that come after it met that anxiety.

**Verse 285 conveys the word of those who believe:**

**لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّن رُّسُلِهِۦ** — "we make no distinction between any of His messengers." **A sentence standing exactly opposite the attitude of distinction treated in the long section on the Children of Israel.**

**وَقَالُوا۟ سَمِعْنَا وَأَطَعْنَا** — "we hear and we obey." **The counterpart of the word *samiʿnā wa ʿaṣaynā* (we hear and we disobey) reported at verse 93 of the sūra.** There is a difference of a single word between the two sentences; the sūra has put these two attitudes at its two ends.

**Verse 286 is the last verse of the sūra and is built as a prayer:**

**لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا** — "God does not burden any soul beyond **what it can bear**."

**وُسْع** — root **و-س-ع**: breadth. **The measure, that is, is the person's own breadth.** This sentence is a direct answer to the anxiety produced by verse 284, and it is the last expression of the principle of ease seen again and again in the legal sections of the sūra (2/185).

**لَهَا مَا كَسَبَتْ وَعَلَيْهَا مَا ٱكْتَسَبَتْ** — "what it has earned is for it, what it has brought upon itself is against it."

**The two verbs are from the same root (**ك-س-ب**) but their patterns differ:** *kasabat* (Form I) and *iktasabat* (Form VIII). **In Arabic Form VIII adds effort and intent.** The lexicographers record this difference and explain it thus: **the plain verb is used for the good, the effortful verb for the evil.**

**This is an observation about the language and can be verified from the patterns;** the meaning drawn from it I give as my own reading: **evil is named as something that requires a person to go after it.**

**And the sūra closes with three prayers:**

| Prayer | What is asked |
|---|---|
| *Rabbanā lā tuʾākhidhnā in nasīnā aw akhṭaʾnā* | That responsibility be lifted for **forgetting and error** |
| *Rabbanā wa lā taḥmil ʿalaynā iṣrā* | That **a heavy burden** not be laid on us |
| *Rabbanā wa lā tuḥammilnā mā lā ṭāqata lanā bih* | That what we **have no strength for** not be laid on us |

**إِصْر** — root **أ-ص-ر**: to bind, to confine; a heavy load. **The word speaks of the burdens upon past communities.**

**وَٱعْفُ عَنَّا وَٱغْفِرْ لَنَا وَٱرْحَمْنَآ أَنتَ مَوْلَىٰنَا فَٱنصُرْنَا عَلَى ٱلْقَوْمِ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ** — the sūra, after a long text of 286 verses of legal ruling, history and dispute, ends **with a request**.

**And all the closing sentences are in the first person plural:** *lā tuʾākhidhnā, lā taḥmil ʿalaynā, waʿfu ʿannā, irḥamnā.* **At the beginning of the sūra (2/2) it was said that the Book is "a guidance for those who guard themselves"; at the end, the speaker is not individual persons but a community.**

---
